# Timeline

Every dated article (bold, with its span) and every timeline entry in the graph, in chronological order. Generated by `scripts/kg.py build`.

| Date | What |
|---|---|
| 0600 | **[Chunkey](../articles/native-nations/chunkey.md)** (Tradition) |
| 0600 | By an estimate of Timothy Pauketat's cited by Maryland's state archaeology lab, chunkey begins in the Cahokia area around this time. — [Chunkey](../articles/native-nations/chunkey.md) |
| 0700 – 1400 | **[Cahokia](../articles/native-nations/cahokia.md)** (Landmark) |
| 0700 | Native people begin living at the site on the Mississippi floodplain east of present-day St. Louis. — [Cahokia](../articles/native-nations/cahokia.md) |
| 0900 | Chunkey stones become common in the American Bottom, the Mississippi floodplain around Cahokia. — [Chunkey](../articles/native-nations/chunkey.md) |
| 0975 | Work begins on Cahokia's Grand Plaza, which later hosts large chunkey games. — [Chunkey](../articles/native-nations/chunkey.md) |
| 1000 | By about 1000–1200 CE, according to recent carbon dating, Polynesian voyagers from the Marquesas have settled the islands; later voyagers come from the Society Islands. — [Hawaii](../articles/states-and-territories/hawaii.md) |
| 1000 | The Mississippian culture flourishes in the river valley; its greatest center, Cahokia, rises near present-day St. Louis and lasts until about 1350. — [Mississippi River](../articles/geography-and-environment/mississippi-river.md) |
| 1050 | A village of perhaps 1,000–2,000 people is rapidly redesigned into a planned city around a great plaza; archaeologist Timothy Pauketat calls it ancient America's "Big Bang." — [Cahokia](../articles/native-nations/cahokia.md) |
| 1050 | Cahokia's rapid growth begins; afterwards communities from the Great Lakes to the Missouri watershed adopt chunkey and other Cahokian practices. — [Chunkey](../articles/native-nations/chunkey.md) |
| 1050 | Cahokia, east of present-day St. Louis, undergoes a rapid political and social transformation; its population grows almost fivefold in a generation. — [Native Americans](../articles/native-nations/native-americans.md) |
| 1050 | Cahokia, near present-day St. Louis, enters a period of rapid growth; at its peak it is the largest settlement north of Mexico. — [United States](../articles/states-and-territories/united-states.md) |
| 1200 | After a peak of roughly 1050–1200, decline sets in; lake sediments record a major Mississippi River flood around this time. — [Cahokia](../articles/native-nations/cahokia.md) |
| 1215 | Magna Carta promises that free men will be judged by their peers or by the law of the land. — [Due process](../articles/rights-and-law/due-process.md) |
| 1354 | An English statute restating Magna Carta first uses the phrase "due process of law." — [Due process](../articles/rights-and-law/due-process.md) |
| 1400 | The city has been abandoned; its people have dispersed and joined other Native communities. — [Cahokia](../articles/native-nations/cahokia.md) |
| 1507 | The mapmaker Martin Waldseemüller labels the Western Hemisphere's landmasses "America" after Amerigo Vespucci. — [United States](../articles/states-and-territories/united-states.md) |
| 1533 | Spanish explorers reach the tip of present-day Baja California and give the name California. — [California](../articles/states-and-territories/california.md) |
| 1540 | Hopi guides lead a party under García López de Cárdenas, part of Coronado's expedition, to the rim of the Grand Canyon. — [Colorado River](../articles/geography-and-environment/colorado-river.md) |
| 1541 | Spanish explorer Hernando de Soto becomes one of the first Europeans to travel the river. — [Mississippi River](../articles/geography-and-environment/mississippi-river.md) |
| 1542 | Juan Rodríguez Cabrillo sails north along the coast as far as present-day Oregon. — [California](../articles/states-and-territories/california.md) |
| 1562 | Diego Gutiérrez maps the bay as Bahia de Santa Maria. — [Chesapeake Bay](../articles/geography-and-environment/chesapeake-bay.md) |
| 1606-04-10 | King James I grants the Virginia Company of London a royal charter to colonize Virginia. — [Jamestown Colony](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/jamestown.md) |
| 1607-05-13 – 1699 | **[Jamestown Colony](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/jamestown.md)** (City) |
| 1607-05-13 | 104 English men and boys choose a site on the James River and name it for the king. — [Jamestown Colony](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/jamestown.md) |
| 1607-06-15 | The colonists finish the triangular James Fort. — [Jamestown Colony](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/jamestown.md) |
| 1608-06-02 | John Smith sets out on the first of two summer voyages around the bay; the second ends on September 7. — [Chesapeake Bay](../articles/geography-and-environment/chesapeake-bay.md) |
| 1609 | Winter of the Starving Time begins as the Powhatans blockade the fort during the First Anglo-Powhatan War. — [Jamestown Colony](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/jamestown.md) |
| 1609-07 | A hurricane scatters the Virginia Company's nine-ship supply fleet; the flagship Sea Venture, carrying the colony's new leaders, wrecks on Bermuda. — [Starving Time of 1609–1610](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/starving-time.md) |
| 1609-08 | The surviving ships reach Jamestown with about 300 new colonists and few supplies. — [Starving Time of 1609–1610](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/starving-time.md) |
| 1609-09-10 | George Percy is elected president of the council. — [Starving Time of 1609–1610](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/starving-time.md) |
| 1609-10 | John Smith, badly hurt in a gunpowder explosion, sails for England. — [Starving Time of 1609–1610](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/starving-time.md) |
| 1609-11 – 1610-06-08 | **[Starving Time of 1609–1610](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/starving-time.md)** (Event) |
| 1609-11 | Powhatan's warriors ambush a party led by John Ratcliffe and Powhatan orders a siege of the fort. — [Starving Time of 1609–1610](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/starving-time.md) |
| 1610-05 | Governor Thomas Gates resolves to abandon Jamestown but turns back on news of Lord De La Warr's relief fleet. — [Jamestown Colony](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/jamestown.md) |
| 1610-05 | The siege lifts in early May as the Powhatans begin spring planting; about 60 colonists remain alive at the fort. — [Starving Time of 1609–1610](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/starving-time.md) |
| 1610-05-24 – 1619-04-18 | **[Lawes Divine, Morall and Martiall](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/lawes-divine-morall-and-martiall.md)** (Document) |
| 1610-05-24 | Thomas Gates and the Sea Venture survivors reach Jamestown in two boats built on Bermuda. — [Starving Time of 1609–1610](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/starving-time.md) |
| 1610-05-24 | Thomas Gates, arriving from Bermuda to find about sixty starving survivors at Jamestown, issues the first orders. — [Lawes Divine, Morall and Martiall](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/lawes-divine-morall-and-martiall.md) |
| 1610-06-07 | Gates announces that the colonists will abandon Jamestown and sail for England. — [Starving Time of 1609–1610](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/starving-time.md) |
| 1610-06-08 | The departing colonists meet the fleet of the new governor, Lord De La Warr, and are ordered back to Jamestown. — [Starving Time of 1609–1610](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/starving-time.md) |
| 1610-06-12 | The new governor, Thomas West, Lord De La Warr, confirms Gates's orders and adds his own. — [Lawes Divine, Morall and Martiall](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/lawes-divine-morall-and-martiall.md) |
| 1611-06-22 | Deputy Governor Thomas Dale reissues and enlarges the orders and adds a law martial for the colony's soldiers. — [Lawes Divine, Morall and Martiall](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/lawes-divine-morall-and-martiall.md) |
| 1612 | John Rolfe introduces a new strain of tobacco that becomes the colony's cash crop. — [Jamestown Colony](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/jamestown.md) |
| 1612 | Smith's map of Virginia, labelling the Chesapeake and its Native towns, is published in England. — [Chesapeake Bay](../articles/geography-and-environment/chesapeake-bay.md) |
| 1612 | William Strachey publishes the collected orders in London as For the Colony in Virginea Britannia. Lawes Divine, Morall and Martiall, &c. — [Lawes Divine, Morall and Martiall](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/lawes-divine-morall-and-martiall.md) |
| 1614-04 | Pocahontas marries John Rolfe, bringing a peace that lasts until 1622. — [Jamestown Colony](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/jamestown.md) |
| 1616 | Epidemics brought by Europeans begin sweeping coastal New England; by 1619 they have killed up to 90 percent of the region's Native people. — [Thanksgiving](../articles/culture-and-arts/thanksgiving.md) |
| 1616-05 | Dale sails for England; George Yeardley serves as deputy governor. — [Lawes Divine, Morall and Martiall](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/lawes-divine-morall-and-martiall.md) |
| 1619 | The first documented Africans are forcibly brought to Virginia to work the tobacco fields. — [Jamestown Colony](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/jamestown.md) |
| 1619 | Virginia brings in its first enslaved African laborers; tobacco comes to dominate the Chesapeake economy. — [Economic history of the United States](../articles/economy-and-work/economic-history-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1619-04-18 | George Yeardley arrives as governor under the company's reformed charter, ending government under the Lawes. — [Lawes Divine, Morall and Martiall](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/lawes-divine-morall-and-martiall.md) |
| 1619-07-30 | Governor George Yeardley convenes the first representative legislative assembly in English America. — [Jamestown Colony](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/jamestown.md) |
| 1619-07-30 | The first General Assembly meets at Jamestown and begins making laws for the colony's civilian government. — [Lawes Divine, Morall and Martiall](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/lawes-divine-morall-and-martiall.md) |
| 1619-08 | The English privateer White Lion lands about twenty captive Africans at Point Comfort, Virginia, and sells them for food. — [Slavery in the United States](../articles/society-and-people/slavery-in-the-united-states.md) |
| 1619-08-25 | The privateer White Lion brings captive Africans from the kingdom of Ndongo to Old Point Comfort, Virginia. — [African Americans](../articles/society-and-people/african-americans.md) |
| 1621 | Wampanoag leader Ousamequin (Massasoit) and about 90 men join the Plymouth colonists for a three-day harvest gathering, probably in late September or early October. — [Thanksgiving](../articles/culture-and-arts/thanksgiving.md) |
| 1622-03-22 | Opechancanough's coordinated attack kills 350 to 400 of about 1,200 colonists; Jamestown itself is warned and spared. — [Jamestown Colony](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/jamestown.md) |
| 1624 | Virginia's Ancient Planters describe the famine, including cannibalism, in a report commissioned by the General Assembly. — [Starving Time of 1609–1610](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/starving-time.md) |
| 1624-05-24 | The Crown revokes the Virginia Company's charter and Virginia becomes a royal colony. — [Jamestown Colony](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/jamestown.md) |
| 1634 | English colonists on the Ark and Dove, including at least two men of African descent, land at St. Clement's Island, Maryland. — [Chesapeake Bay](../articles/geography-and-environment/chesapeake-bay.md) |
| 1638 | Swedes and Finns found New Sweden on the Delaware River, among Lenape communities already living on both banks. — [Pennsylvania](../articles/states-and-territories/pennsylvania.md) |
| 1641 | Sir Edward Coke's Second Institutes equates "law of the land" with "due process of law," shaping the founders' understanding. — [Due process](../articles/rights-and-law/due-process.md) |
| 1662 | Virginia makes slavery hereditary by law; Maryland follows in 1663. — [Chesapeake Bay](../articles/geography-and-environment/chesapeake-bay.md) |
| 1662-12 | Virginia's General Assembly rules that a child is enslaved or free according to the mother's status, making slavery hereditary. — [Slavery in the United States](../articles/society-and-people/slavery-in-the-united-states.md) |
| 1673 | French explorer Louis Jolliet notes the low divide at Chicago between the Great Lakes and Mississippi drainage systems. — [Chicago](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/chicago.md) |
| 1673 | Louis Jolliet and the Jesuit Jacques Marquette travel the river for France. — [Mississippi River](../articles/geography-and-environment/mississippi-river.md) |
| 1676-09 | Followers of Nathaniel Bacon burn Jamestown during Bacon's Rebellion. — [Jamestown Colony](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/jamestown.md) |
| 1681-03-04 | Charles II grants William Penn the Charter of Pennsylvania, settling a debt owed to Penn's father. — [Pennsylvania](../articles/states-and-territories/pennsylvania.md) |
| 1682-10 | Penn arrives on the Welcome; Philadelphia is laid out as the capital. — [Pennsylvania](../articles/states-and-territories/pennsylvania.md) |
| 1682-10-28 | **[Philadelphia](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/philadelphia.md)** (City) |
| 1682-10-28 | William Penn arrives on the Welcome; within two years about 4,000 settlers have come. — [Philadelphia](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/philadelphia.md) |
| 1682-12-07 | The provincial Assembly adopts the Great Law. — [Pennsylvania](../articles/states-and-territories/pennsylvania.md) |
| 1683 | Penn and surveyor Thomas Holme lay out the street grid with five public squares. — [Philadelphia](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/philadelphia.md) |
| 1692-01 | Pierre Motteux's London Gentleman's Journal carries the motto, the earliest printing Deutsch traced; the Gentleman's Magazine later puts it on its title page. — [E pluribus unum](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/e-pluribus-unum.md) |
| 1698 | Fire destroys the statehouse and prison. — [Jamestown Colony](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/jamestown.md) |
| 1699 | Pierre Le Moyne d'Iberville's French expedition learns from Native guides of a ridge linking the Mississippi to Bayou St. John and Lake Pontchartrain. — [New Orleans](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/new-orleans.md) |
| 1699 | The capital moves to Middle Plantation, renamed Williamsburg, and Jamestown ceases to be a town. — [Jamestown Colony](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/jamestown.md) |
| 1701 | The Charter of Privileges guarantees worship to anyone who acknowledges one almighty God; it stays in force until 1776. — [Pennsylvania](../articles/states-and-territories/pennsylvania.md) |
| 1701-07-24 | **[Detroit](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/detroit.md)** (City) |
| 1701-07-24 | Antoine de la Mothe Cadillac's party chooses the site for Fort Pontchartrain du détroit; a chapel to Ste. Anne is begun two days later. — [Detroit](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/detroit.md) |
| 1705 | A Virginia law classes enslaved people as real estate. — [Slavery in the United States](../articles/society-and-people/slavery-in-the-united-states.md) |
| 1706-01-17 – 1790-04-17 | **[Benjamin Franklin](../articles/presidents-and-founders/benjamin-franklin.md)** (Person) |
| 1706-01-17 | Born in Boston, Massachusetts (January 6 under the Old Style calendar then in use). — [Benjamin Franklin](../articles/presidents-and-founders/benjamin-franklin.md) |
| 1707 | Cadillac begins granting narrow 'ribbon farms' running inland from the river. — [Detroit](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/detroit.md) |
| 1718 | **[New Orleans](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/new-orleans.md)** (City) |
| 1718 | Jean-Baptiste Le Moyne de Bienville founds La Nouvelle-Orléans near the river's mouth. — [New Orleans](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/new-orleans.md) |
| 1722 | France makes New Orleans the capital of French Louisiana. — [New Orleans](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/new-orleans.md) |
| 1722 | The Tuscarora, who had moved north from North Carolina and Virginia, join the confederacy, which becomes the Six Nations. — [Haudenosaunee](../articles/native-nations/haudenosaunee.md) |
| 1723 | Strikes out on his own and finds work as a journeyman printer in Philadelphia. — [Benjamin Franklin](../articles/presidents-and-founders/benjamin-franklin.md) |
| 1730 | Becomes sole proprietor of the printing business he had opened with a partner in 1728. — [Benjamin Franklin](../articles/presidents-and-founders/benjamin-franklin.md) |
| 1731 | Helps organize the Library Company of Philadelphia. — [Benjamin Franklin](../articles/presidents-and-founders/benjamin-franklin.md) |
| 1732-02-22 – 1799-12-14 | **[George Washington](../articles/presidents-and-founders/george-washington.md)** (Person) |
| 1732-02-22 | Born at Popes Creek in Westmoreland County, Virginia (February 11 under the Julian calendar then in use). — [George Washington](../articles/presidents-and-founders/george-washington.md) |
| 1737 | Becomes postmaster of Philadelphia. — [Benjamin Franklin](../articles/presidents-and-founders/benjamin-franklin.md) |
| 1737-09-19 | The Walking Purchase begins; proprietary agents use an unsigned 1686 draft deed to take perhaps over a million acres of Lenape land. — [Pennsylvania](../articles/states-and-territories/pennsylvania.md) |
| 1741 | A Russian naval expedition under Vitus Bering and Aleksei Chirikov reaches Alaska, opening the way for Siberian fur traders. — [Alaska](../articles/states-and-territories/alaska.md) |
| 1743-04-13 – 1826-07-04 | **[Thomas Jefferson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/thomas-jefferson.md)** (Person) |
| 1743-04-13 | Born at Shadwell plantation in western Virginia. — [Thomas Jefferson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/thomas-jefferson.md) |
| 1750 | Georgia lifts its 1735 ban on slavery, and enslaved West Africans are set to work growing rice, indigo and cotton on its island plantations. — [Gullah Geechee](../articles/society-and-people/gullah-geechee.md) |
| 1751 | Letters describing his electrical experiments are published in London; the Academy of Philadelphia, later the University of Pennsylvania, is founded. — [Benjamin Franklin](../articles/presidents-and-founders/benjamin-franklin.md) |
| 1751-03-16 – 1836-06-28 | **[James Madison](../articles/presidents-and-founders/james-madison.md)** (Person) |
| 1751-03-16 | Born at Port Conway, Virginia, on the Rappahannock River. — [James Madison](../articles/presidents-and-founders/james-madison.md) |
| 1751-03-20 | Benjamin Franklin, urging a union of the British colonies, points to the Six Nations' union as proof that one is possible. — [Haudenosaunee](../articles/native-nations/haudenosaunee.md) |
| 1751-11-01 | Superintendents of the Pennsylvania State House order a bell of about 2,000 pounds from London, with a verse from Leviticus as its inscription. — [Liberty Bell](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/liberty-bell.md) |
| 1752 | Elizabeth Griscom, later known as Betsy Ross, is born in Gloucester City, New Jersey, and raised a Quaker. — [Betsy Ross flag myth](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/betsy-ross-flag-myth.md) |
| 1752-06 | Flies a kite in a Philadelphia thunderstorm to collect electrical charge from the clouds. — [Benjamin Franklin](../articles/presidents-and-founders/benjamin-franklin.md) |
| 1752-08 | The bell, cast at the Whitechapel foundry, cracks on its first test strike soon after arriving in Philadelphia. — [Liberty Bell](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/liberty-bell.md) |
| 1752-10-19 | Publishes an account of the kite experiment in the Pennsylvania Gazette. — [Benjamin Franklin](../articles/presidents-and-founders/benjamin-franklin.md) |
| 1753 | **[Liberty Bell](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/liberty-bell.md)** (Symbol) |
| 1753 | Philadelphia metalworkers John Pass and John Stow recast it; the bell face bears the date MDCCLIII. — [Liberty Bell](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/liberty-bell.md) |
| 1753 | Receives the Royal Society's Copley Medal and becomes deputy postmaster general for the northern colonies. — [Benjamin Franklin](../articles/presidents-and-founders/benjamin-franklin.md) |
| 1754-05-09 | Prints the "Join, or Die" snake woodcut in the Pennsylvania Gazette, urging colonial union. — [Benjamin Franklin](../articles/presidents-and-founders/benjamin-franklin.md) |
| 1754-07-03 | Surrenders Fort Necessity to French forces, a clash that helped open the Seven Years' War. — [George Washington](../articles/presidents-and-founders/george-washington.md) |
| 1755-09-24 – 1835-07-06 | **[John Marshall](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/john-marshall.md)** (Person) |
| 1755-09-24 | Born near Germantown in Fauquier County, Virginia, one of fourteen children of Thomas and Mary Randolph Marshall. — [John Marshall](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/john-marshall.md) |
| 1757 | Sails to London as agent of the Pennsylvania Assembly, taking two enslaved men, Peter and King. — [Benjamin Franklin](../articles/presidents-and-founders/benjamin-franklin.md) |
| 1759-02 | Takes his seat in the Virginia House of Burgesses, weeks after marrying Martha Dandridge Custis. — [George Washington](../articles/presidents-and-founders/george-washington.md) |
| 1760 | The British take over Detroit during the French and Indian War. — [Detroit](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/detroit.md) |
| 1763 | Odawa leader Pontiac (Obwandiyag) besieges the British fort, which is never taken. — [Detroit](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/detroit.md) |
| 1764 | French colonists begin building St. Louis across the river, amid what were probably hundreds of mounds. — [Cahokia](../articles/native-nations/cahokia.md) |
| 1765 | The bell rings in protest as a ship carrying stamped paper for the Stamp Act approaches. — [Liberty Bell](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/liberty-bell.md) |
| 1767 | Mason and Dixon survey the boundary with Maryland. — [Pennsylvania](../articles/states-and-territories/pennsylvania.md) |
| 1767-03-15 – 1845-06-08 | **[Andrew Jackson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/andrew-jackson.md)** (Person) |
| 1767-03-15 | Born in the Waxhaw settlement on the Carolina frontier, a few months after his father's death. — [Andrew Jackson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/andrew-jackson.md) |
| 1767-04 | Admitted to the Virginia bar. — [Thomas Jefferson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/thomas-jefferson.md) |
| 1768 | The Boundary Line Treaty at Fort Stanwix pushes the colonial boundary deep into Oneida land. — [Haudenosaunee](../articles/native-nations/haudenosaunee.md) |
| 1769 | Enters the Virginia House of Burgesses, where he serves until 1774. — [Thomas Jefferson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/thomas-jefferson.md) |
| 1769-07-16 | Junípero Serra founds Mission San Diego de Alcalá, the first of 21 Franciscan missions, in Kumeyaay country. — [California](../articles/states-and-territories/california.md) |
| 1771 | Graduates from the College of New Jersey (now Princeton). — [James Madison](../articles/presidents-and-founders/james-madison.md) |
| 1773 | She elopes with upholsterer John Ross and is expelled by the Quakers for marrying outside the meeting. — [Betsy Ross flag myth](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/betsy-ross-flag-myth.md) |
| 1775 – 1865-12-06 | **[Abolitionism](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/abolitionism.md)** (Movement) |
| 1775 | A Native uprising burns the San Diego mission church. — [California](../articles/states-and-territories/california.md) |
| 1775 | Philadelphians, sixteen of the twenty-four of them Quakers, found the Society for the Relief of Free Negroes Unlawfully Held in Bondage, later the Pennsylvania Abolition Society. — [Abolitionism](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/abolitionism.md) |
| 1775 | Returns to Philadelphia and serves as a delegate to the Continental Congress. — [Benjamin Franklin](../articles/presidents-and-founders/benjamin-franklin.md) |
| 1775-04-19 – 1783-09-03 | **[American Revolutionary War](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/american-revolutionary-war.md)** (Conflict) |
| 1775-04-19 | British regulars march to seize militia stores at Concord; fighting at Lexington, Concord's North Bridge and along the road back to Boston opens the war. — [American Revolutionary War](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/american-revolutionary-war.md) |
| 1775-04-20 | New England militia begin the siege of British-held Boston. — [American Revolutionary War](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/american-revolutionary-war.md) |
| 1775-05-10 – 1781-03-01 | **[Second Continental Congress](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/second-continental-congress.md)** (Institution) |
| 1775-05-10 | The Second Continental Congress convenes in Philadelphia, weeks after fighting at Lexington and Concord. — [Second Continental Congress](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/second-continental-congress.md) |
| 1775-06-14 | The Continental Congress votes to raise a Continental Army and the next day unanimously chooses George Washington to command it. — [American Revolutionary War](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/american-revolutionary-war.md) |
| 1775-06-15 | Congress chooses George Washington to command the Continental forces; he accepts the next day. — [Second Continental Congress](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/second-continental-congress.md) |
| 1775-06-15 | The Second Continental Congress appoints him commander in chief of the new Continental Army. — [George Washington](../articles/presidents-and-founders/george-washington.md) |
| 1775-06-17 | Congress agrees to Washington's commission as General and Commander in chief. — [Second Continental Congress](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/second-continental-congress.md) |
| 1775-07-06 | Congress adopts a declaration on taking up arms that disclaims any aim of separation. — [Second Continental Congress](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/second-continental-congress.md) |
| 1775-07-08 | Delegates of twelve colonies sign the Olive Branch Petition to George III. — [Second Continental Congress](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/second-continental-congress.md) |
| 1775-07-13 | Congress approves a speech asking the Six Nations to stay neutral. — [Second Continental Congress](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/second-continental-congress.md) |
| 1775-11-29 | Congress creates the Committee of Secret Correspondence to contact friends abroad. — [Second Continental Congress](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/second-continental-congress.md) |
| 1776 | As a delegate to the Second Continental Congress, drafts the Declaration of Independence. — [Thomas Jefferson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/thomas-jefferson.md) |
| 1776 | At the Virginia Convention, wins wording that widens religious toleration into a right of conscience. — [James Madison](../articles/presidents-and-founders/james-madison.md) |
| 1776 | Enlists as a lieutenant; he rises to captain in the Continental Army by July 1778. — [John Marshall](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/john-marshall.md) |
| 1776 | Maryland's and Virginia's new constitutions adopt Magna Carta's "law of the land" protections. — [Due process](../articles/rights-and-law/due-process.md) |
| 1776 | Sent to France as one of the American commissioners. — [Benjamin Franklin](../articles/presidents-and-founders/benjamin-franklin.md) |
| 1776 | The Domínguez–Escalante expedition fords the river at the Crossing of the Fathers, now beneath Lake Powell. — [Colorado River](../articles/geography-and-environment/colorado-river.md) |
| 1776-01-01 | The Continental Union (Grand Union) flag, with British crosses in its canton, flies over Washington's camp in Massachusetts. — [Betsy Ross flag myth](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/betsy-ross-flag-myth.md) |
| 1776-04-06 | Congress opens American ports to the ships of all nations except Britain. — [Second Continental Congress](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/second-continental-congress.md) |
| 1776-06-07 | Congress receives Richard Henry Lee's resolution for independence. — [Second Continental Congress](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/second-continental-congress.md) |
| 1776-06-07 | Richard Henry Lee of Virginia moves in Congress that the colonies are, and ought to be, free and independent states. — [Declaration of Independence](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/declaration-of-independence.md) |
| 1776-06-11 | After the Lee Resolution, the Second Continental Congress appoints a committee, one member per colony, to plan a confederation. — [Articles of Confederation](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/articles-of-confederation.md) |
| 1776-06-28 | The drafting committee presents Jefferson's draft, edited by Adams and Franklin, to Congress. — [Declaration of Independence](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/declaration-of-independence.md) |
| 1776-07-01 | A first vote on Lee's resolution: nine colonies in favour, Pennsylvania and South Carolina opposed, Delaware deadlocked and New York abstaining. — [Declaration of Independence](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/declaration-of-independence.md) |
| 1776-07-02 | Congress votes for independence. — [Second Continental Congress](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/second-continental-congress.md) |
| 1776-07-02 | The Second Continental Congress votes for independence by adopting the Lee Resolution. — [United States](../articles/states-and-territories/united-states.md) |
| 1776-07-02 | Twelve colonies vote for independence, with New York abstaining. — [Declaration of Independence](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/declaration-of-independence.md) |
| 1776-07-04 | **[Declaration of Independence](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/declaration-of-independence.md)** (Document) |
| 1776-07-04 | **[National symbols of the United States](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/national-symbols-of-the-united-states.md)** (Concept) |
| 1776-07-04 | **[United States](../articles/states-and-territories/united-states.md)** (Country) |
| 1776-07-04 | Congress adopts the Declaration of Independence. — [Second Continental Congress](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/second-continental-congress.md) |
| 1776-07-04 | Congress adopts the Declaration of Independence. — [United States](../articles/states-and-territories/united-states.md) |
| 1776-07-04 | Congress approves the revised text and orders it printed; John Dunlap prints about 200 broadsides. — [Declaration of Independence](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/declaration-of-independence.md) |
| 1776-07-04 | Congress names Franklin, Adams and Jefferson a committee to devise a seal for the new nation. — [Great Seal of the United States](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/great-seal-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1776-07-04 | The Continental Congress appoints Benjamin Franklin, John Adams and Thomas Jefferson as a committee to design a seal. — [E pluribus unum](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/e-pluribus-unum.md) |
| 1776-07-04 | The Second Continental Congress appoints Benjamin Franklin, John Adams and Thomas Jefferson to design a seal for the United States. — [National symbols of the United States](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/national-symbols-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1776-07-04 | The Second Continental Congress, meeting in the State House, approves the Declaration of Independence. — [Philadelphia](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/philadelphia.md) |
| 1776-07-08 | John Nixon gives the first public reading in the State House Yard in Philadelphia. — [Declaration of Independence](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/declaration-of-independence.md) |
| 1776-07-12 | The committee, with John Dickinson as principal writer, presents its draft to Congress. — [Articles of Confederation](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/articles-of-confederation.md) |
| 1776-07-19 | Congress orders the text engrossed on parchment under a new title for members to sign. — [Declaration of Independence](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/declaration-of-independence.md) |
| 1776-08-02 | John Hancock and most of the delegates sign the engrossed parchment. — [Declaration of Independence](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/declaration-of-independence.md) |
| 1776-08-20 | **[E pluribus unum](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/e-pluribus-unum.md)** (Symbol) |
| 1776-08-20 | The committee reports a design drawn up with artist Pierre Eugène Du Simitière bearing the motto E PLURIBUS UNUM; Congress tables it. — [E pluribus unum](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/e-pluribus-unum.md) |
| 1776-08-20 | The first committee's report, with the Eye of Providence and the motto E pluribus unum, is read and tabled. — [Great Seal of the United States](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/great-seal-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1776-08-20 | The first seal committee reports its design; Congress tables it, and two more committees follow in 1780 and 1782. — [National symbols of the United States](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/national-symbols-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1776-09-09 | Congress orders that "United States" replace "United Colonies" in official documents. — [United States](../articles/states-and-territories/united-states.md) |
| 1776-09-28 | Pennsylvania adopts its first state constitution, with a one-house Assembly and no governor. — [Pennsylvania](../articles/states-and-territories/pennsylvania.md) |
| 1776-12 | Crosses the Delaware on Christmas night and wins at Trenton, then at Princeton days later. — [George Washington](../articles/presidents-and-founders/george-washington.md) |
| 1776-12-20 | With the British army advancing across New Jersey, Congress begins sitting in Baltimore. — [Second Continental Congress](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/second-continental-congress.md) |
| 1776-12-25 | After losing New York, Washington crosses the Delaware and surprises the Hessian garrison at Trenton. — [American Revolutionary War](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/american-revolutionary-war.md) |
| 1777 | Fearing the British would melt it for munitions, Philadelphians hide it in Allentown's Zion Reformed Church; it returns in 1778. — [Liberty Bell](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/liberty-bell.md) |
| 1777-01-18 | Congress authorizes a printing that carries the signers' names for the first time. — [Declaration of Independence](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/declaration-of-independence.md) |
| 1777-05-29 | The Pennsylvania State Navy Board pays Ross for making ship's colours, her earliest documented flag work. — [Betsy Ross flag myth](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/betsy-ross-flag-myth.md) |
| 1777-06-14 | The Continental Congress adopts a flag of 13 red and white stripes and 13 white stars on a blue field. — [National symbols of the United States](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/national-symbols-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1777-06-14 | The Continental Congress resolves that the flag be thirteen red and white stripes and thirteen white stars on blue. — [Betsy Ross flag myth](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/betsy-ross-flag-myth.md) |
| 1777-08-06 | Oneida warriors fight beside New York militia at Oriskany as the Haudenosaunee Confederacy splits over the war. — [American Revolutionary War](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/american-revolutionary-war.md) |
| 1777-08-06 | Oneida warriors fight beside New York militia at Oriskany while most of the Six Nations side with Britain, splitting the confederacy. — [Haudenosaunee](../articles/native-nations/haudenosaunee.md) |
| 1777-09-30 | After the British capture of Philadelphia, Congress begins meeting at the York, Pennsylvania, court house. — [Second Continental Congress](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/second-continental-congress.md) |
| 1777-10-17 | John Burgoyne surrenders his army to Horatio Gates at Saratoga, New York. — [American Revolutionary War](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/american-revolutionary-war.md) |
| 1777-11-15 – 1789 | **[Articles of Confederation](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/articles-of-confederation.md)** (Document) |
| 1777-11-15 | After debate sharpened by the British capture of Philadelphia, Congress adopts the Articles. — [Articles of Confederation](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/articles-of-confederation.md) |
| 1777-11-15 | Congress adopts the Articles of Confederation, whose first article names the confederacy "The United States of America." — [United States](../articles/states-and-territories/united-states.md) |
| 1777-11-17 | Congress sends the Articles to the states, all thirteen of which must ratify. — [Articles of Confederation](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/articles-of-confederation.md) |
| 1777-12 | Spends the winter at Valley Forge with Washington's army. — [John Marshall](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/john-marshall.md) |
| 1777-12-16 | Virginia becomes the first state to ratify. — [Articles of Confederation](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/articles-of-confederation.md) |
| 1778 | British captain James Cook reaches the islands. — [Hawaii](../articles/states-and-territories/hawaii.md) |
| 1778 | France recognizes American independence and concludes an alliance with the United States. — [Benjamin Franklin](../articles/presidents-and-founders/benjamin-franklin.md) |
| 1778 | The United States begins making treaties with Native nations; the Senate ratifies 370 of them by 1871. — [Native Americans](../articles/native-nations/native-americans.md) |
| 1778 | The United States signs its first treaty with a Native nation, the Lenape (Delaware); both sides treat early treaties as diplomatic agreements between sovereigns. — [Tribal sovereignty](../articles/native-nations/tribal-sovereignty.md) |
| 1778-02-06 | American commissioners sign the Treaty of Alliance and the Treaty of Amity and Commerce with France. — [American Revolutionary War](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/american-revolutionary-war.md) |
| 1778-06-27 | Congress's last day in York before returning to Philadelphia. — [Second Continental Congress](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/second-continental-congress.md) |
| 1778-11-20 | New Jersey ratifies after holding out over western land claims. — [Articles of Confederation](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/articles-of-confederation.md) |
| 1779 | Presents his credentials to the French court, becoming the first American minister received by a foreign government. — [Benjamin Franklin](../articles/presidents-and-founders/benjamin-franklin.md) |
| 1779 | The Clinton-Sullivan expedition burns many Seneca, Onondaga and Cayuga towns. — [Haudenosaunee](../articles/native-nations/haudenosaunee.md) |
| 1779-02-01 | Delaware ratifies, leaving Maryland the last holdout. — [Articles of Confederation](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/articles-of-confederation.md) |
| 1779-06-21 | Spain enters the war against Britain. — [American Revolutionary War](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/american-revolutionary-war.md) |
| 1779-08-14 | Congress sets its terms for peace; John Adams is named to negotiate the next month. — [Second Continental Congress](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/second-continental-congress.md) |
| 1780 | Enters the Continental Congress as its youngest member, at 29. — [James Madison](../articles/presidents-and-founders/james-madison.md) |
| 1780 | Francis Hopkinson asks Congress to pay him for designing the flag and other emblems; the claim is refused. — [Betsy Ross flag myth](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/betsy-ross-flag-myth.md) |
| 1780 | One of the principal Oneida villages is destroyed, a year after Oneida scouts guided the Clinton-Sullivan expedition against the British-allied Six Nations. — [American Revolutionary War](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/american-revolutionary-war.md) |
| 1780 | Pennsylvania passes a gradual emancipation law. — [Slavery in the United States](../articles/society-and-people/slavery-in-the-united-states.md) |
| 1780 | Studies law at the College of William & Mary. — [John Marshall](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/john-marshall.md) |
| 1780-03 | A second committee, advised by Francis Hopkinson, adds the 13 stripes, 13 stars and olive branch but produces no accepted seal. — [E pluribus unum](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/e-pluribus-unum.md) |
| 1780-03-01 | Pennsylvania enacts the first state law for the gradual abolition of slavery. — [Abolitionism](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/abolitionism.md) |
| 1780-03-01 | The Assembly passes the Gradual Abolition Act, the first emancipation statute in the United States. — [Pennsylvania](../articles/states-and-territories/pennsylvania.md) |
| 1780-03-25 | A second committee, advised by Francis Hopkinson, takes up the design; its report is recommitted in May. — [Great Seal of the United States](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/great-seal-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1781 | Captured by the British with his brother Robert during the Revolutionary War; both contract smallpox, and Robert dies. — [Andrew Jackson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/andrew-jackson.md) |
| 1781-01-10 | Congress creates a Department of Foreign Affairs. — [Articles of Confederation](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/articles-of-confederation.md) |
| 1781-03-01 | Maryland ratifies after Virginia agrees to give up its western claims; the Articles take effect. — [Articles of Confederation](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/articles-of-confederation.md) |
| 1781-03-01 | Maryland's ratification puts the Articles of Confederation into effect, ending the Second Continental Congress's provisional rule. — [Second Continental Congress](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/second-continental-congress.md) |
| 1781-03-01 | Maryland's ratification puts the Articles of Confederation into force. — [United States](../articles/states-and-territories/united-states.md) |
| 1781-06-16 | De Grasse's fleet from France reaches Cap-Français in Saint-Domingue, where the Spanish official Francisco de Saavedra meets him the next day. — [Battle of the Virginia Capes](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/battle-of-the-capes.md) |
| 1781-07 | In late July de Grasse writes to Rochambeau that he will bring his fleet to the Chesapeake. — [Battle of the Virginia Capes](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/battle-of-the-capes.md) |
| 1781-08-05 | De Grasse sails for the Chesapeake through the Bahama passages to avoid British fleets. — [Battle of the Virginia Capes](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/battle-of-the-capes.md) |
| 1781-08-14 | Washington learns of de Grasse's plan and turns the American and French armies toward Virginia. — [Battle of the Virginia Capes](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/battle-of-the-capes.md) |
| 1781-08-15 | Havana residents pledge about 500,000 pesos in loans in a single day to pay de Grasse's expedition. — [Battle of the Virginia Capes](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/battle-of-the-capes.md) |
| 1781-08-18 | The frigate Aigrette delivers the Havana money to de Grasse off Matanzas, Cuba. — [Battle of the Virginia Capes](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/battle-of-the-capes.md) |
| 1781-08-25 | Samuel Hood's British squadron looks into the empty Chesapeake and sails on to New York. — [Battle of the Virginia Capes](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/battle-of-the-capes.md) |
| 1781-08-30 | De Grasse's fleet enters the Chesapeake and lands about 3,000 troops near Jamestown. — [Battle of the Virginia Capes](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/battle-of-the-capes.md) |
| 1781-08-31 | Thomas Graves leaves New York with 19 ships of the line, including Hood's squadron. — [Battle of the Virginia Capes](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/battle-of-the-capes.md) |
| 1781-09-05 | **[Battle of the Virginia Capes](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/battle-of-the-capes.md)** (Conflict) |
| 1781-09-05 | A French fleet under de Grasse fights a British fleet at the Battle of the Capes, leaving France in control of the bay. — [Chesapeake Bay](../articles/geography-and-environment/chesapeake-bay.md) |
| 1781-09-05 | The French fleet under de Grasse turns back a British fleet at the Battle of the Capes, sealing off the Chesapeake. — [American Revolutionary War](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/american-revolutionary-war.md) |
| 1781-09-05 | The fleets fight from late afternoon until sunset off the capes at the bay's mouth. — [Battle of the Virginia Capes](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/battle-of-the-capes.md) |
| 1781-09-10 | De Grasse returns to the bay, where the comte de Barras's squadron from Newport has arrived. — [Battle of the Virginia Capes](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/battle-of-the-capes.md) |
| 1781-09-13 | Graves, outnumbered and with damaged ships, turns back to New York. — [Battle of the Virginia Capes](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/battle-of-the-capes.md) |
| 1781-09-25 | Washington urges de Grasse not to leave the bay; the admiral stays. — [Battle of the Virginia Capes](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/battle-of-the-capes.md) |
| 1781-10-19 | Cornwallis surrenders at Yorktown; the British relief fleet sails from New York that same day. — [Battle of the Virginia Capes](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/battle-of-the-capes.md) |
| 1781-10-19 | Cornwallis's army surrenders to American and French forces at Yorktown, Virginia. — [American Revolutionary War](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/american-revolutionary-war.md) |
| 1781-10-19 | Lord Cornwallis surrenders the British army at Yorktown, Virginia. — [George Washington](../articles/presidents-and-founders/george-washington.md) |
| 1782 | Enters the Virginia House of Delegates and the state's Council of State. — [John Marshall](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/john-marshall.md) |
| 1782-03-20 | Lord North resigns as prime minister; the new British government opens peace talks in Paris. — [American Revolutionary War](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/american-revolutionary-war.md) |
| 1782-05 | A third committee turns to Philadelphia lawyer William Barton, who drafts an eagle and a thirteen-step pyramid. — [Great Seal of the United States](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/great-seal-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1782-06-13 | Congress hands all three committees' material to its secretary, Charles Thomson. — [Great Seal of the United States](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/great-seal-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1782-06-13 | Congress hands the work of all three committees to its secretary, Charles Thomson, who puts the motto on a scroll in the eagle's beak. — [E pluribus unum](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/e-pluribus-unum.md) |
| 1782-06-20 | **[Great Seal of the United States](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/great-seal-of-the-united-states.md)** (Symbol) |
| 1782-06-20 | Congress adopts Thomson's design and his written explanation of its symbols. — [Great Seal of the United States](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/great-seal-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1782-06-20 | The Confederation Congress adopts the Great Seal with the motto. — [E pluribus unum](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/e-pluribus-unum.md) |
| 1782-06-20 | The Confederation Congress approves the Great Seal, with a bald eagle holding arrows and an olive branch. — [National symbols of the United States](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/national-symbols-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1782-08-19 | British rangers and Native allies rout Kentucky militia at Blue Licks, ten months after Yorktown. — [American Revolutionary War](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/american-revolutionary-war.md) |
| 1782-09-16 | The first brass die is used on a document empowering George Washington to negotiate over prisoners of war. — [Great Seal of the United States](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/great-seal-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1782-09-27 | Formal peace negotiations open in Paris between British envoys and the American commissioners Benjamin Franklin, John Adams and John Jay. — [Treaty of Paris (1783)](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/treaty-of-paris-1783.md) |
| 1782-11-06 | His wife, Martha Wayles Jefferson, dies after childbirth. — [Thomas Jefferson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/thomas-jefferson.md) |
| 1782-11-30 | American and British commissioners sign preliminary articles of peace. — [American Revolutionary War](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/american-revolutionary-war.md) |
| 1782-11-30 | The British and American commissioners sign preliminary articles of peace, to take effect only once Britain and France agree terms. — [Treaty of Paris (1783)](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/treaty-of-paris-1783.md) |
| 1782-12-14 | The British evacuate Charleston, South Carolina, taking loyalists and thousands of Black people with them. — [American Revolutionary War](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/american-revolutionary-war.md) |
| 1783 | Marries Mary "Polly" Ambler and settles in Richmond; an enslaved man, Robin Spurlock, is given to the couple as a wedding present. — [John Marshall](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/john-marshall.md) |
| 1783-01-20 | Britain and France sign their own preliminary articles, and an armistice is declared. — [Treaty of Paris (1783)](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/treaty-of-paris-1783.md) |
| 1783-09-03 | **[Treaty of Paris (1783)](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/treaty-of-paris-1783.md)** (Document) |
| 1783-09-03 | David Hartley and the three American commissioners sign the definitive treaty at the Hôtel de York in Paris; Britain's treaties with France and Spain are signed at Versailles the same day. — [Treaty of Paris (1783)](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/treaty-of-paris-1783.md) |
| 1783-09-03 | The Treaty of Paris is signed, ending the war and recognizing American independence. — [American Revolutionary War](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/american-revolutionary-war.md) |
| 1783-09-03 | The Treaty of Paris recognizes U.S. independence, with a western boundary at the Mississippi River. — [United States](../articles/states-and-territories/united-states.md) |
| 1783-11-25 | British forces leave New York City, their last stronghold in the United States. — [American Revolutionary War](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/american-revolutionary-war.md) |
| 1783-12-23 | Resigns his commission to Congress at Annapolis, Maryland. — [George Washington](../articles/presidents-and-founders/george-washington.md) |
| 1784 | Prince Hall establishes African Lodge No. 459 in Boston, the start of Black Freemasonry. — [African Americans](../articles/society-and-people/african-americans.md) |
| 1784 | The Treaty of Fort Stanwix affirms that the Haudenosaunee and the United States are separate nations and sets boundaries to stop settlement. — [Haudenosaunee](../articles/native-nations/haudenosaunee.md) |
| 1784-01-14 | The Confederation Congress, meeting in Annapolis, ratifies the treaty. — [Treaty of Paris (1783)](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/treaty-of-paris-1783.md) |
| 1784-01-26 | Franklin writes an unsent letter to his daughter mocking the eagle on the Society of the Cincinnati badge and praising the turkey. — [National symbols of the United States](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/national-symbols-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1784-10 | Confederation commissioners impose the Treaty of Fort Stanwix on Haudenosaunee delegates while New York pursues its own negotiations. — [Articles of Confederation](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/articles-of-confederation.md) |
| 1784-10-22 | With no terms for Britain's Native allies in the treaty, U.S. commissioners dictate a separate peace to the Six Nations at Fort Stanwix. — [Treaty of Paris (1783)](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/treaty-of-paris-1783.md) |
| 1785 | Begins about four years as American minister to France. — [Thomas Jefferson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/thomas-jefferson.md) |
| 1785 | Thomas Jefferson succeeds him in France; back home he is chosen president of Pennsylvania. — [Benjamin Franklin](../articles/presidents-and-founders/benjamin-franklin.md) |
| 1786-01-16 | Virginia's General Assembly adopts his bill for religious freedom, first drafted in 1777. — [Thomas Jefferson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/thomas-jefferson.md) |
| 1786-09 | Attends the Annapolis Convention, which calls for a national convention the next May. — [James Madison](../articles/presidents-and-founders/james-madison.md) |
| 1786-09-11 | Delegates from several states gather at the Annapolis Convention. — [Articles of Confederation](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/articles-of-confederation.md) |
| 1787 | Becomes president of the Pennsylvania abolition society and sits as the oldest delegate to the Constitutional Convention. — [Benjamin Franklin](../articles/presidents-and-founders/benjamin-franklin.md) |
| 1787 | George Mason is one of three delegates present on the Constitutional Convention's last day who refuse to sign, objecting that the Constitution lacks a bill of rights. — [Bill of Rights](../articles/rights-and-law/bill-of-rights.md) |
| 1787 | Richard Allen and Absalom Jones found the Free African Society in Philadelphia. — [African Americans](../articles/society-and-people/african-americans.md) |
| 1787 | Richard Allen, Absalom Jones and others found the Free African Society. — [Philadelphia](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/philadelphia.md) |
| 1787 | The Philadelphia Convention drafts a Constitution that divides power between a national government and the states. — [Federalism](../articles/constitution-and-government/federalism.md) |
| 1787-02 | New York's refusal of the revised impost ends Congress's attempt to win a national tariff. — [Articles of Confederation](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/articles-of-confederation.md) |
| 1787-02-21 | The Confederation Congress calls a convention to meet in Philadelphia in May to revise the Articles of Confederation. — [Constitutional Convention](../articles/constitution-and-government/constitutional-convention.md) |
| 1787-05 | The Constitutional Convention meets in Philadelphia to revise the Articles and instead drafts a new constitution. — [Articles of Confederation](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/articles-of-confederation.md) |
| 1787-05-14 | The convention's scheduled opening day; too few delegations are present, so members adjourn from day to day. — [Constitutional Convention](../articles/constitution-and-government/constitutional-convention.md) |
| 1787-05-25 – 1787-09-17 | **[Constitutional Convention](../articles/constitution-and-government/constitutional-convention.md)** (Event) |
| 1787-05-25 | A quorum of seven states is finally present and the Federal Convention opens in the Pennsylvania State House. — [United States Constitution](../articles/constitution-and-government/united-states-constitution.md) |
| 1787-05-25 | Elected president of the Constitutional Convention in Philadelphia. — [George Washington](../articles/presidents-and-founders/george-washington.md) |
| 1787-05-25 | Seven states form a quorum and the delegates unanimously elect George Washington president of the convention. — [Constitutional Convention](../articles/constitution-and-government/constitutional-convention.md) |
| 1787-05-25 | The Constitutional Convention assembles; Madison's Virginia Plan frames its debates. — [James Madison](../articles/presidents-and-founders/james-madison.md) |
| 1787-05-29 | Edmund Randolph presents the Virginia Plan, and the convention adopts a rule keeping its proceedings secret. — [Constitutional Convention](../articles/constitution-and-government/constitutional-convention.md) |
| 1787-06-01 | At the Constitutional Convention, James Wilson moves that the executive be a single person; the vote is postponed. — [Presidency of the United States](../articles/constitution-and-government/presidency-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1787-06-11 | James Wilson and Charles Pinckney propose counting three-fifths of enslaved people for representation. — [Constitutional Convention](../articles/constitution-and-government/constitutional-convention.md) |
| 1787-07-12 | The Constitutional Convention votes to tie representation and direct taxes to population, counting enslaved people at three-fifths. — [Census and apportionment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/census-and-apportionment.md) |
| 1787-07-12 | The convention adopts the three-fifths rule, six states to two with two divided. — [Constitutional Convention](../articles/constitution-and-government/constitutional-convention.md) |
| 1787-07-16 | Delegates at the Constitutional Convention agree on a two-chamber legislature with equal state representation in the Senate. — [United States Congress](../articles/constitution-and-government/united-states-congress.md) |
| 1787-07-16 | The Great (Connecticut) Compromise passes by a margin of one state. — [Constitutional Convention](../articles/constitution-and-government/constitutional-convention.md) |
| 1787-07-19 | At the Constitutional Convention, James Madison says direct popular election would leave the Southern states little influence because enslaved people could not vote, and leans toward electors. — [Electoral College](../articles/democracy-and-elections/electoral-college.md) |
| 1787-08 | A bargain between New England and Deep South delegates protects the Atlantic slave trade until 1808. — [Constitutional Convention](../articles/constitution-and-government/constitutional-convention.md) |
| 1787-09-17 | **[United States Constitution](../articles/constitution-and-government/united-states-constitution.md)** (Document) |
| 1787-09-17 | Delegates sign the finished four-page Constitution in Philadelphia. — [United States Constitution](../articles/constitution-and-government/united-states-constitution.md) |
| 1787-09-17 | The Constitutional Convention completes its work in the State House. — [Philadelphia](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/philadelphia.md) |
| 1787-09-17 | The Philadelphia Convention proposes the Constitution and sends it to the states for ratification. — [The Federalist Papers](../articles/constitution-and-government/federalist-papers.md) |
| 1787-09-17 | Thirty-eight delegates sign thirty-nine names to the finished Constitution; three present delegates refuse. — [Constitutional Convention](../articles/constitution-and-government/constitutional-convention.md) |
| 1787-09-28 | Congress unanimously resolves to send the Constitution to the states for ratifying conventions. — [Constitutional Convention](../articles/constitution-and-government/constitutional-convention.md) |
| 1787-10-27 – 1788-05-28 | **[The Federalist Papers](../articles/constitution-and-government/federalist-papers.md)** (Document) |
| 1787-10-27 | The first Publius essay appears in the Independent Journal in New York City. — [The Federalist Papers](../articles/constitution-and-government/federalist-papers.md) |
| 1787-11-23 | Madison's essay on faction, later numbered No. 10, appears in the New York Packet. — [The Federalist Papers](../articles/constitution-and-government/federalist-papers.md) |
| 1787-12-07 | Delaware becomes the first state to ratify. — [Constitutional Convention](../articles/constitution-and-government/constitutional-convention.md) |
| 1787-12-07 | Delaware becomes the first state to ratify. — [United States Constitution](../articles/constitution-and-government/united-states-constitution.md) |
| 1787-12-12 | **[Pennsylvania](../articles/states-and-territories/pennsylvania.md)** (State) |
| 1787-12-12 | Pennsylvania becomes the second state to ratify the U.S. Constitution. — [Pennsylvania](../articles/states-and-territories/pennsylvania.md) |
| 1788 | Moves to Nashville as district attorney for the Mero District. — [Andrew Jackson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/andrew-jackson.md) |
| 1788-02-08 | Federalist No. 51 in the New York Packet describes the compound republic and its double security for the people's rights. — [Federalism](../articles/constitution-and-government/federalism.md) |
| 1788-02-12 | The essay on apportionment and the counting of enslaved people, later No. 54, appears in the New York Packet. — [The Federalist Papers](../articles/constitution-and-government/federalist-papers.md) |
| 1788-03-18 | Federalist No. 70, by Alexander Hamilton, defends a single, energetic executive. — [Presidency of the United States](../articles/constitution-and-government/presidency-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1788-03-22 | John McLean publishes the first bound volume, with 36 essays and a preface by Hamilton. — [The Federalist Papers](../articles/constitution-and-government/federalist-papers.md) |
| 1788-04-02 | The 76th essay of the newspaper run appears; the rest first appear in the bound volumes. — [The Federalist Papers](../articles/constitution-and-government/federalist-papers.md) |
| 1788-05-28 | McLean publishes the second volume, bringing the collection to 85 essays. — [The Federalist Papers](../articles/constitution-and-government/federalist-papers.md) |
| 1788-06 | Votes to ratify the U.S. Constitution as a delegate to the Virginia convention. — [John Marshall](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/john-marshall.md) |
| 1788-06-17 | New York's ratifying convention meets at Poughkeepsie with Antifederalists outnumbering Federalists by more than two to one. — [The Federalist Papers](../articles/constitution-and-government/federalist-papers.md) |
| 1788-06-21 | New Hampshire becomes the ninth state to ratify, enough for the Constitution to take effect. — [Constitutional Convention](../articles/constitution-and-government/constitutional-convention.md) |
| 1788-06-21 | New Hampshire becomes the ninth state to ratify, enough for the Constitution to take effect. — [United States Constitution](../articles/constitution-and-government/united-states-constitution.md) |
| 1788-06-25 | Virginia's ratifying convention approves the Constitution, 89 to 79, after Madison answers Patrick Henry. — [James Madison](../articles/presidents-and-founders/james-madison.md) |
| 1788-07-26 | New York ratifies the Constitution by a vote of 30 to 27, after New Hampshire and Virginia have already ratified. — [The Federalist Papers](../articles/constitution-and-government/federalist-papers.md) |
| 1788-08-16 | Newspaper reprinting of the essays that first appeared in the second volume ends. — [The Federalist Papers](../articles/constitution-and-government/federalist-papers.md) |
| 1789 | The Articles cease to be in force as government under the Constitution begins. — [Articles of Confederation](../articles/colonial-and-revolutionary/articles-of-confederation.md) |
| 1789 | The House, 17 to 32, and then the Senate reject adding 'expressly' to the amendment that becomes the Tenth. — [Federalism](../articles/constitution-and-government/federalism.md) |
| 1789 | The present-day Constitution goes into effect, replacing the Articles. — [United States](../articles/states-and-territories/united-states.md) |
| 1789-03-04 | **[United States Congress](../articles/constitution-and-government/united-states-congress.md)** (Institution) |
| 1789-03-04 | The First Federal Congress is scheduled to convene at Federal Hall in New York City, but neither chamber has a quorum. — [United States Congress](../articles/constitution-and-government/united-states-congress.md) |
| 1789-03-04 | The date the framers set for the new Congress to convene. — [United States Constitution](../articles/constitution-and-government/united-states-constitution.md) |
| 1789-04-01 | **[United States House of Representatives](../articles/constitution-and-government/house-of-representatives.md)** (Institution) |
| 1789-04-01 | The House of Representatives achieves the first quorum. — [United States Congress](../articles/constitution-and-government/united-states-congress.md) |
| 1789-04-01 | The House reaches its first quorum in New York and elects Frederick Muhlenberg of Pennsylvania as Speaker and John Beckley of Virginia as Clerk. — [United States House of Representatives](../articles/constitution-and-government/house-of-representatives.md) |
| 1789-04-06 | The Senate achieves its first quorum and the electoral votes for president are counted. — [United States Congress](../articles/constitution-and-government/united-states-congress.md) |
| 1789-04-06 | The Senate achieves its first quorum. — [United States Constitution](../articles/constitution-and-government/united-states-constitution.md) |
| 1789-04-30 | **[Presidency of the United States](../articles/constitution-and-government/presidency-of-the-united-states.md)** (Office) |
| 1789-04-30 | George Washington takes the first presidential oath at Federal Hall in New York City. — [Presidency of the United States](../articles/constitution-and-government/presidency-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1789-04-30 | In his first inaugural address, George Washington suggests that Congress propose amendments securing rights. — [Bill of Rights](../articles/rights-and-law/bill-of-rights.md) |
| 1789-04-30 | Takes the oath as the first president at Federal Hall in New York City. — [George Washington](../articles/presidents-and-founders/george-washington.md) |
| 1789-06-08 | Introduces proposed amendments in the House of Representatives. — [James Madison](../articles/presidents-and-founders/james-madison.md) |
| 1789-06-08 | James Madison introduces proposed amendments in the House of Representatives. — [Bill of Rights](../articles/rights-and-law/bill-of-rights.md) |
| 1789-06-08 | Representative James Madison proposes constitutional amendments on the House floor. — [United States Congress](../articles/constitution-and-government/united-states-congress.md) |
| 1789-09-15 | An act of Congress renames the Department of Foreign Affairs the Department of State and makes the Secretary of State keeper of the seal. — [Great Seal of the United States](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/great-seal-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1789-09-24 | **[Supreme Court of the United States](../articles/constitution-and-government/supreme-court-of-the-united-states.md)** (Institution) |
| 1789-09-24 | The Judiciary Act of 1789 organizes the Court with a chief justice and five associate justices. — [Supreme Court of the United States](../articles/constitution-and-government/supreme-court-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1789-09-25 | After the House passes seventeen amendments and the Senate trims them to twelve, Congress sends twelve proposed amendments to the states. — [Bill of Rights](../articles/rights-and-law/bill-of-rights.md) |
| 1789-09-25 | Congress proposes twelve amendments, including the article containing the Due Process Clause. — [Due process](../articles/rights-and-law/due-process.md) |
| 1789-09-25 | The First Congress proposes twelve amendments to the states. — [United States Constitution](../articles/constitution-and-government/united-states-constitution.md) |
| 1789-10-02 | President Washington transmits copies of the twelve amendments to the states. — [Bill of Rights](../articles/rights-and-law/bill-of-rights.md) |
| 1789-10-03 | At Congress's request, George Washington proclaims Thursday, November 26, 1789, a day of public thanksgiving and prayer. — [Thanksgiving](../articles/culture-and-arts/thanksgiving.md) |
| 1789-12-01 | A joint committee of Georgia's legislature reports against the amendments, which the state rejects as premature. — [Bill of Rights](../articles/rights-and-law/bill-of-rights.md) |
| 1790 | Becomes secretary of state under George Washington; resigns in 1793. — [Thomas Jefferson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/thomas-jefferson.md) |
| 1790 | Congress moves from New York; Philadelphia is the temporary national capital until 1800. — [Philadelphia](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/philadelphia.md) |
| 1790 | The Residence Act fixes the permanent seat of government on the Potomac River; Congress moves to Washington in 1800. — [United States](../articles/states-and-territories/united-states.md) |
| 1790-02-02 | The Court first convenes in the Exchange Building in New York City, a day late because some justices could not arrive in time. — [Supreme Court of the United States](../articles/constitution-and-government/supreme-court-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1790-02-03 | Benjamin Franklin, as president of the Pennsylvania Abolition Society, signs an antislavery petition to the First Congress, which tables it. — [Abolitionism](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/abolitionism.md) |
| 1790-02-03 | Signs the society's petition asking the First Congress to act against slavery. — [Benjamin Franklin](../articles/presidents-and-founders/benjamin-franklin.md) |
| 1790-04-17 | Dies in Philadelphia, no longer owning any enslaved people. — [Benjamin Franklin](../articles/presidents-and-founders/benjamin-franklin.md) |
| 1790-05-29 | Rhode Island becomes the thirteenth and last original state to ratify. — [United States Constitution](../articles/constitution-and-government/united-states-constitution.md) |
| 1790-05-29 | Rhode Island ratifies the Constitution, joining the Union and raising the number of states needed to approve amendments. — [Bill of Rights](../articles/rights-and-law/bill-of-rights.md) |
| 1790-08-02 | U.S. marshals and their deputies begin the first federal census, which counts 3,929,214 people. — [Census and apportionment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/census-and-apportionment.md) |
| 1790-12-06 | Congress begins a ten-year residence in Philadelphia. — [United States Congress](../articles/constitution-and-government/united-states-congress.md) |
| 1791 | Kamehameha completes Puʻukoholā Heiau on Hawaiʻi Island during his wars of unification. — [Hawaii](../articles/states-and-territories/hawaii.md) |
| 1791-02 | President Washington signs the bill chartering Alexander Hamilton's Bank of the United States. — [Economic history of the United States](../articles/economy-and-work/economic-history-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1791-08-03 | The Court issues its first opinion, in West v. Barnes. — [Supreme Court of the United States](../articles/constitution-and-government/supreme-court-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1791-11-04 | Western Confederacy warriors led by Little Turtle rout Arthur St. Clair's army on the Wabash. — [George Washington](../articles/presidents-and-founders/george-washington.md) |
| 1791-12-12 | The First Bank of the United States opens in Philadelphia with a twenty-year charter. — [Economic history of the United States](../articles/economy-and-work/economic-history-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1791-12-15 | **[Bill of Rights](../articles/rights-and-law/bill-of-rights.md)** (Law) |
| 1791-12-15 | **[Due process](../articles/rights-and-law/due-process.md)** (Right) |
| 1791-12-15 | Ratification of the Bill of Rights adds the Fifth Amendment's Due Process Clause to the Constitution. — [Due process](../articles/rights-and-law/due-process.md) |
| 1791-12-15 | Ten of the amendments are ratified as the Bill of Rights. — [James Madison](../articles/presidents-and-founders/james-madison.md) |
| 1791-12-15 | Ten of the twelve proposed amendments are ratified as the Bill of Rights. — [United States Constitution](../articles/constitution-and-government/united-states-constitution.md) |
| 1791-12-15 | Virginia becomes the eleventh state to ratify, supplying the three-fourths majority that makes ten of the twelve articles part of the Constitution. — [Bill of Rights](../articles/rights-and-law/bill-of-rights.md) |
| 1792 | In Hayburn's Case, justices riding circuit refuse to administer a federal pension act they consider unconstitutional. — [Judicial review](../articles/constitution-and-government/judicial-review.md) |
| 1792-03-01 | Secretary of State Thomas Jefferson notifies the states that ten amendments have been adopted. — [Bill of Rights](../articles/rights-and-law/bill-of-rights.md) |
| 1792-04-05 | George Washington casts the first presidential veto, rejecting a 120-seat apportionment bill as unconstitutional. — [Census and apportionment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/census-and-apportionment.md) |
| 1792-04-14 | Washington signs a replacement act giving the House 105 seats under Jefferson's method. — [Census and apportionment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/census-and-apportionment.md) |
| 1793 – 1865 | **[Underground Railroad](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/underground-railroad.md)** (Movement) |
| 1793 | Congress passes the first federal fugitive slave law, letting enslavers seize freedom seekers even in free states. — [Underground Railroad](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/underground-railroad.md) |
| 1793 | Eli Whitney develops the cotton gin, speeding the spread of cotton growing by enslaved people. — [Economic history of the United States](../articles/economy-and-work/economic-history-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1793 | Yellow fever kills an estimated 5,000 people; federal and state officials leave the city. — [Philadelphia](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/philadelphia.md) |
| 1793-04 | Issues a proclamation of neutrality in the war between France and Britain. — [George Washington](../articles/presidents-and-founders/george-washington.md) |
| 1794 | President Washington leads an army to suppress the Whiskey Rebellion in western Pennsylvania. — [Pennsylvania](../articles/states-and-territories/pennsylvania.md) |
| 1794-01-01 | Delegates from abolition societies in six states meet in Philadelphia and agree to petition Congress against the slave trade. — [Abolitionism](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/abolitionism.md) |
| 1794-09-15 | Marries Dolley Payne Todd. — [James Madison](../articles/presidents-and-founders/james-madison.md) |
| 1794-11-11 | The Treaty of Canandaigua establishes peace and friendship and recognizes Seneca, Oneida, Onondaga and Cayuga lands. — [Haudenosaunee](../articles/native-nations/haudenosaunee.md) |
| 1795 | A treaty with Spain (Pinckney's Treaty) lets Americans navigate the Mississippi and deposit goods at New Orleans for export. — [Louisiana Purchase](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/louisiana-purchase.md) |
| 1795-10-27 | Pinckney's Treaty with Spain opens the river to American ships and duty-free transport through New Orleans. — [Mississippi River](../articles/geography-and-environment/mississippi-river.md) |
| 1796 | Elected Tennessee's first member of the U.S. House of Representatives; the next year the legislature sends him to the Senate. — [Andrew Jackson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/andrew-jackson.md) |
| 1796 | In Hylton v. United States the Supreme Court hears argument on whether a federal law is constitutional and upholds it. — [Judicial review](../articles/constitution-and-government/judicial-review.md) |
| 1796 | Under the original two-vote rule, Federalist John Adams becomes president and his rival, Democratic-Republican Thomas Jefferson, vice president. — [Electoral College](../articles/democracy-and-elections/electoral-college.md) |
| 1796-07-11 | Britain hands Detroit and the surrounding territory over to the United States. — [Detroit](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/detroit.md) |
| 1796-09-19 | His Farewell Address appears in a Philadelphia newspaper. — [George Washington](../articles/presidents-and-founders/george-washington.md) |
| 1797 – 1883-11-26 | **[Sojourner Truth](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/sojourner-truth.md)** (Person) |
| 1797 | Becomes vice president under John Adams after finishing second in the 1796 electoral vote. — [Thomas Jefferson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/thomas-jefferson.md) |
| 1797 | Born Isabella Baumfree, about this year, to James and Elizabeth Baumfree, enslaved in Ulster County, New York. — [Sojourner Truth](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/sojourner-truth.md) |
| 1797 | Sent by President John Adams as one of three envoys to France; the failed mission becomes the XYZ Affair. — [John Marshall](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/john-marshall.md) |
| 1797-03-04 | Leaves office after two terms. — [George Washington](../articles/presidents-and-founders/george-washington.md) |
| 1797-10-08 | Talleyrand gives the three American envoys a brief audience to receive their credentials but does not open negotiations. — [XYZ Affair](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/xyz-affair.md) |
| 1797-10-18 – 1798-04-03 | **[XYZ Affair](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/xyz-affair.md)** (Event) |
| 1797-10-18 | Jean Hottinguer, later called X, meets the envoys and sets out the French conditions for talks. — [XYZ Affair](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/xyz-affair.md) |
| 1797-10-19 | The envoys receive the demands in writing: a loan, payment of claims, and money for Talleyrand and the Directory. — [XYZ Affair](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/xyz-affair.md) |
| 1797-10-21 | Pressed again for money, Charles Cotesworth Pinckney refuses any payment. — [XYZ Affair](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/xyz-affair.md) |
| 1798 | The Kentucky and Virginia legislatures pass resolutions against the Sedition Act that articulate the idea of nullification. — [Federalism](../articles/constitution-and-government/federalism.md) |
| 1798-04-03 | Adams sends Congress the envoys' instructions and dispatches, with the French agents' names left out. — [XYZ Affair](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/xyz-affair.md) |
| 1798-04-23 | John Marshall leaves France for the United States; Elbridge Gerry stays on in Paris. — [XYZ Affair](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/xyz-affair.md) |
| 1798-06-23 | A congressional dinner honoring Marshall's return hears Robert Goodloe Harper's toast to millions for defence. — [XYZ Affair](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/xyz-affair.md) |
| 1798-07-07 | Congress declares the 1778 treaties and the 1788 consular convention with France no longer binding. — [XYZ Affair](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/xyz-affair.md) |
| 1798-12-24 | Virginia adopts his resolutions against the Alien and Sedition Acts. — [James Madison](../articles/presidents-and-founders/james-madison.md) |
| 1799 | Tsar Paul I charters the Russian-American Company with a monopoly on trade and government in Russian America. — [Alaska](../articles/states-and-territories/alaska.md) |
| 1799-12 | Takes his seat in the U.S. House of Representatives for a Virginia district. — [John Marshall](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/john-marshall.md) |
| 1799-12-14 | Dies at Mount Vernon of an acute throat infection. — [George Washington](../articles/presidents-and-founders/george-washington.md) |
| 1800 | A new American commission and Napoleon Bonaparte's government sign the Convention of 1800, ending the undeclared naval war that followed the affair. — [XYZ Affair](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/xyz-affair.md) |
| 1800 | France reacquires Louisiana from Spain. — [Louisiana Purchase](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/louisiana-purchase.md) |
| 1800 | The Court follows the capital from Philadelphia to Washington and is lent space in the Capitol. — [Supreme Court of the United States](../articles/constitution-and-government/supreme-court-of-the-united-states.md) |
