| 1800-05 | Jean Baptiste Point DuSable, the first non-Native person to set up a permanent post at Chicago, sells his property there. — [Chicago](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/chicago.md) |
| 1800-06-06 | Enters duty as Adams's secretary of state. — [John Marshall](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/john-marshall.md) |
| 1800-11 | Democratic-Republicans defeat the Federalists in the presidential and congressional elections; the lame-duck Federalists keep control until March 1801. — [Marbury v. Madison](../articles/rights-and-law/marbury-v-madison.md) |
| 1800-11-17 | The Senate moves into the unfinished Capitol in Washington, D.C. — [United States Congress](../articles/constitution-and-government/united-states-congress.md) |
| 1801 | After Thomas Jefferson and Aaron Burr tie in the Electoral College, the House elects Jefferson on its 36th ballot. — [United States House of Representatives](../articles/constitution-and-government/house-of-representatives.md) |
| 1801-01-01 | The 123 people Washington enslaved become free under his will, after Martha Washington signs a deed of manumission. — [George Washington](../articles/presidents-and-founders/george-washington.md) |
| 1801-01-27 | Confirmed by the Senate as Chief Justice of the United States. — [John Marshall](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/john-marshall.md) |
| 1801-02-04 | Begins serving as Chief Justice while remaining acting secretary of state until March 4. — [John Marshall](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/john-marshall.md) |
| 1801-02-13 | The outgoing Congress passes the Judiciary Act of 1801, creating sixteen circuit judgeships. — [Marbury v. Madison](../articles/rights-and-law/marbury-v-madison.md) |
| 1801-02-17 | After Jefferson and Aaron Burr tie at 73 electoral votes each, the House elects Jefferson on its 36th ballot. — [Electoral College](../articles/democracy-and-elections/electoral-college.md) |
| 1801-02-17 | The House of Representatives elects him president on its 36th ballot, breaking a 73-73 electoral tie with Aaron Burr. — [Thomas Jefferson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/thomas-jefferson.md) |
| 1801-03-02 | President John Adams nominates William Marbury as a justice of the peace for the District of Columbia. — [Marbury v. Madison](../articles/rights-and-law/marbury-v-madison.md) |
| 1801-03-03 | The Senate confirms Marbury; Adams signs the commission and Secretary of State John Marshall affixes the seal, but it is never delivered. — [Marbury v. Madison](../articles/rights-and-law/marbury-v-madison.md) |
| 1801-05-02 | Takes office as Thomas Jefferson's secretary of state, serving until March 3, 1809. — [James Madison](../articles/presidents-and-founders/james-madison.md) |
| 1801-12 | Marbury asks the Supreme Court for a writ of mandamus ordering Secretary of State James Madison to deliver the commission. — [Marbury v. Madison](../articles/rights-and-law/marbury-v-madison.md) |
| 1802 | Elected major general of the Tennessee militia. — [Andrew Jackson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/andrew-jackson.md) |
| 1802 | Georgia cedes its western lands to the federal government, which promises in return to extinguish Indian land title within the state (the Compact of 1802). — [Indian Removal Act](../articles/native-nations/indian-removal-act.md) |
| 1802 | Sitka Tlingit destroy the Russian post of Fort Saint Michael. — [Alaska](../articles/states-and-territories/alaska.md) |
| 1802-03-08 | The new Republican Congress repeals the Judiciary Act of 1801, abolishing the sixteen circuit judgeships. — [Marbury v. Madison](../articles/rights-and-law/marbury-v-madison.md) |
| 1802-04-29 | The Judiciary Act of 1802 sets a single annual Supreme Court session beginning in February and cancels the June 1802 term. — [Marbury v. Madison](../articles/rights-and-law/marbury-v-madison.md) |
| 1802-10 | Spain's King Charles IV signs the decree transferring Louisiana to France, and the Spanish official at New Orleans revokes Americans' right of deposit. — [Louisiana Purchase](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/louisiana-purchase.md) |
| 1803 | Delivers the Court's opinion in Marbury v. Madison, establishing judicial review of acts of Congress. — [John Marshall](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/john-marshall.md) |
| 1803 | In Marbury v. Madison the Court for the first time declares an act of Congress unconstitutional. — [Supreme Court of the United States](../articles/constitution-and-government/supreme-court-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1803 | The U.S. Army erects Fort Dearborn near the mouth of the Chicago River. — [Chicago](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/chicago.md) |
| 1803 | The United States buys Louisiana from France for $15 million. — [Thomas Jefferson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/thomas-jefferson.md) |
| 1803 | The United States buys the Louisiana territory, about 530 million acres, from France for $15 million. — [United States](../articles/states-and-territories/united-states.md) |
| 1803-01 | Jefferson sends James Monroe as minister extraordinary to join Robert Livingston in Paris, authorized to pay up to $10 million for New Orleans and the Floridas. — [Louisiana Purchase](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/louisiana-purchase.md) |
| 1803-01-18 | Jefferson sends Congress a confidential message asking for $2,500 to explore the Missouri River and open trade with the Native nations along it. — [Lewis and Clark Expedition](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/lewis-and-clark-expedition.md) |
| 1803-02-24 | **[Marbury v. Madison](../articles/rights-and-law/marbury-v-madison.md)** (CourtCase) |
| 1803-02-24 | In Marbury v. Madison the Court, through Chief Justice John Marshall, holds part of the Judiciary Act of 1789 unconstitutional. — [Judicial review](../articles/constitution-and-government/judicial-review.md) |
| 1803-02-24 | Marshall announces the unanimous opinion: Marbury has a right to the commission, but the Court lacks power to issue the writ. — [Marbury v. Madison](../articles/rights-and-law/marbury-v-madison.md) |
| 1803-02-27 | In a private letter to William Henry Harrison, lays out a plan to acquire Native lands through trade debts. — [Thomas Jefferson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/thomas-jefferson.md) |
| 1803-03 | In Stuart v. Laird the Court accepts the transfer of a case to a court organized under the 1802 act. — [Marbury v. Madison](../articles/rights-and-law/marbury-v-madison.md) |
| 1803-04-11 | French foreign minister Talleyrand tells Livingston that France will sell all of Louisiana. — [Louisiana Purchase](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/louisiana-purchase.md) |
| 1803-04-30 – 1803-12-20 | **[Louisiana Purchase](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/louisiana-purchase.md)** (Event) |
| 1803-04-30 | Livingston, Monroe and François Barbé-Marbois sign the treaty of cession and two financial conventions in Paris. — [Louisiana Purchase](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/louisiana-purchase.md) |
| 1803-06-20 | Jefferson signs detailed instructions for Meriwether Lewis; that month Lewis invites William Clark to share command. — [Lewis and Clark Expedition](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/lewis-and-clark-expedition.md) |
| 1803-07-04 | The purchase is officially announced in the United States. — [Louisiana Purchase](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/louisiana-purchase.md) |
| 1803-10-20 | The Senate ratifies the treaty by a vote of 24 to 7. — [Louisiana Purchase](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/louisiana-purchase.md) |
| 1803-11-30 | Spain formally returns Louisiana to France. — [Louisiana Purchase](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/louisiana-purchase.md) |
| 1803-12 | Clark sets up Camp Wood near the meeting of the Mississippi and Missouri Rivers for the winter. — [Lewis and Clark Expedition](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/lewis-and-clark-expedition.md) |
| 1803-12-20 | France transfers Louisiana to the United States at New Orleans. — [Louisiana Purchase](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/louisiana-purchase.md) |
| 1803-12-20 | The U.S. flag is raised in New Orleans as Louisiana passes to the United States. — [New Orleans](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/new-orleans.md) |
| 1804 | Alexander Baranov returns to Sitka Sound; the Tlingit withdraw after losing their gunpowder, and the Russians found New Archangel. — [Alaska](../articles/states-and-territories/alaska.md) |
| 1804 | Congress creates the Territory of Orleans from the southern part of the purchase. — [Louisiana Purchase](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/louisiana-purchase.md) |
| 1804-05-14 – 1806-09-23 | **[Lewis and Clark Expedition](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/lewis-and-clark-expedition.md)** (Event) |
| 1804-05-14 | The Corps of Discovery, about 45 people, starts up the Missouri River from Camp Wood. — [Lewis and Clark Expedition](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/lewis-and-clark-expedition.md) |
| 1804-06 | New Hampshire supplies the final ratification of the Twelfth Amendment, which gives electors separate votes for president and vice president. — [Electoral College](../articles/democracy-and-elections/electoral-college.md) |
| 1804-06-15 | The Twelfth Amendment is ratified, so electors cast separate ballots for president and vice president. — [Presidency of the United States](../articles/constitution-and-government/presidency-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1804-08-20 | Sergeant Charles Floyd dies, probably of a ruptured appendix, near present-day Sioux City, Iowa — the expedition's only death. — [Lewis and Clark Expedition](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/lewis-and-clark-expedition.md) |
| 1804-09 | A council with the Lakota (called Teton Sioux in the journals) near present-day Pierre, South Dakota, nearly turns violent. — [Lewis and Clark Expedition](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/lewis-and-clark-expedition.md) |
| 1804-10 | The expedition reaches the Mandan and Hidatsa villages and builds Fort Mandan, where Toussaint Charbonneau and Sacagawea join it. — [Lewis and Clark Expedition](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/lewis-and-clark-expedition.md) |
| 1804-12-15 | Sergeant John Ordway of the Lewis and Clark Expedition describes a Mandan game played with a stone ring and sliding sticks. — [Chunkey](../articles/native-nations/chunkey.md) |
| 1805-04-07 | The keelboat heads back to St. Louis with specimens, maps and reports while the permanent party pushes west. — [Lewis and Clark Expedition](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/lewis-and-clark-expedition.md) |
| 1805-06-11 | Fire destroys nearly the whole town; Judge Augustus Woodward lays out a new radial street plan. — [Detroit](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/detroit.md) |
| 1805-08-17 | The party reaches the head of navigation on the Missouri, meets the Shoshone and crosses the Continental Divide at Lemhi Pass. — [Lewis and Clark Expedition](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/lewis-and-clark-expedition.md) |
| 1805-09 | After a starving crossing of the Bitterroots on the Lolo Trail, the Corps is fed by the Nez Perce at Weippe Prairie. — [Lewis and Clark Expedition](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/lewis-and-clark-expedition.md) |
| 1805-11 | The expedition reaches the Pacific Ocean; members including York and Sacagawea vote on where to spend the winter. — [Lewis and Clark Expedition](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/lewis-and-clark-expedition.md) |
| 1805-12 | The Corps builds Fort Clatsop near present-day Astoria, Oregon. — [Lewis and Clark Expedition](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/lewis-and-clark-expedition.md) |
| 1806-03-23 | The return journey begins. — [Lewis and Clark Expedition](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/lewis-and-clark-expedition.md) |
| 1806-07 | Lewis's detachment fights Piegan Blackfeet on the Marias River, killing two men — the journey's only deadly clash. — [Lewis and Clark Expedition](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/lewis-and-clark-expedition.md) |
| 1806-09-23 | The expedition returns to St. Louis. — [Lewis and Clark Expedition](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/lewis-and-clark-expedition.md) |
| 1807 | John Colter becomes the first known European American to visit the area of present-day Yellowstone. — [Yellowstone National Park](../articles/geography-and-environment/yellowstone-national-park.md) |
| 1807-02-24 | Congress adds a seventh seat. — [Supreme Court of the United States](../articles/constitution-and-government/supreme-court-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1807-03-02 | Signs the act banning the importation of enslaved people, effective January 1, 1808. — [Thomas Jefferson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/thomas-jefferson.md) |
| 1807-12 | Congress passes his Embargo Act, barring American ships from leaving U.S. ports. — [Thomas Jefferson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/thomas-jefferson.md) |
| 1808 | The Shawnee brothers Tecumseh and Tenskwatawa found Prophetstown on the Wabash and Tippecanoe rivers as the center of a Native alliance. — [War of 1812](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/war-of-1812.md) |
| 1808-01-01 | The federal ban on importing enslaved people takes effect. — [Slavery in the United States](../articles/society-and-people/slavery-in-the-united-states.md) |
| 1809 | Becomes the fourth president of the United States. — [James Madison](../articles/presidents-and-founders/james-madison.md) |
| 1809-02-12 – 1865-04-15 | **[Abraham Lincoln](../articles/presidents-and-founders/abraham-lincoln.md)** (Person) |
| 1809-02-12 | Born near Hodgenville, Kentucky. — [Abraham Lincoln](../articles/presidents-and-founders/abraham-lincoln.md) |
| 1809-03-01 | Signs the Non-Intercourse Act replacing the embargo, three days before leaving office. — [Thomas Jefferson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/thomas-jefferson.md) |
| 1810 | Bought by John Dumont of West Park, New York, after being sold three times as a child. — [Sojourner Truth](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/sojourner-truth.md) |
| 1810 | Kamehameha I unites the islands into one kingdom after Kauaʻi comes under his rule. — [Hawaii](../articles/states-and-territories/hawaii.md) |
| 1811 | Congress declines to renew the First Bank's charter after a tie in the Senate is broken against it. — [Economic history of the United States](../articles/economy-and-work/economic-history-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1811 | More than eighty enslaved people launch the German Coast uprising upriver from New Orleans. — [Louisiana Purchase](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/louisiana-purchase.md) |
| 1812 | Louisiana is admitted as a state; President James Madison signs the statehood law. — [Louisiana Purchase](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/louisiana-purchase.md) |
| 1812 | The state capital moves to Harrisburg, after a stay in Lancaster from 1799. — [Pennsylvania](../articles/states-and-territories/pennsylvania.md) |
| 1812-06-01 | President James Madison sends Congress a message listing British offenses against the United States. — [War of 1812](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/war-of-1812.md) |
| 1812-06-04 | The House votes for war, 79 to 49. — [War of 1812](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/war-of-1812.md) |
| 1812-06-17 | The Senate votes for war, 19 to 13; Madison signs the declaration the next day. — [War of 1812](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/war-of-1812.md) |
| 1812-06-18 – 1815-02 | **[War of 1812](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/war-of-1812.md)** (Conflict) |
| 1812-06-18 | Signs the declaration of war against Britain the day after the Senate votes for it. — [James Madison](../articles/presidents-and-founders/james-madison.md) |
| 1812-08-16 | General William Hull surrenders Detroit and all of Michigan Territory to British General Isaac Brock. — [War of 1812](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/war-of-1812.md) |
| 1812-08-16 | General William Hull surrenders Fort Detroit and about 2,500 men to British Major General Isaac Brock. — [Detroit](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/detroit.md) |
| 1812-08-19 | USS Constitution defeats HMS Guerriere and earns the nickname 'Old Ironsides'. — [War of 1812](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/war-of-1812.md) |
| 1813-01-22 | British, Canadian and Native forces rout a Kentucky army at the River Raisin; only 33 Americans escape. — [War of 1812](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/war-of-1812.md) |
| 1813-09-10 | Oliver Hazard Perry's squadron wins the Battle of Lake Erie. — [War of 1812](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/war-of-1812.md) |
| 1813-10-05 | William Henry Harrison's army wins the Battle of the Thames in Upper Canada; Tecumseh is killed. — [War of 1812](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/war-of-1812.md) |
| 1814 | After Andrew Jackson's forces defeat the Creeks at Horseshoe Bend, the Creeks surrender more than twenty million acres. — [Indian Removal Act](../articles/native-nations/indian-removal-act.md) |
| 1814 | Nicholas Biddle's History of the Expedition appears with Clark's map of the West. — [Lewis and Clark Expedition](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/lewis-and-clark-expedition.md) |
| 1814-03 | Defeats the Red Stick Creeks at Horseshoe Bend; the Treaty of Fort Jackson in August takes most Creek land in Alabama and southern Georgia. — [Andrew Jackson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/andrew-jackson.md) |
| 1814-03-27 | Andrew Jackson defeats the Red Stick Creeks at Horseshoe Bend. — [War of 1812](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/war-of-1812.md) |
| 1814-04-02 | British Vice Admiral Alexander Cochrane offers free settlement to Americans who reach British ships, drawing thousands of enslaved people to freedom. — [War of 1812](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/war-of-1812.md) |
| 1814-08-09 | The Treaty of Fort Jackson forces the Creek Nation to cede more than 21 million acres. — [War of 1812](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/war-of-1812.md) |
| 1814-08-24 | After the Battle of Bladensburg, British troops occupy Washington and burn the Capitol and the President's house. — [War of 1812](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/war-of-1812.md) |
| 1814-08-24 | British forces landed from the bay defeat Americans at Bladensburg and burn the Capitol and the White House. — [Chesapeake Bay](../articles/geography-and-environment/chesapeake-bay.md) |
| 1814-08-24 | British troops set fire to the Capitol during the War of 1812. — [United States Congress](../articles/constitution-and-government/united-states-congress.md) |
| 1814-09-11 | A U.S. naval victory on Lake Champlain leads British land forces to abandon their attack at Plattsburgh, New York. — [War of 1812](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/war-of-1812.md) |
| 1814-09-13 | British ships begin a 25-hour bombardment of Fort McHenry at Baltimore but fail to force its surrender. — [Chesapeake Bay](../articles/geography-and-environment/chesapeake-bay.md) |
| 1814-09-13 | The Royal Navy begins a 25-hour bombardment of Fort McHenry at Baltimore, which Francis Scott Key watches. — [War of 1812](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/war-of-1812.md) |
| 1814-09-13 | The Royal Navy begins bombarding Fort McHenry; Francis Scott Key watches from an American truce boat held under British guard. — [The Star-Spangled Banner](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/star-spangled-banner.md) |
| 1814-09-14 | **[The Star-Spangled Banner](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/star-spangled-banner.md)** (Symbol) |
| 1814-09-14 | At dawn the British break off the attack; Key, released hours later, completes his verses in Baltimore. — [The Star-Spangled Banner](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/star-spangled-banner.md) |
| 1814-09-17 | Printed copies of the verses, headed 'Defence of Fort M'Henry', are handed out at the fort and in Baltimore. — [The Star-Spangled Banner](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/star-spangled-banner.md) |
| 1814-10-19 | The song is first performed on stage, at Baltimore's Holliday Street Theatre. — [The Star-Spangled Banner](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/star-spangled-banner.md) |
| 1814-12-24 | American and British commissioners sign the Treaty of Ghent in present-day Belgium. — [War of 1812](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/war-of-1812.md) |
| 1814-12-24 | The Treaty of Ghent ends the War of 1812 on the basis of prewar boundaries. — [James Madison](../articles/presidents-and-founders/james-madison.md) |
| 1815-01-08 | American forces defeat a British assault at New Orleans, the last major land battle of the war. — [War of 1812](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/war-of-1812.md) |
| 1815-01-08 | Andrew Jackson's forces defeat a British army below the city, after the Treaty of Ghent was signed but before it took effect. — [New Orleans](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/new-orleans.md) |
| 1815-01-08 | Defeats the British at New Orleans, weeks after the Treaty of Ghent was signed. — [Andrew Jackson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/andrew-jackson.md) |
| 1815-02 | The United States ratifies the Treaty of Ghent, legally ending the war. — [War of 1812](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/war-of-1812.md) |
| 1816 | Signs the charter of the Second Bank of the United States. — [James Madison](../articles/presidents-and-founders/james-madison.md) |
| 1816 | The African Methodist Episcopal Church is organized as an independent denomination, with Allen elected its bishop. — [African Americans](../articles/society-and-people/african-americans.md) |
| 1816 | The American Colonization Society is founded to resettle free Black Americans in Africa. — [Abolitionism](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/abolitionism.md) |
| 1816-04 | President James Madison signs the act chartering the Second Bank of the United States for twenty years. — [McCulloch v. Maryland](../articles/rights-and-law/mcculloch-v-maryland.md) |
| 1817 | New Orleans begins sanctioning Sunday ring shouts by enslaved and free Black people at Place Congo (Congo Square), a practice allowed until 1856. — [Jazz](../articles/culture-and-arts/jazz.md) |
| 1817 | Vetoes an internal-improvements bill and retires to Montpelier. — [James Madison](../articles/presidents-and-founders/james-madison.md) |
| 1817-01 | The Second Bank opens in Philadelphia; it later sets up branches, including one in Baltimore. — [McCulloch v. Maryland](../articles/rights-and-law/mcculloch-v-maryland.md) |
| 1818 | Invades Spanish Florida during the First Seminole War; Spain cedes Florida in an 1819 treaty. — [Andrew Jackson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/andrew-jackson.md) |
| 1818 | The Gideon edition prints the essays with Madison's own attributions of authorship. — [The Federalist Papers](../articles/constitution-and-government/federalist-papers.md) |
| 1818-02 – 1895-02-20 | **[Frederick Douglass](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/frederick-douglass.md)** (Person) |
| 1818-02 | Born Frederick Augustus Washington Bailey in Talbot County, Maryland, according to a later ledger notation; he himself believed he was born about 1817. — [Frederick Douglass](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/frederick-douglass.md) |
| 1818-02-11 | Maryland's legislature passes a stamp tax on banks operating in the state without a state charter, effective May 1, 1818. — [McCulloch v. Maryland](../articles/rights-and-law/mcculloch-v-maryland.md) |
| 1818-05-01 | The Maryland tax takes effect; James W. McCulloch, cashier of the Baltimore branch, later issues notes without the required stamps. — [McCulloch v. Maryland](../articles/rights-and-law/mcculloch-v-maryland.md) |
| 1819 | Kamehameha I dies; his son Liholiho (Kamehameha II) soon ends the kapu system. — [Hawaii](../articles/states-and-territories/hawaii.md) |
| 1819 | McCulloch v. Maryland upholds Congress's implied power to charter a national bank and affirms national supremacy. — [Federalism](../articles/constitution-and-government/federalism.md) |
| 1819 | The Adams-Onís (Transcontinental) Treaty with Spain finally settles the purchase's disputed borders. — [Louisiana Purchase](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/louisiana-purchase.md) |
| 1819 | Writes for the Court in McCulloch v. Maryland and the Dartmouth College case. — [John Marshall](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/john-marshall.md) |
| 1819-02-15 | Representative James Tallmadge Jr. of New York proposes amending the Missouri statehood bill to bar further slavery there; the House adopts it on sectional lines, but the Senate strikes it and the bill dies. — [Missouri Compromise](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/missouri-compromise.md) |
| 1819-03-06 | **[McCulloch v. Maryland](../articles/rights-and-law/mcculloch-v-maryland.md)** (CourtCase) |
| 1819-03-06 | The Supreme Court unanimously rules for McCulloch, upholding the bank and striking down Maryland's tax. — [McCulloch v. Maryland](../articles/rights-and-law/mcculloch-v-maryland.md) |
| 1819-06-11 | Virginia judge Spencer Roane begins his four 'Hampden' essays attacking the decision in the Richmond Enquirer. — [McCulloch v. Maryland](../articles/rights-and-law/mcculloch-v-maryland.md) |
| 1819-09-02 | James Madison writes to Roane objecting to the breadth of the Court's rule of construction. — [McCulloch v. Maryland](../articles/rights-and-law/mcculloch-v-maryland.md) |
| 1819-12 | The Sixteenth Congress convenes; Senate leaders go on to tie Maine's statehood to Missouri's. — [Missouri Compromise](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/missouri-compromise.md) |
| 1820-02-15 – 1906-03-13 | **[Susan B. Anthony](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/susan-b-anthony.md)** (Person) |
| 1820-02-15 | Born in Adams, Massachusetts, to Daniel and Lucy Anthony. — [Susan B. Anthony](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/susan-b-anthony.md) |
| 1820-03-02 | The House accepts the compromise, admitting Missouri without restriction and closing the territory north of 36°30′ to slavery. — [Missouri Compromise](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/missouri-compromise.md) |
| 1820-03-06 – 1854 | **[Missouri Compromise](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/missouri-compromise.md)** (Law) |
| 1820-03-06 | President James Monroe signs the act. — [Missouri Compromise](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/missouri-compromise.md) |
| 1820-03-15 | Maine becomes the 23rd state. — [Missouri Compromise](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/missouri-compromise.md) |
| 1820-04-22 | Thomas Jefferson writes to John Holmes that the Missouri question struck him like a fire bell in the night. — [Missouri Compromise](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/missouri-compromise.md) |
| 1820-07-19 | Missouri adopts a constitution that protects slavery and directs the legislature to keep free Black people out of the state. — [Missouri Compromise](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/missouri-compromise.md) |
| 1821 | Mexico wins independence from Spain, and California becomes a Mexican province. — [California](../articles/states-and-territories/california.md) |
| 1821 | New York becomes the first state to write "due process of law" into its own constitution. — [Due process](../articles/rights-and-law/due-process.md) |
| 1821 | Russia claims the Pacific coast of North America north of the 51st parallel, extending its Alaska colony southward. — [Monroe Doctrine](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/monroe-doctrine.md) |
| 1821-06 | Under a second compromise brokered by Henry Clay, Missouri's legislature pledges that the disputed clauses will not be enforced. — [Missouri Compromise](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/missouri-compromise.md) |
| 1821-08-10 | Monroe proclaims Missouri the 24th state. — [Missouri Compromise](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/missouri-compromise.md) |
| 1822 – 1913-03-10 | **[Harriet Tubman](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/harriet-tubman.md)** (Person) |
| 1822 | Born Araminta "Minty" Ross, probably in February or early March, on Anthony Thompson's plantation at Peter's Neck, Dorchester County, Maryland. — [Harriet Tubman](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/harriet-tubman.md) |
| 1822-03 | Monroe formally recognizes Argentina, Peru, Colombia, Chile and Mexico, newly independent of Spain. — [Monroe Doctrine](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/monroe-doctrine.md) |
| 1823 | British Foreign Minister George Canning proposes a joint U.S.-British declaration against European intervention in the Americas. — [Monroe Doctrine](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/monroe-doctrine.md) |
| 1823 | Grounds federal land title in the doctrine of discovery in Johnson v. M'Intosh. — [John Marshall](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/john-marshall.md) |
| 1823 | Thomas Jefferson, writing to Justice William Johnson, criticizes the opinion as unnecessary and calls its mandamus discussion obiter dictum. — [Marbury v. Madison](../articles/rights-and-law/marbury-v-madison.md) |
| 1823-06-12 | Thomas Jefferson writes to Justice William Johnson criticizing Marbury and naming the people as the ultimate arbiter. — [Judicial review](../articles/constitution-and-government/judicial-review.md) |
| 1823-11 | Persuaded by Secretary of State John Quincy Adams, Monroe decides on a unilateral U.S. statement instead. — [Monroe Doctrine](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/monroe-doctrine.md) |
| 1823-12-02 | **[Monroe Doctrine](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/monroe-doctrine.md)** (Policy) |
| 1823-12-02 | James Monroe's annual message warns European powers against new colonization or intervention in the Americas. — [Roosevelt Corollary](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/roosevelt-corollary.md) |
| 1823-12-02 | Monroe announces the principles in his seventh annual message, at the opening of the 18th Congress. — [Monroe Doctrine](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/monroe-doctrine.md) |
| 1824 | In Osborn v. Bank of the United States the Court holds that Congress may give a national bank the powers essential to its operation. — [McCulloch v. Maryland](../articles/rights-and-law/mcculloch-v-maryland.md) |
| 1824 | Leads the popular and electoral vote in a four-way race but loses in the House of Representatives to John Quincy Adams. — [Andrew Jackson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/andrew-jackson.md) |
| 1824 | Reads Congress's commerce power broadly in Gibbons v. Ogden. — [John Marshall](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/john-marshall.md) |
| 1825-02-09 | Voting by state delegations, the House chooses John Quincy Adams as president on its first ballot. — [United States House of Representatives](../articles/constitution-and-government/house-of-representatives.md) |
| 1825-02-09 | With no electoral majority, the House chooses John Quincy Adams over Andrew Jackson, who had led in electoral and popular votes. — [Electoral College](../articles/democracy-and-elections/electoral-college.md) |
| 1826 | Dumont breaks his promise to free her; she leaves with her infant daughter Sophia and is taken in by the Van Wagenen family. — [Sojourner Truth](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/sojourner-truth.md) |
| 1826 | Sent to Baltimore to live with Hugh and Sophia Auld, where he begins learning to read. — [Frederick Douglass](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/frederick-douglass.md) |
| 1826-07-04 | Dies at Monticello, fifty years after the Declaration's approval. — [Thomas Jefferson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/thomas-jefferson.md) |
| 1826-12-23 | A U.S.–Hawaiian treaty indirectly recognizes the kingdom's independence. — [Hawaii](../articles/states-and-territories/hawaii.md) |
| 1827 | Samuel E. Cornish and John B. Russwurm found Freedom's Journal in New York. — [Abolitionism](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/abolitionism.md) |
| 1827 | Sarah Josepha Hale, editing the Ladies' Magazine in Boston, begins writing in favor of a national Thanksgiving. — [Thanksgiving](../articles/culture-and-arts/thanksgiving.md) |
| 1827 | The Cherokee Nation adopts a written constitution modeled on the U.S. Constitution. — [Cherokee Nation v. Georgia](../articles/native-nations/cherokee-nation-v-georgia.md) |
| 1827 | The Cherokee Nation adopts a written constitution; Georgia begins extending state jurisdiction over Cherokee territory. — [Indian Removal Act](../articles/native-nations/indian-removal-act.md) |
| 1827-07-04 | New York's emancipation law takes effect. — [Sojourner Truth](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/sojourner-truth.md) |
| 1827-07-04 | Slavery ends in New York under its 1817 law. — [Slavery in the United States](../articles/society-and-people/slavery-in-the-united-states.md) |
| 1828 | Georgia declares the Cherokee government abolished and places Cherokee citizens under state law; gold is found on Cherokee land in the north Georgia mountains. — [Cherokee Nation v. Georgia](../articles/native-nations/cherokee-nation-v-georgia.md) |
| 1828 | Gold is found on Native lands in Georgia. — [Indian Removal Act](../articles/native-nations/indian-removal-act.md) |
| 1828 | Thomas Dartmouth Rice first performs the blackface minstrel routine "Jump Jim Crow," source of the later name for segregation. — [Jim Crow laws](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/jim-crow-laws.md) |
| 1828 | Wins People v. Solomon Gedney in New York's courts, recovering her son Peter, who had been illegally sold to Alabama. — [Sojourner Truth](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/sojourner-truth.md) |
| 1828-12-22 | Rachel Jackson dies weeks after his victory over Adams. — [Andrew Jackson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/andrew-jackson.md) |
| 1829 | David Walker publishes his Appeal to the Colored Citizens of the World. — [Abolitionism](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/abolitionism.md) |
| 1829 | Mexico abolishes slavery. — [Mexican–American War](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/mexican-american-war.md) |
| 1829 | Sits in the Virginia Constitutional Convention of 1829–30. — [John Marshall](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/john-marshall.md) |
| 1829-03-04 | Inaugurated as the seventh president. — [Andrew Jackson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/andrew-jackson.md) |
| 1830-05-28 | **[Indian Removal Act](../articles/native-nations/indian-removal-act.md)** (Law) |
| 1830-05-28 | After passing the Senate 28 to 19 and the House 102 to 97, the Indian Removal Act is signed by President Jackson. — [Indian Removal Act](../articles/native-nations/indian-removal-act.md) |
| 1830-05-28 | President Andrew Jackson signs the Indian Removal Act. — [Cherokee Nation v. Georgia](../articles/native-nations/cherokee-nation-v-georgia.md) |
| 1830-05-28 | President Andrew Jackson signs the Indian Removal Act. — [United States](../articles/states-and-territories/united-states.md) |
| 1830-05-28 | Signs the Indian Removal Act. — [Andrew Jackson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/andrew-jackson.md) |
| 1830-12-06 | Jackson tells Congress that removal is nearing a 'happy consummation' and that two nations have agreed to terms. — [Indian Removal Act](../articles/native-nations/indian-removal-act.md) |
| 1831 | In Cherokee Nation v. Georgia the Supreme Court calls tribes "domestic dependent nations" and declines to hear the Cherokee suit. — [Tribal sovereignty](../articles/native-nations/tribal-sovereignty.md) |
| 1831 | Joseph Henry rings a bell at the far end of a mile-long wire with a powerful electromagnet and publishes an article suggesting an electric telegraph. — [Electric telegraph](../articles/science-and-innovation/telegraph.md) |
| 1831 | The Supreme Court in Cherokee Nation v. Georgia calls the Cherokees a 'domestic dependent nation' and declines to hear their suit; Choctaw removal begins. — [Indian Removal Act](../articles/native-nations/indian-removal-act.md) |
| 1831 | Tice Davids crosses the Ohio River from Kentucky; folklore says his enslaver claimed he had vanished on an underground road, one of several legends about the name. — [Underground Railroad](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/underground-railroad.md) |
| 1831 | William Lloyd Garrison founds The Liberator; a New York convention of free Black people rejects colonization. — [Abolitionism](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/abolitionism.md) |
| 1831-03-05 | The Cherokee Nation's motion for an injunction against Georgia is set for hearing before the Supreme Court; Georgia does not appear. — [Cherokee Nation v. Georgia](../articles/native-nations/cherokee-nation-v-georgia.md) |
| 1831-03-18 | **[Cherokee Nation v. Georgia](../articles/native-nations/cherokee-nation-v-georgia.md)** (CourtCase) |
| 1831-03-18 | The Court denies the injunction and dismisses the Cherokee Nation's bill for want of jurisdiction. — [Cherokee Nation v. Georgia](../articles/native-nations/cherokee-nation-v-georgia.md) |
| 1831-08-22 | Nat Turner's rebellion begins in Southampton County, Virginia. — [Slavery in the United States](../articles/society-and-people/slavery-in-the-united-states.md) |
| 1832 | By this year almost every state has its voters, rather than its legislature, choose presidential electors. — [Electoral College](../articles/democracy-and-elections/electoral-college.md) |
| 1832 | George Catlin sketches the Mandan game of tchung-kee, later painting it in 1832–1833. — [Chunkey](../articles/native-nations/chunkey.md) |
| 1832 | Holds in Worcester v. Georgia that Georgia's laws have no force in the Cherokee Nation. — [John Marshall](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/john-marshall.md) |
| 1832 | In Worcester v. Georgia the Court holds that Georgia law has no force within the Cherokee Nation. — [Tribal sovereignty](../articles/native-nations/tribal-sovereignty.md) |
| 1832 | In Worcester v. Georgia the Court holds that Georgia's laws have no force within the Cherokee Nation. — [Cherokee Nation v. Georgia](../articles/native-nations/cherokee-nation-v-georgia.md) |
| 1832 | In Worcester v. Georgia the Court rules that Georgia's laws have no force in Cherokee territory; Georgia and the president do not enforce the ruling. — [Indian Removal Act](../articles/native-nations/indian-removal-act.md) |
| 1832 | Samuel F. B. Morse reportedly conceives of electric signaling on a voyage home from Europe. — [Electric telegraph](../articles/science-and-innovation/telegraph.md) |
| 1832 | South Carolina passes an Ordinance of Nullification against the federal tariffs of 1828 and 1832. — [Federalism](../articles/constitution-and-government/federalism.md) |
| 1832-07-10 | President Andrew Jackson vetoes a bill to recharter the bank, arguing the Court's view does not bind Congress or the President. — [McCulloch v. Maryland](../articles/rights-and-law/mcculloch-v-maryland.md) |
| 1832-07-10 | Vetoes the bill to recharter the Second Bank of the United States. — [Andrew Jackson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/andrew-jackson.md) |
| 1832-12-10 | Issues his proclamation against South Carolina's nullification of the tariff. — [Andrew Jackson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/andrew-jackson.md) |
| 1833 | Barron v. Baltimore holds that the Fifth Amendment and the rest of the Bill of Rights limit only the federal government. — [Due process](../articles/rights-and-law/due-process.md) |
| 1833 | Chicago receives its first town charter; the Treaty of Chicago forces the Potawatomi to cede their last Illinois lands and move west of the Mississippi. — [Chicago](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/chicago.md) |
| 1833 | Congress passes the Force Bill and a Compromise Tariff, and South Carolina backs down. — [Federalism](../articles/constitution-and-government/federalism.md) |
| 1833 | In Barron v. Baltimore the Supreme Court holds that the Bill of Rights limits only the federal government. — [Bill of Rights](../articles/rights-and-law/bill-of-rights.md) |
| 1833 | Reformers from ten states found the American Anti-Slavery Society, pledged to immediate emancipation. — [Abolitionism](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/abolitionism.md) |
| 1833-12-01 | Dr. John Emerson, an army surgeon, takes the enslaved Dred Scott to Fort Armstrong in the free state of Illinois. — [Dred Scott v. Sandford](../articles/rights-and-law/dred-scott-v-sandford.md) |
| 1834 | First elected to the Illinois legislature. — [Abraham Lincoln](../articles/presidents-and-founders/abraham-lincoln.md) |
| 1834 | Fur traders William Sublette and Robert Campbell found the trading post that becomes Fort Laramie. — [Wyoming](../articles/states-and-territories/wyoming.md) |
| 1834 | Hired out to Edward Covey, a so-called slave breaker, and fights back against his beatings. — [Frederick Douglass](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/frederick-douglass.md) |
| 1834 | Mexico begins secularizing the missions; their lands pass largely to ranching families rather than to Native Californians. — [California](../articles/states-and-territories/california.md) |
| 1834-02 | **[Labor movement](../articles/economy-and-work/labor-movement.md)** (Movement) |
| 1834-02 | About 800 women mill workers in Lowell, Massachusetts, walk out against a 12.5 percent wage cut. — [Labor movement](../articles/economy-and-work/labor-movement.md) |
| 1834-03-28 | The Senate censures him, 26 to 20, over the removal of federal deposits from the Bank. — [Andrew Jackson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/andrew-jackson.md) |
| 1835 | A small Cherokee faction without authority under Cherokee law signs the Treaty of New Echota. — [Cherokee Nation v. Georgia](../articles/native-nations/cherokee-nation-v-georgia.md) |
| 1835-02 | The American Anti-Slavery Society's Anti-Slavery Record calls it 'The Liberty Bell', the earliest known use of the name. — [Liberty Bell](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/liberty-bell.md) |
| 1835-07-06 | Dies after 34 years as Chief Justice. — [John Marshall](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/john-marshall.md) |
| 1835-10-21 | A Boston mob breaks up a meeting of the Boston Female Anti-Slavery Society and threatens Garrison's life. — [Abolitionism](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/abolitionism.md) |
| 1835-12-29 | A minority Treaty Party signs the Treaty of New Echota without the authority of Principal Chief John Ross or the Cherokee government; the Second Seminole War begins the same year. — [Indian Removal Act](../articles/native-nations/indian-removal-act.md) |
| 1836 | After Richard M. Johnson falls one vote short of an electoral majority for vice president, the Senate elects him 33 to 16, the only time it has chosen a vice president. — [Electoral College](../articles/democracy-and-elections/electoral-college.md) |
| 1836 | Betsy Ross dies in Philadelphia; her grandson William Canby is eleven years old. — [Betsy Ross flag myth](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/betsy-ross-flag-myth.md) |
| 1836 | The House adopts the gag rule, barring discussion of abolitionist petitions. — [Abolitionism](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/abolitionism.md) |
| 1836 | The Second Bank's federal charter expires without renewal. — [McCulloch v. Maryland](../articles/rights-and-law/mcculloch-v-maryland.md) |
| 1836 | The Senate ratifies the New Echota treaty 31 to 15; most Muscogee (Creek) people are forced west. — [Indian Removal Act](../articles/native-nations/indian-removal-act.md) |
| 1836-05-04 | Emerson is transferred to Fort Snelling in the Wisconsin Territory, where the Missouri Compromise barred slavery; there Scott marries Harriet Robinson. — [Dred Scott v. Sandford](../articles/rights-and-law/dred-scott-v-sandford.md) |
| 1836-06-28 | Dies at Montpelier, holding about 100 people in slavery and freeing none in his will. — [James Madison](../articles/presidents-and-founders/james-madison.md) |
| 1836-10 | Between 1,500 and 2,000 Lowell workers strike over higher board charges and form the Factory Girls' Association. — [Labor movement](../articles/economy-and-work/labor-movement.md) |
| 1837 | **[Chicago](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/chicago.md)** (City) |
| 1837 | A mob in Illinois kills the abolitionist editor Elijah Lovejoy. — [Abolitionism](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/abolitionism.md) |
| 1837 | Illinois grants Chicago its first city charter, with six wards and a mayor. — [Chicago](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/chicago.md) |
| 1837 | Robert Purvis brings a vigilance committee to Philadelphia to aid freedom seekers. — [Underground Railroad](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/underground-railroad.md) |
| 1837 | The African Institute, later Cheyney University, opens in Pennsylvania, the first HBCU. — [African Americans](../articles/society-and-people/african-americans.md) |
| 1837-01 | The Senate votes to expunge the censure; his second term ends on March 4. — [Andrew Jackson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/andrew-jackson.md) |
| 1837-01-26 | Michigan is admitted to the Union with Detroit as its first state capital. — [Detroit](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/detroit.md) |
| 1837-03-03 | Congress adds two more seats, bringing the Court to nine. — [Supreme Court of the United States](../articles/constitution-and-government/supreme-court-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1838 | A mob burns Pennsylvania Hall in Philadelphia during the Anti-Slavery Convention of American Women. — [Abolitionism](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/abolitionism.md) |
| 1838 | A new state constitution disenfranchises free African Americans. — [Pennsylvania](../articles/states-and-territories/pennsylvania.md) |
| 1838 | Cherokees are forcibly removed to Indian Territory in 1838–39; about 4,000 of 16,000 die on the Trail of Tears. — [United States](../articles/states-and-territories/united-states.md) |
| 1838 | Morse demonstrates his telegraph in New York and Washington and receives a U.S. patent. — [Electric telegraph](../articles/science-and-innovation/telegraph.md) |
| 1838-05 | President Martin Van Buren orders General Winfield Scott to remove the remaining Cherokees, beginning the Trail of Tears. — [Cherokee Nation v. Georgia](../articles/native-nations/cherokee-nation-v-georgia.md) |
| 1838-05-23 | Under orders from President Martin Van Buren, the army begins rounding up Cherokees into stockades. — [Indian Removal Act](../articles/native-nations/indian-removal-act.md) |
| 1838-09-03 | Escapes to New York disguised as a sailor, using a free Black sailor's papers and a ticket paid for by Anna Murray. — [Frederick Douglass](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/frederick-douglass.md) |
| 1838-09-15 | Marries Anna Murray in New York; the couple settles in New Bedford, Massachusetts, as the Douglasses. — [Frederick Douglass](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/frederick-douglass.md) |
| 1839 | An English traveler describes the music of enslaved people on a Georgia rice plantation, an early account of music that would later evolve into the blues. — [Blues](../articles/culture-and-arts/blues.md) |
| 1839-03 | The last Cherokee detachments reach Indian Territory, in present-day Oklahoma. — [Indian Removal Act](../articles/native-nations/indian-removal-act.md) |
| 1839-09-06 | The reunited Cherokee Nation adopts a new constitution with its capital at Tahlequah. — [Indian Removal Act](../articles/native-nations/indian-removal-act.md) |
| 1840 | The American Anti-Slavery Society splits over women's leadership and politics; the Liberty Party runs James G. Birney for president. — [Abolitionism](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/abolitionism.md) |
| 1840 | The city's enslaved population peaks at 23,448, nearly a quarter of its residents. — [New Orleans](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/new-orleans.md) |
| 1841 | A new steel die with only six arrows and five-pointed stars departs from the law and is later called the illegal seal. — [Great Seal of the United States](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/great-seal-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1841 | Hired as a lecturer by the Massachusetts Anti-Slavery Society. — [Frederick Douglass](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/frederick-douglass.md) |
| 1841-04 | John Tyler becomes the first vice president to succeed a president who died in office and claims the office's full powers. — [Presidency of the United States](../articles/constitution-and-government/presidency-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1842 | In Commonwealth v. Hunt the Massachusetts Supreme Court holds that a union is not in itself a criminal conspiracy. — [Labor movement](../articles/economy-and-work/labor-movement.md) |
| 1842 | Prigg v. Pennsylvania upholds the 1793 law but frees state officials from enforcing it; the phrase underground railroad appears in the New York Spectator. — [Underground Railroad](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/underground-railroad.md) |
| 1843 | Congress appropriates $30,000 for an experimental line of about 40 miles from Washington to Baltimore. — [Electric telegraph](../articles/science-and-innovation/telegraph.md) |
| 1843 | Large-scale wagon migration west begins; emigrants cross the Continental Divide at South Pass. — [Wyoming](../articles/states-and-territories/wyoming.md) |
| 1843-06-01 | Takes the name Sojourner Truth and begins travelling to preach and speak against slavery. — [Sojourner Truth](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/sojourner-truth.md) |
| 1844 | Probably marries John Tubman, a free Black man, and takes the name Harriet. — [Harriet Tubman](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/harriet-tubman.md) |
| 1844-05-24 | Morse sends the first message on the line from the Supreme Court chamber in the Capitol to Baltimore. — [Electric telegraph](../articles/science-and-innovation/telegraph.md) |
| 1845 | Frederick Douglass publishes his Narrative. — [Abolitionism](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/abolitionism.md) |
| 1845 | Publishes Narrative of the Life of Frederick Douglass and begins a lecture tour of Britain and Ireland. — [Frederick Douglass](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/frederick-douglass.md) |
| 1845 | The Anthony family moves to a farm near Rochester, New York, where antislavery Quakers gather on Sundays. — [Susan B. Anthony](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/susan-b-anthony.md) |
| 1845-06-08 | Dies at the Hermitage, his cotton plantation near Nashville. — [Andrew Jackson](../articles/presidents-and-founders/andrew-jackson.md) |
| 1845-12 | Texas, annexed by joint resolution of Congress, enters the Union as a slave state; Mexico still claims it. — [Mexican–American War](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/mexican-american-war.md) |
| 1846 | The Magnetic Telegraph Company opens the first commercial line, from New York to Washington. — [Electric telegraph](../articles/science-and-innovation/telegraph.md) |
| 1846 | War between the United States and Mexico begins; in California, the Bear Flag Revolt breaks out. — [California](../articles/states-and-territories/california.md) |
| 1846-01-13 | President James K. Polk orders General Zachary Taylor to advance from Corpus Christi to the Rio Grande, into land Mexico considered its own. — [Mexican–American War](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/mexican-american-war.md) |
| 1846-02 | Workers drill out a hairline crack before Washington's Birthday; a new fissure opens and the bell is silenced for good. — [Liberty Bell](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/liberty-bell.md) |
| 1846-04-06 | Dred and Harriet Scott file separate freedom suits against Irene Emerson in the St. Louis Circuit Court. — [Dred Scott v. Sandford](../articles/rights-and-law/dred-scott-v-sandford.md) |
| 1846-04-25 – 1848-05-30 | **[Mexican–American War](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/mexican-american-war.md)** (Conflict) |
| 1846-04-25 | Mexican troops who crossed the Rio Grande ambush a U.S. patrol near Rancho de Carricitos, killing eleven American soldiers. — [Mexican–American War](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/mexican-american-war.md) |
| 1846-05-08 | Battle of Palo Alto, the first battle of the war; Taylor's army wins again at Resaca de la Palma the next day. — [Mexican–American War](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/mexican-american-war.md) |
| 1846-05-11 | Polk asks Congress to recognize that a state of war exists, blaming Mexico for beginning hostilities. — [Mexican–American War](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/mexican-american-war.md) |
| 1846-05-13 | War is formally declared after the House (174 to 14) and the Senate (40 to 2) approve it. — [Mexican–American War](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/mexican-american-war.md) |
| 1846-07 | The U.S. Navy's Pacific squadron occupies Monterey and San Francisco in Mexican California. — [Mexican–American War](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/mexican-american-war.md) |
| 1846-09 | Taylor captures Monterrey in northern Mexico; Polk repudiates the armistice Taylor grants. — [Mexican–American War](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/mexican-american-war.md) |
| 1847 | George Lippard's fictional story 'Ring, Grandfather, Ring' ties the bell to July 4, 1776. — [Liberty Bell](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/liberty-bell.md) |
| 1847 | Returns legally free after supporters pay Hugh Auld $711.66; launches the North Star in Rochester, New York. — [Frederick Douglass](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/frederick-douglass.md) |
| 1847-01 | A joint army-navy force under Stephen W. Kearny and Robert F. Stockton secures southern California. — [Mexican–American War](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/mexican-american-war.md) |
| 1847-02-23 | Taylor's outnumbered army defeats Santa Anna's at Buena Vista after two days of fighting. — [Mexican–American War](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/mexican-american-war.md) |
| 1847-03-09 | Winfield Scott's army of about 10,000 lands near Veracruz in the first large-scale U.S. amphibious assault. — [Mexican–American War](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/mexican-american-war.md) |
| 1847-03-16 | The governor signs the bill moving Michigan's capital from Detroit to Lansing. — [Detroit](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/detroit.md) |
| 1847-06-30 | The first trial ends against the Scotts on a technicality, and their lawyer wins a new trial. — [Dred Scott v. Sandford](../articles/rights-and-law/dred-scott-v-sandford.md) |
| 1847-09-14 | After battles at Cerro Gordo, Contreras, Churubusco, Molino del Rey and Chapultepec, Scott's army enters Mexico City. — [Mexican–American War](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/mexican-american-war.md) |
| 1847-12-22 | As a first-term Whig congressman, introduces the Spot Resolutions challenging the case for war with Mexico. — [Abraham Lincoln](../articles/presidents-and-founders/abraham-lincoln.md) |
| 1847-12-22 | Representative Abraham Lincoln introduces his 'Spot' Resolutions challenging Polk's account of where the war began. — [Mexican–American War](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/mexican-american-war.md) |
| 1848 | Newspapers form the Associated Press to pool telegraphed news. — [Electric telegraph](../articles/science-and-innovation/telegraph.md) |
| 1848 | Teaching in Canajoharie, New York, she learns that male teachers earn $10 a month and women $2.50. — [Susan B. Anthony](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/susan-b-anthony.md) |
| 1848 | The Illinois & Michigan Canal is completed, the first railroad opens, the telegraph arrives and the Board of Trade is founded. — [Chicago](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/chicago.md) |
| 1848-01-24 – 1855 | **[California Gold Rush](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/california-gold-rush.md)** (Event) |
| 1848-01-24 | Gold is found on the American River, setting off the Gold Rush. — [California](../articles/states-and-territories/california.md) |
| 1848-01-24 | James Marshall finds a gold nugget at the sawmill he is building for John Sutter on the South Fork of the American River at Coloma. — [California Gold Rush](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/california-gold-rush.md) |
| 1848-02-02 | Nicholas Trist, though recalled by Polk, signs the Treaty of Guadalupe Hidalgo with Mexican commissioners. — [Mexican–American War](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/mexican-american-war.md) |
| 1848-02-02 | The Treaty of Guadalupe Hidalgo is signed; Mexico cedes 55 percent of its territory, including present-day California, Nevada and Utah. — [The West](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/the-west.md) |
| 1848-02-02 | The Treaty of Guadalupe Hidalgo transfers California and other lands from Mexico to the United States. — [California](../articles/states-and-territories/california.md) |
| 1848-03-10 | The Senate approves the treaty after striking Article X, which protected Mexican land grants. — [Mexican–American War](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/mexican-american-war.md) |
| 1848-05-30 | Ratifications are exchanged at Querétaro and U.S. troops withdraw from Mexico City; Polk proclaims the treaty on July 4. — [Mexican–American War](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/mexican-american-war.md) |
| 1848-07 | California's military commander visits the diggings and finds about 4,000 people collecting gold; Coloma alone has about 4,000 people. — [California Gold Rush](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/california-gold-rush.md) |
| 1848-07 | Seconds Elizabeth Cady Stanton's suffrage resolution at the Seneca Falls women's rights convention. — [Frederick Douglass](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/frederick-douglass.md) |
| 1848-07-19 – 1920-08-26 | **[Women's suffrage movement](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/womens-suffrage-movement.md)** (Movement) |
| 1848-07-19 | The two-day women's rights convention at Seneca Falls, New York, opens and drafts the Declaration of Sentiments, which demands the vote. — [Women's suffrage movement](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/womens-suffrage-movement.md) |
| 1848-12-05 | President James K. Polk confirms the reports of gold to Congress and asks for a branch mint in California. — [California Gold Rush](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/california-gold-rush.md) |
| 1849 | About 25,000 emigrants travel overland to California, the first great year of the rush. — [California Gold Rush](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/california-gold-rush.md) |
| 1849 | After her enslaver Edward Brodess dies in March, she fears being sold, escapes alone in the late fall and reaches Philadelphia. — [Harriet Tubman](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/harriet-tubman.md) |
| 1849 | Chinese immigration to the United States begins in earnest with the California Gold Rush. — [Chinese Exclusion Act](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/chinese-exclusion-act.md) |
| 1849 | Forty-eight delegates in Monterey write a state constitution, published in English and Spanish, that limits voting to white men. — [California](../articles/states-and-territories/california.md) |
| 1849 | The U.S. Army buys Fort Laramie. — [Wyoming](../articles/states-and-territories/wyoming.md) |
| 1849-03-23 | Henry "Box" Brown is shipped in a crate by rail from Richmond to Philadelphia, a 27-hour journey. — [Underground Railroad](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/underground-railroad.md) |
| 1849-11-13 | California voters ratify a state constitution, paving the way for statehood. — [California Gold Rush](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/california-gold-rush.md) |
| 1850 | Congress begins fixing the size of the House before apportionment and adopts the Vinton (Hamilton) method. — [Census and apportionment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/census-and-apportionment.md) |
| 1850 | Congress passes a Fugitive Slave Act that denies accused people a jury trial. — [Slavery in the United States](../articles/society-and-people/slavery-in-the-united-states.md) |
| 1850 | Congress passes the Fugitive Slave Act; Tubman makes her first rescue, of her niece Kessiah and Kessiah's two children. — [Harriet Tubman](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/harriet-tubman.md) |
| 1850 | The Fugitive Slave Act penalizes Northerners who aid freedom seekers. — [Abolitionism](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/abolitionism.md) |
| 1850 | The Narrative of Sojourner Truth, dictated to Olive Gilbert, is published. — [Sojourner Truth](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/sojourner-truth.md) |
| 1850 | The United States and Britain sign the Clayton-Bulwer Treaty to limit rivalry over a proposed isthmian canal. — [Panama Canal](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/panama-canal.md) |
| 1850 | The legislature imposes a $20-a-month license fee on foreign miners, aimed chiefly at Latin American miners; it is repealed the next year. — [California Gold Rush](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/california-gold-rush.md) |
| 1850 | The legislature passes the Act for the Government and Protection of Indians, allowing Native people to be held as indentured servants. — [California](../articles/states-and-territories/california.md) |
| 1850-01-12 | A St. Louis jury finds the Scotts free. — [Dred Scott v. Sandford](../articles/rights-and-law/dred-scott-v-sandford.md) |
| 1850-04 | The first state legislature passes the Act for the Government and Protection of Indians, allowing Native people to be held as indentured laborers. — [California Gold Rush](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/california-gold-rush.md) |
| 1850-09-09 | **[California](../articles/states-and-territories/california.md)** (State) |
| 1850-09-09 | California is admitted as the 31st state, free of slavery, as part of the Compromise of 1850. — [California Gold Rush](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/california-gold-rush.md) |
| 1850-09-09 | California is admitted as the 31st state, free of slavery, as part of the Compromise of 1850. — [California](../articles/states-and-territories/california.md) |
| 1850-09-18 | The Fugitive Slave Act of 1850 is approved, commanding bystanders to help catch freedom seekers and penalizing anyone who aids them. — [Underground Railroad](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/underground-railroad.md) |
| 1851 | Amelia Bloomer introduces her to Elizabeth Cady Stanton in Seneca Falls, New York. — [Susan B. Anthony](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/susan-b-anthony.md) |
| 1851 | Railroads begin dispatching trains by telegraph; the company that becomes Western Union is incorporated. — [Electric telegraph](../articles/science-and-innovation/telegraph.md) |
| 1851 | The first Treaty of Fort Laramie sets terms for contact between Plains nations and Americans. — [Wyoming](../articles/states-and-territories/wyoming.md) |
| 1851-05-29 | Speaks at the Woman's Rights Convention in Akron, Ohio. — [Sojourner Truth](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/sojourner-truth.md) |
| 1851-06-21 | Marius Robinson's account of her Akron speech appears in the Anti-Slavery Bugle. — [Sojourner Truth](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/sojourner-truth.md) |
| 1851-09-11 | At Christiana, Pennsylvania, Black residents resist a Maryland slaveholder's attempt to recapture freedom seekers; the slaveholder is killed. — [Underground Railroad](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/underground-railroad.md) |
| 1851-11-18 | Illinois College professor Jonathan Baldwin Turner proposes state industrial universities at a farmers' convention in Putnam County, Illinois. — [Morrill Land-Grant College Act](../articles/science-and-innovation/morrill-land-grant-college-act.md) |
| 1852 | A state fugitive slave law makes it illegal for enslaved people to flee within California. — [California](../articles/states-and-territories/california.md) |
| 1852 | About 67,000 people arrive in California, 20,000 of them from China, the peak year; a new foreign miners' tax falls mainly on Chinese miners. — [California Gold Rush](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/california-gold-rush.md) |
| 1852-03-22 | The Missouri Supreme Court reverses, rejecting the principle of once free, always free. — [Dred Scott v. Sandford](../articles/rights-and-law/dred-scott-v-sandford.md) |
| 1852-07-05 | Delivers "What to the Slave Is the Fourth of July?" at Corinthian Hall, Rochester. — [Frederick Douglass](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/frederick-douglass.md) |
| 1852-07-08 | The U.S. Senate refuses to ratify 18 treaties negotiated with California tribes, which had promised them about 7.5 million acres. — [California](../articles/states-and-territories/california.md) |
| 1853 | In O'Reilly v. Morse (December Term 1853) the Supreme Court upholds Morse's patent but strikes down his broadest claim. — [Electric telegraph](../articles/science-and-innovation/telegraph.md) |
| 1854 | Anthony Burns escapes from Richmond; he is arrested in Boston and returned to slavery under the 1850 law. — [Underground Railroad](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/underground-railroad.md) |
| 1854 | The Consolidation Act merges the city with all of Philadelphia County, growing it from about 2 to nearly 130 square miles. — [Philadelphia](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/philadelphia.md) |
| 1854 | The Kansas-Nebraska Act repeals the 36°30′ restriction after 34 years. — [Missouri Compromise](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/missouri-compromise.md) |
| 1854 | The state capital moves to Sacramento after stints in San Jose, Vallejo and Benicia. — [California](../articles/states-and-territories/california.md) |
| 1854-05 | In the federal suit against John F. A. Sanford, Judge Robert W. Wells rules that Missouri law governs and finds for Sanford. — [Dred Scott v. Sandford](../articles/rights-and-law/dred-scott-v-sandford.md) |
| 1854-12-25 | Rescues her three remaining brothers from Dorchester County. — [Harriet Tubman](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/harriet-tubman.md) |
| 1856 | Works as an agent of the American Anti-Slavery Society. — [Susan B. Anthony](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/susan-b-anthony.md) |
| 1856-02 | The U.S. Supreme Court hears the first oral argument; it orders re-argument, held in December 1856. — [Dred Scott v. Sandford](../articles/rights-and-law/dred-scott-v-sandford.md) |
| 1857 | Brings her aging parents north after learning her father faces arrest. — [Harriet Tubman](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/harriet-tubman.md) |
| 1857 | Dred Scott v. Sandford becomes the second federal statute the Court holds unconstitutional, more than fifty years after Marbury. — [Marbury v. Madison](../articles/rights-and-law/marbury-v-madison.md) |
| 1857 | In Dred Scott the Court suggests that freeing an enslaved man would deprive his owner of property without due process. — [Due process](../articles/rights-and-law/due-process.md) |
| 1857 | In Dred Scott v. Sandford the Supreme Court declares the Missouri Compromise unconstitutional. — [Missouri Compromise](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/missouri-compromise.md) |
| 1857 | In Scott v. Sandford the Court strikes down the Missouri Compromise, the next federal statute it holds unconstitutional. — [Judicial review](../articles/constitution-and-government/judicial-review.md) |
| 1857 | Moves to Harmonia, Michigan, near Battle Creek. — [Sojourner Truth](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/sojourner-truth.md) |
| 1857-03-04 | In his inaugural address, President James Buchanan says he will submit to the Court's coming decision. — [Dred Scott v. Sandford](../articles/rights-and-law/dred-scott-v-sandford.md) |
| 1857-03-06 | **[Dred Scott v. Sandford](../articles/rights-and-law/dred-scott-v-sandford.md)** (CourtCase) |
| 1857-03-06 | Chief Justice Roger B. Taney announces the Dred Scott decision. — [Supreme Court of the United States](../articles/constitution-and-government/supreme-court-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1857-03-06 | The Court rules 7 to 2 against Scott, with Chief Justice Roger B. Taney delivering the opinion of the Court. — [Dred Scott v. Sandford](../articles/rights-and-law/dred-scott-v-sandford.md) |
| 1857-05-26 | Taylor Blow emancipates Dred and Harriet Scott. — [Dred Scott v. Sandford](../articles/rights-and-law/dred-scott-v-sandford.md) |
| 1857-12-14 | Representative Justin Smith Morrill of Vermont introduces a national land-grant college bill. — [Morrill Land-Grant College Act](../articles/science-and-innovation/morrill-land-grant-college-act.md) |
| 1858 | Meets John Brown at St. Catharines, Ontario, and recruits support for his plans, though she does not join the 1859 Harpers Ferry raid. — [Harriet Tubman](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/harriet-tubman.md) |
| 1858-06-16 | Gives the "House Divided" speech; later loses the Senate race to Stephen A. Douglas. — [Abraham Lincoln](../articles/presidents-and-founders/abraham-lincoln.md) |
| 1858-09-17 | Dred Scott dies of tuberculosis in St. Louis. — [Dred Scott v. Sandford](../articles/rights-and-law/dred-scott-v-sandford.md) |
| 1858-10-27 – 1919-01-06 | **[Theodore Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/theodore-roosevelt.md)** (Person) |
| 1858-10-27 | Born at 28 East 20th Street in New York City. — [Theodore Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/theodore-roosevelt.md) |
| 1859 | Commercial oil production begins near Titusville. — [Pennsylvania](../articles/states-and-territories/pennsylvania.md) |
| 1859-02-24 | President James Buchanan vetoes the bill passed by Congress, citing cost and constitutional limits on federal power. — [Morrill Land-Grant College Act](../articles/science-and-innovation/morrill-land-grant-college-act.md) |
| 1859-05 | Buys a house and seven acres near Auburn, New York, from Senator William H. Seward. — [Harriet Tubman](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/harriet-tubman.md) |
| 1860 | The South produces about two-thirds of the world's cotton, held in bondage by nearly four million enslaved people. — [Economic history of the United States](../articles/economy-and-work/economic-history-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1860 | The census counts 3,953,760 enslaved people, the peak and the last census to count them. — [Slavery in the United States](../articles/society-and-people/slavery-in-the-united-states.md) |
| 1860 | The first federal census of California counts about 308,000 residents. — [California Gold Rush](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/california-gold-rush.md) |
| 1860-11-04 | Elected 16th president with a majority of electoral votes from the free states. — [Abraham Lincoln](../articles/presidents-and-founders/abraham-lincoln.md) |
| 1860-12 | Last rescue mission to Maryland brings away seven people. — [Harriet Tubman](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/harriet-tubman.md) |
| 1860-12-20 | A South Carolina convention votes 169 to 0 to secede after Abraham Lincoln's election. — [American Civil War](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/american-civil-war.md) |
| 1860-12-20 | South Carolina secedes, citing northern hostility to slavery and northern laws obstructing the return of fugitives from slavery. — [Federalism](../articles/constitution-and-government/federalism.md) |
| 1861 | Congress passes the Corwin Amendment, which would have shielded slavery in the states; it is never ratified. — [Thirteenth Amendment](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/thirteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1861 | Morrill reintroduces the bill after Southern states secede. — [Morrill Land-Grant College Act](../articles/science-and-innovation/morrill-land-grant-college-act.md) |
| 1861-02-04 | Delegates from seven seceded states meet in Montgomery, Alabama, and choose Jefferson Davis as Confederate president. — [American Civil War](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/american-civil-war.md) |
| 1861-03-04 | Abraham Lincoln's first inaugural address argues that Court rulings bind the parties but should not irrevocably fix national policy. — [Judicial review](../articles/constitution-and-government/judicial-review.md) |
| 1861-03-04 | Inaugurated after seven Southern states had seceded. — [Abraham Lincoln](../articles/presidents-and-founders/abraham-lincoln.md) |
| 1861-04-12 – 1865-04-09 | **[American Civil War](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/american-civil-war.md)** (Conflict) |
| 1861-04-12 | Confederate forces fire on Fort Sumter in Charleston Harbor; it surrenders the next day and Lincoln calls for 75,000 volunteers. — [American Civil War](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/american-civil-war.md) |
| 1861-05 | At Fort Monroe, General Benjamin Butler begins receiving people fleeing slavery as "contraband of war." — [American Civil War](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/american-civil-war.md) |
| 1861-10-24 | The first transcontinental telegraph line is completed, and the Pony Express soon shuts down. — [Electric telegraph](../articles/science-and-innovation/telegraph.md) |
| 1862 | Arrives at Port Royal, South Carolina, to serve the Union Army as a scout, spy, cook and nurse. — [Harriet Tubman](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/harriet-tubman.md) |
| 1862 | Congress passes the Homestead Act, offering 160-acre claims to settlers who live on and improve the land for five years. — [The West](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/the-west.md) |
| 1862 | Union gunboats attack Vicksburg from upriver in the spring but eventually withdraw; Grant's first overland and river attempts follow that fall. — [Siege of Vicksburg](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/siege-of-vicksburg.md) |
| 1862-03 | The ironclads CSS Virginia and USS Monitor fight to a draw near Hampton Roads. — [Chesapeake Bay](../articles/geography-and-environment/chesapeake-bay.md) |
| 1862-04 | Congress abolishes slavery in the District of Columbia. — [American Civil War](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/american-civil-war.md) |
| 1862-06 | Congress bans slavery in all western territories, undoing the decision's holding on territorial slavery. — [Dred Scott v. Sandford](../articles/rights-and-law/dred-scott-v-sandford.md) |
| 1862-06 | Laura Towne and Ellen Murray open the Penn School on St. Helena Island, South Carolina, for formerly enslaved Sea Islanders. — [Gullah Geechee](../articles/society-and-people/gullah-geechee.md) |
| 1862-07-02 | **[Morrill Land-Grant College Act](../articles/science-and-innovation/morrill-land-grant-college-act.md)** (Law) |
| 1862-07-02 | Lincoln signs the Morrill Act; states in rebellion are excluded from its grants. — [Morrill Land-Grant College Act](../articles/science-and-innovation/morrill-land-grant-college-act.md) |
| 1862-09-17 | The Battle of Antietam, the bloodiest single day in American history, gives Lincoln the victory he awaited to announce emancipation. — [American Civil War](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/american-civil-war.md) |
| 1862-12-26 | Thirty-eight Dakota men are hanged at Mankato after Lincoln commutes most of the death sentences. — [Abraham Lincoln](../articles/presidents-and-founders/abraham-lincoln.md) |
| 1863 – 1877 | **[Reconstruction era](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/reconstruction-era.md)** (Era) |
| 1863 | California repeals its laws legalizing Indian indenture, and African Americans win the right to testify in court. — [California](../articles/states-and-territories/california.md) |
| 1863 | Helps found the Women's Loyal National League to petition for emancipation. — [Susan B. Anthony](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/susan-b-anthony.md) |
| 1863 | In the fall, President Abraham Lincoln offers amnesty and lets loyal Southerners form new state governments once 10 percent of voters swear an oath of loyalty. — [Reconstruction era](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/reconstruction-era.md) |
| 1863-01-01 | The Emancipation Proclamation takes effect in areas in rebellion. — [Abraham Lincoln](../articles/presidents-and-founders/abraham-lincoln.md) |
| 1863-01-01 | The Emancipation Proclamation takes effect in areas under Confederate control. — [American Civil War](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/american-civil-war.md) |
| 1863-01-01 | The Emancipation Proclamation takes effect, but only in areas in rebellion. — [Thirteenth Amendment](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/thirteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1863-03-03 | A tenth seat is authorized during the Civil War. — [Supreme Court of the United States](../articles/constitution-and-government/supreme-court-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1863-04-23 | Frances Dana Gage publishes her dialect version of the Akron speech in The Independent. — [Sojourner Truth](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/sojourner-truth.md) |
| 1863-04-30 | Grant's army crosses the Mississippi from Louisiana into Mississippi south of Vicksburg on the night of April 30–May 1. — [Siege of Vicksburg](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/siege-of-vicksburg.md) |
| 1863-05 | The War Department creates the Bureau of Colored Troops to organize Black regiments. — [American Civil War](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/american-civil-war.md) |
| 1863-05 | The War Department creates the Bureau of Colored Troops; about 179,000 Black men go on to serve in the Union Army. — [African Americans](../articles/society-and-people/african-americans.md) |
| 1863-05-01 | Union victory at Port Gibson opens an inland campaign that wins at Raymond, Jackson, Champion Hill and the Big Black River by May 17. — [Siege of Vicksburg](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/siege-of-vicksburg.md) |
| 1863-05-18 – 1863-07-04 | **[Siege of Vicksburg](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/siege-of-vicksburg.md)** (Conflict) |
| 1863-05-18 | Grant's Army of the Tennessee reaches the Confederate defenses of Vicksburg. — [Siege of Vicksburg](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/siege-of-vicksburg.md) |
| 1863-05-19 | Sherman's corps assaults the Stockade Redan and is repulsed with about 1,000 Union casualties. — [Siege of Vicksburg](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/siege-of-vicksburg.md) |
| 1863-05-22 | A second general assault on a three-mile front fails, and Grant settles into a siege. — [Siege of Vicksburg](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/siege-of-vicksburg.md) |
| 1863-06-02 | Helps lead the Combahee River raid with Colonel James Montgomery, freeing more than 700 enslaved people. — [Harriet Tubman](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/harriet-tubman.md) |
| 1863-06-07 | Black Union regiments help repel a Confederate attack on the supply post at Milliken's Bend, Louisiana. — [Siege of Vicksburg](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/siege-of-vicksburg.md) |
| 1863-06-25 | Union engineers explode 2,200 pounds of black powder under a Confederate redan on the Jackson Road; the crater fight gains no ground. — [Siege of Vicksburg](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/siege-of-vicksburg.md) |
| 1863-07 | Union forces under Meade defeat Lee's invading army at Gettysburg. — [Pennsylvania](../articles/states-and-territories/pennsylvania.md) |
| 1863-07-02 | Eastern Shoshone leaders, among them Washakie, sign the first Fort Bridger treaty. — [Wyoming](../articles/states-and-territories/wyoming.md) |
| 1863-07-03 | Pemberton and Grant meet between the lines; that night Grant offers to parole the garrison. — [Siege of Vicksburg](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/siege-of-vicksburg.md) |
| 1863-07-03 | Pickett's Charge fails, ending the three-day Battle of Gettysburg. — [American Civil War](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/american-civil-war.md) |
| 1863-07-04 | At 10 a.m. some 29,000 Confederates march out, stack their arms and surrender the city. — [Siege of Vicksburg](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/siege-of-vicksburg.md) |
| 1863-07-04 | Confederate forces surrender Vicksburg, Mississippi, ending a campaign for control of the river. — [Mississippi River](../articles/geography-and-environment/mississippi-river.md) |
| 1863-07-04 | Vicksburg surrenders to Ulysses S. Grant, splitting the Confederacy in two. — [American Civil War](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/american-civil-war.md) |
