| 1863-07-09 | Port Hudson, Louisiana, surrenders after learning of Vicksburg's fall, opening the whole Mississippi to Union navigation. — [Siege of Vicksburg](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/siege-of-vicksburg.md) |
| 1863-08 | The U.S. Army begins forcing Navajo people on the Long Walk to Bosque Redondo, where they are held until 1866. — [The West](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/the-west.md) |
| 1863-08-10 | Meets President Lincoln to protest unequal pay and treatment of Black soldiers. — [Frederick Douglass](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/frederick-douglass.md) |
| 1863-08-26 | Lincoln's public letter to James C. Conkling celebrates the reopened river and credits Black soldiers. — [Siege of Vicksburg](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/siege-of-vicksburg.md) |
| 1863-10-03 | Abraham Lincoln proclaims the last Thursday of November a national day of thanksgiving, after a letter from Hale dated September 28. — [Thanksgiving](../articles/culture-and-arts/thanksgiving.md) |
| 1863-11-19 | Delivers the Gettysburg Address. — [Abraham Lincoln](../articles/presidents-and-founders/abraham-lincoln.md) |
| 1864-02-09 | Senator Charles Sumner presents the first 100,000 signatures from the Women's Loyal National League's emancipation petition. — [Abolitionism](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/abolitionism.md) |
| 1864-02-10 | The Senate Judiciary Committee under Lyman Trumbull reports the amendment to the full Senate. — [Thirteenth Amendment](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/thirteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1864-02-16 | Her copyright claim for cartes de visite titled "I sell the Shadow to Support the Substance" is recorded. — [Sojourner Truth](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/sojourner-truth.md) |
| 1864-03-01 | The House passes the first bill for a freedmen's bureau by a vote of 69 to 67. — [Freedmen's Bureau](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/freedmens-bureau.md) |
| 1864-04-08 | The Senate passes the amendment, 38 to 6. — [Thirteenth Amendment](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/thirteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1864-06 | Congress grants Black soldiers pay equal to white soldiers, retroactively. — [American Civil War](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/american-civil-war.md) |
| 1864-09-02 | William T. Sherman's army captures Atlanta before marching to the sea. — [American Civil War](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/american-civil-war.md) |
| 1864-10-29 | Meets President Abraham Lincoln at the White House while working for the National Freedman's Relief Association. — [Sojourner Truth](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/sojourner-truth.md) |
| 1864-11-08 | Reelected president. — [Abraham Lincoln](../articles/presidents-and-founders/abraham-lincoln.md) |
| 1864-11-29 | Colorado militia under John Chivington kill about 200 Cheyenne men, women and children at Sand Creek. — [The West](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/the-west.md) |
| 1865 | The Union Stock Yard opens at 39th and Halsted Streets. — [Chicago](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/chicago.md) |
| 1865 | The United States backs Mexican President Benito Juárez against the French-supported Emperor Maximilian. — [Monroe Doctrine](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/monroe-doctrine.md) |
| 1865 | The end of the Civil War closes the era of escape from slavery in the United States. — [Underground Railroad](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/underground-railroad.md) |
| 1865 | The federal government establishes the Colorado River Reservation for the Mohave and Chemehuevi peoples. — [Colorado River](../articles/geography-and-environment/colorado-river.md) |
| 1865-01 | General William T. Sherman's Special Field Order No. 15 sets aside abandoned coastal land for freedpeople. — [Freedmen's Bureau](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/freedmens-bureau.md) |
| 1865-01-16 | Sherman's Special Field Order No. 15 sets aside the Sea Islands and a coastal strip from Charleston to the St. Johns River for Black families in forty-acre plots. — [Gullah Geechee](../articles/society-and-people/gullah-geechee.md) |
| 1865-01-31 | Congress passes the Thirteenth Amendment abolishing slavery; it is ratified by the end of the year. — [Reconstruction era](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/reconstruction-era.md) |
| 1865-01-31 | The House passes the Thirteenth Amendment, 119 to 56, after the Senate had passed it in April 1864. — [Abolitionism](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/abolitionism.md) |
| 1865-01-31 | The House passes the amendment, 119 to 56, after earlier votes fell short. — [Thirteenth Amendment](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/thirteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1865-02-01 | Lincoln approves the joint resolution sending the amendment to the states. — [Thirteenth Amendment](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/thirteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1865-02-06 | Lakota and Cheyenne fighters, retaliating for the Sand Creek Massacre, attack the Mud Springs telegraph station in Nebraska; its operator wires for troops. — [Electric telegraph](../articles/science-and-innovation/telegraph.md) |
| 1865-03-03 – 1872-06 | **[Freedmen's Bureau](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/freedmens-bureau.md)** (Institution) |
| 1865-03-03 | Congress passes the act creating the bureau in the War Department, and President Lincoln signs it the same day. — [Freedmen's Bureau](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/freedmens-bureau.md) |
| 1865-04-09 | Robert E. Lee surrenders the Army of Northern Virginia to Grant at Appomattox. — [American Civil War](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/american-civil-war.md) |
| 1865-04-14 | Lincoln is shot and dies the next morning; Andrew Johnson becomes president and begins a lenient Presidential Reconstruction. — [Reconstruction era](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/reconstruction-era.md) |
| 1865-04-14 | Shot by John Wilkes Booth at Ford's Theatre; dies the next morning. — [Abraham Lincoln](../articles/presidents-and-founders/abraham-lincoln.md) |
| 1865-05 | President Andrew Johnson appoints Major General Oliver Otis Howard as commissioner. — [Freedmen's Bureau](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/freedmens-bureau.md) |
| 1865-06-19 | General Order No. 3 announces freedom in Galveston, Texas, the origin of Juneteenth. — [Slavery in the United States](../articles/society-and-people/slavery-in-the-united-states.md) |
| 1865-12 | Alabama adopts a Black Code controlling the labor and movement of freed people. — [Jim Crow laws](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/jim-crow-laws.md) |
| 1865-12-06 | **[Thirteenth Amendment](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/thirteenth-amendment.md)** (Law) |
| 1865-12-06 | Georgia becomes the 27th state to ratify, completing the three-fourths needed. — [Thirteenth Amendment](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/thirteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1865-12-06 | The Thirteenth Amendment is ratified, abolishing slavery except as punishment for crime. — [United States](../articles/states-and-territories/united-states.md) |
| 1865-12-06 | The Thirteenth Amendment is ratified, abolishing slavery nationwide. — [Slavery in the United States](../articles/society-and-people/slavery-in-the-united-states.md) |
| 1865-12-06 | The Thirteenth Amendment is ratified, abolishing slavery throughout the United States. — [Abolitionism](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/abolitionism.md) |
| 1865-12-13 | Congress creates the Joint Committee of Fifteen on Reconstruction. — [Fourteenth Amendment](../articles/rights-and-law/fourteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1865-12-18 | Secretary of State William H. Seward proclaims the amendment ratified. — [Thirteenth Amendment](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/thirteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1866 | Congress passes the Civil Rights Act of 1866 over Johnson's veto; the Ku Klux Klan is organized in Pulaski, Tennessee; deadly riots against Black residents erupt in Memphis and New Orleans. — [Reconstruction era](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/reconstruction-era.md) |
| 1866 | Elizabeth Cady Stanton and Susan B. Anthony help form the American Equal Rights Association to seek suffrage for Black Americans and women alike. — [Women's suffrage movement](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/womens-suffrage-movement.md) |
| 1866 | Western Union absorbs its last major rivals, and the first successful Atlantic telegraph cable is laid. — [Electric telegraph](../articles/science-and-innovation/telegraph.md) |
| 1866-01-23 | New Jersey's governor signs a state constitutional amendment ending slavery there. — [Slavery in the United States](../articles/society-and-people/slavery-in-the-united-states.md) |
| 1866-02-19 | Johnson vetoes a bill to extend the bureau; the Senate fails to override the next day. — [Freedmen's Bureau](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/freedmens-bureau.md) |
| 1866-04 | Congress passes the Civil Rights Act of 1866. — [Fourteenth Amendment](../articles/rights-and-law/fourteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1866-06-08 | The Senate passes the amendment, 33 to 11. — [Fourteenth Amendment](../articles/rights-and-law/fourteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1866-06-13 | Congress passes the Fourteenth Amendment, guaranteeing birthright citizenship, due process and equal protection. — [Reconstruction era](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/reconstruction-era.md) |
| 1866-06-13 | The House approves the proposed Fourteenth Amendment, 120-32, carrying the equal protection language. — [Equal protection](../articles/rights-and-law/equal-protection.md) |
| 1866-06-13 | The House passes the amendment, 120 to 32. — [Fourteenth Amendment](../articles/rights-and-law/fourteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1866-06-16 | The joint resolution is submitted to the states. — [Fourteenth Amendment](../articles/rights-and-law/fourteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1866-07-16 | A second Freedmen's Bureau Act becomes law over Johnson's veto, extending the agency for two years. — [Freedmen's Bureau](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/freedmens-bureau.md) |
| 1866-07-23 | Congress reduces the authorized seats to seven, to be reached as vacancies occur. — [Supreme Court of the United States](../articles/constitution-and-government/supreme-court-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1866-07-30 | White mobs, joined by police, kill nearly fifty people at the Mechanics' Institute during a convention seeking Black men's voting rights. — [New Orleans](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/new-orleans.md) |
| 1867 | Congress enfranchises Black men in the District of Columbia and the territories and requires seceded states to guarantee Black male suffrage as a condition of readmission. — [Fifteenth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/fifteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1867 | The Military Reconstruction Act places the bureau under the commanders of five military districts. — [Freedmen's Bureau](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/freedmens-bureau.md) |
| 1867-03-02 | The Reconstruction Act places former Confederate states except Tennessee under military districts. — [Fourteenth Amendment](../articles/rights-and-law/fourteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1867-03-02 | The first Reconstruction Act becomes law over Johnson's veto, placing the former Confederate states except Tennessee under five military districts. — [Reconstruction era](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/reconstruction-era.md) |
| 1867-03-30 | Secretary of State William H. Seward and Russian minister Edouard de Stoeckl sign the treaty of cession. — [Alaska](../articles/states-and-territories/alaska.md) |
| 1867-04-09 | The Senate approves the treaty 37–2. — [Alaska](../articles/states-and-territories/alaska.md) |
| 1867-06-24 | Chinese workers of the Central Pacific Railroad stop work along a 30-mile stretch between Cisco and Truckee, California, demanding higher pay and shorter hours. — [Chinese Americans](../articles/society-and-people/chinese-americans.md) |
| 1867-10-18 | Alaska, bought from Russia for $7.2 million, is formally transferred to the United States. — [United States](../articles/states-and-territories/united-states.md) |
| 1867-10-18 | Russia formally transfers the territory to the United States. — [Alaska](../articles/states-and-territories/alaska.md) |
| 1868 | Becomes an editor, with Stanton, of the newspaper The Revolution. — [Susan B. Anthony](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/susan-b-anthony.md) |
| 1868 | Ratification of the Fourteenth Amendment gives later courts the Due Process Clause through which most of the Bill of Rights is applied to the states. — [Bill of Rights](../articles/rights-and-law/bill-of-rights.md) |
| 1868 | The Burlingame Treaty secures free migration between the United States and China. — [Chinese Exclusion Act](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/chinese-exclusion-act.md) |
| 1868 | The Fourteenth Amendment replaces the three-fifths formula with a count of the whole number of persons. — [Census and apportionment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/census-and-apportionment.md) |
| 1868 | The Fourteenth Amendment's citizenship clause makes all persons born in the United States citizens, overturning the decision's citizenship holding. — [Dred Scott v. Sandford](../articles/rights-and-law/dred-scott-v-sandford.md) |
| 1868-02-23 – 1963-08-27 | **[W. E. B. Du Bois](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/w-e-b-du-bois.md)** (Person) |
| 1868-02-23 | Born in Great Barrington, Massachusetts. — [W. E. B. Du Bois](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/w-e-b-du-bois.md) |
| 1868-06-22 | Arkansas becomes the first state readmitted to congressional representation under the Reconstruction Acts. — [Reconstruction era](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/reconstruction-era.md) |
| 1868-07-03 | The second Fort Bridger treaty sets aside about 3.2 million acres in the Wind River Valley for the Eastern Shoshone. — [Wyoming](../articles/states-and-territories/wyoming.md) |
| 1868-07-09 | **[Equal protection](../articles/rights-and-law/equal-protection.md)** (Right) |
| 1868-07-09 | **[Fourteenth Amendment](../articles/rights-and-law/fourteenth-amendment.md)** (Law) |
| 1868-07-09 | Louisiana and South Carolina ratify, completing the three-fourths needed. — [Fourteenth Amendment](../articles/rights-and-law/fourteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1868-07-09 | Ratification of the Fourteenth Amendment extends a due process guarantee against the states. — [Due process](../articles/rights-and-law/due-process.md) |
| 1868-07-09 | Ratification of the Fourteenth Amendment writes the Equal Protection Clause into the Constitution. — [Equal protection](../articles/rights-and-law/equal-protection.md) |
| 1868-07-14 | After more than a year's delay, the House votes 113–43 to fund the $7.2 million purchase. — [Alaska](../articles/states-and-territories/alaska.md) |
| 1868-07-25 | Congress creates Wyoming Territory; the 1868 Fort Laramie treaty negotiated with Red Cloud closes the Bozeman Trail the same year. — [Wyoming](../articles/states-and-territories/wyoming.md) |
| 1868-07-28 | Secretary of State William H. Seward certifies ratification by 28 states. — [Fourteenth Amendment](../articles/rights-and-law/fourteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1868-07-28 | The Fourteenth Amendment is certified as ratified by 28 of 37 states. — [Reconstruction era](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/reconstruction-era.md) |
| 1869 | Congress cuts most of the bureau's funding; offices close but educational work continues for a time. — [Freedmen's Bureau](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/freedmens-bureau.md) |
| 1869 | Sarah Bradford publishes the first biography of Tubman; Tubman marries Civil War veteran Nelson Davis in Auburn. — [Harriet Tubman](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/harriet-tubman.md) |
| 1869 | Skilled garment cutters in Philadelphia found the Knights of Labor. — [Labor movement](../articles/economy-and-work/labor-movement.md) |
| 1869 | The Central Pacific and Union Pacific railroads meet in Utah, completing the first transcontinental line. — [The West](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/the-west.md) |
| 1869 | The movement splits over the Fifteenth Amendment into the National and American Woman Suffrage Associations; Wyoming Territory enfranchises women. — [Women's suffrage movement](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/womens-suffrage-movement.md) |
| 1869 | With Stanton, forms the National Woman Suffrage Association after the movement splits over the Fifteenth Amendment. — [Susan B. Anthony](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/susan-b-anthony.md) |
| 1869 | Wyoming Territory gives women the vote on equal terms with men, and keeps it at statehood. — [Nineteenth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/nineteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1869-01-11 | Representative George Boutwell proposes a suffrage amendment in the House. — [Fifteenth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/fifteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1869-02-26 | After a conference committee narrows the text to racial discrimination, Congress gives final approval (House 144 to 44, Senate 39 to 13). — [Fifteenth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/fifteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1869-04-10 | Congress fixes the Court at nine justices, where it has remained. — [Supreme Court of the United States](../articles/constitution-and-government/supreme-court-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1869-05-10 | At Promontory Summit, Utah Territory, eight Chinese workers move the final rail into place for the transcontinental railroad. — [Chinese Americans](../articles/society-and-people/chinese-americans.md) |
| 1869-05-12 | At the American Equal Rights Association meeting, argues that Black men's voting rights are a matter of life and death. — [Frederick Douglass](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/frederick-douglass.md) |
| 1869-05-12 | The American Equal Rights Association splits over the amendment's omission of women at its annual meeting. — [Fifteenth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/fifteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1869-05-19 | The territorial government is formally inaugurated. — [Wyoming](../articles/states-and-territories/wyoming.md) |
| 1869-05-24 | John Wesley Powell's expedition launches four boats at Green River, Wyoming; it leaves the Grand Canyon on August 28. — [Colorado River](../articles/geography-and-environment/colorado-river.md) |
| 1869-12-10 | Governor John Campbell signs the act granting the territory's women the right to vote and hold office. — [Wyoming](../articles/states-and-territories/wyoming.md) |
| 1870 | **[Betsy Ross flag myth](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/betsy-ross-flag-myth.md)** (Myth) |
| 1870 – 1900 | **[Gilded Age](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/gilded-age.md)** (Era) |
| 1870 | California's Native population has fallen below 30,000; the foreign miners' tax is voided. — [California Gold Rush](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/california-gold-rush.md) |
| 1870 | Campaigns for federal land grants to formerly enslaved people and meets President Ulysses S. Grant. — [Sojourner Truth](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/sojourner-truth.md) |
| 1870 | Congress makes Thanksgiving a holiday in the District of Columbia, leaving its date to the President. — [Thanksgiving](../articles/culture-and-arts/thanksgiving.md) |
| 1870 | The Washburn–Langford–Doane Expedition explores the plateau and names Old Faithful. — [Yellowstone National Park](../articles/geography-and-environment/yellowstone-national-park.md) |
| 1870 | The bureau supports more than 1,500 schools serving over 100,000 pupils. — [Freedmen's Bureau](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/freedmens-bureau.md) |
| 1870 | William Canby reads a paper to the Historical Society of Pennsylvania telling the family story of the first flag. — [Betsy Ross flag myth](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/betsy-ross-flag-myth.md) |
| 1870-02-03 | **[Fifteenth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/fifteenth-amendment.md)** (Law) |
| 1870-02-03 | The amendment is ratified by three-fourths of the states. — [Fifteenth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/fifteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1870-02-17 | Esther Hobart Morris is appointed justice of the peace at South Pass City. — [Wyoming](../articles/states-and-territories/wyoming.md) |
| 1870-02-25 | Hiram Revels is sworn in as the first African American senator. — [United States Congress](../articles/constitution-and-government/united-states-congress.md) |
| 1870-05-19 | A Baltimore parade celebrates the Fifteenth Amendment, which bars racial discrimination in voting; the same year Congress passes the first Enforcement Act against intimidation at the polls. — [Reconstruction era](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/reconstruction-era.md) |
| 1870-05-19 | Black Baltimoreans hold a parade celebrating ratification. — [Fifteenth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/fifteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1870-09-06 | Louisa Swain of Laramie casts the first ballot by a woman under the new law. — [Wyoming](../articles/states-and-territories/wyoming.md) |
| 1870-12 | Joseph Hayne Rainey of South Carolina, born into slavery, takes his seat as the first Black member of the House. — [United States House of Representatives](../articles/constitution-and-government/house-of-representatives.md) |
| 1871 | Congress ends treaty-making with Indian tribes. — [Native Americans](../articles/native-nations/native-americans.md) |
| 1871 | Congress forbids further treaty-making with tribes; more than 370 ratified treaties remain law. — [Tribal sovereignty](../articles/native-nations/tribal-sovereignty.md) |
| 1871 | Ferdinand Hayden's government survey, with painter Thomas Moran and photographer William Henry Jackson, documents the region. — [Yellowstone National Park](../articles/geography-and-environment/yellowstone-national-park.md) |
| 1871 | The Ku Klux Klan Act lets federal courts try crimes against freedpeople and allows suspension of habeas corpus. — [Reconstruction era](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/reconstruction-era.md) |
| 1871-03-03 | A sundry civil act provides $40,000 to continue Hayden's survey of the territories. — [Hayden Geological Survey of 1871](../articles/geography-and-environment/hayden-geological-survey-of-1871.md) |
| 1871-06 – 1871-10-02 | **[Hayden Geological Survey of 1871](../articles/geography-and-environment/hayden-geological-survey-of-1871.md)** (Event) |
| 1871-06 | The party of more than 30 men sets out in early June; the USGS dates its departure from Ogden, Utah, to June 8. — [Hayden Geological Survey of 1871](../articles/geography-and-environment/hayden-geological-survey-of-1871.md) |
| 1871-07-10 | The survey reaches Fort Ellis near present-day Bozeman, Montana. — [Hayden Geological Survey of 1871](../articles/geography-and-environment/hayden-geological-survey-of-1871.md) |
| 1871-07-21 | It enters the Yellowstone region, reaching Mammoth Hot Springs and then Tower Fall. — [Hayden Geological Survey of 1871](../articles/geography-and-environment/hayden-geological-survey-of-1871.md) |
| 1871-07-28 | The party arrives at Yellowstone Lake, where members build the boat Annie to take depth soundings. — [Hayden Geological Survey of 1871](../articles/geography-and-environment/hayden-geological-survey-of-1871.md) |
| 1871-08-06 | Exploration of the Lower, Midway and Upper Geyser Basins continues through this date. — [Hayden Geological Survey of 1871](../articles/geography-and-environment/hayden-geological-survey-of-1871.md) |
| 1871-08-20 | Several earthquakes shake the camp at Steamboat Point on Yellowstone Lake. — [Hayden Geological Survey of 1871](../articles/geography-and-environment/hayden-geological-survey-of-1871.md) |
| 1871-09-29 | The survey reaches Fort Bridger in southwestern Wyoming Territory. — [Hayden Geological Survey of 1871](../articles/geography-and-environment/hayden-geological-survey-of-1871.md) |
| 1871-10-02 | Hayden formally ends the expedition. — [Hayden Geological Survey of 1871](../articles/geography-and-environment/hayden-geological-survey-of-1871.md) |
| 1871-10-08 | The Great Fire begins in the O'Leary barn on DeKoven Street and burns about 3.5 square miles of the city. — [Chicago](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/chicago.md) |
| 1871-12-18 | Park bills S. 392 and H.R. 764 are introduced in the Senate and House. — [Yellowstone National Park](../articles/geography-and-environment/yellowstone-national-park.md) |
| 1871-12-18 | Senator Pomeroy introduces S. 392 to set the Yellowstone region apart as a park. — [Hayden Geological Survey of 1871](../articles/geography-and-environment/hayden-geological-survey-of-1871.md) |
| 1872 | Susan B. Anthony is arrested for voting in Rochester, New York, and Sojourner Truth is turned away from the polls in Battle Creek, Michigan. — [Women's suffrage movement](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/womens-suffrage-movement.md) |
| 1872 | William Still publishes The Underground Railroad, based on his Philadelphia vigilance committee records. — [Underground Railroad](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/underground-railroad.md) |
| 1872 | Yellowstone is established, beginning the reservation of western lands as parks. — [The West](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/the-west.md) |
| 1872 | Yellowstone, mostly in Wyoming Territory, is set aside as the first national park. — [Wyoming](../articles/states-and-territories/wyoming.md) |
| 1872-01-30 | The Senate passes S. 392. — [Yellowstone National Park](../articles/geography-and-environment/yellowstone-national-park.md) |
| 1872-02-20 | Hayden dates his report on the season's work to Interior Secretary Delano. — [Hayden Geological Survey of 1871](../articles/geography-and-environment/hayden-geological-survey-of-1871.md) |
| 1872-02-27 | The House passes S. 392 by a vote of 115 to 65. — [Yellowstone National Park](../articles/geography-and-environment/yellowstone-national-park.md) |
| 1872-03-01 | **[Yellowstone National Park](../articles/geography-and-environment/yellowstone-national-park.md)** (Landmark) |
| 1872-03-01 | President Ulysses S. Grant signs the Yellowstone National Park Protection Act. — [Yellowstone National Park](../articles/geography-and-environment/yellowstone-national-park.md) |
| 1872-03-01 | The Yellowstone park bill becomes law, about ten weeks after its introduction. — [Hayden Geological Survey of 1871](../articles/geography-and-environment/hayden-geological-survey-of-1871.md) |
| 1872-06 | The bureau is discontinued. — [Freedmen's Bureau](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/freedmens-bureau.md) |
| 1872-11-01 | Registers to vote in Rochester's Eighth Ward with her three sisters and other women, citing the Fourteenth Amendment. — [Susan B. Anthony](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/susan-b-anthony.md) |
| 1872-11-05 | Votes in the presidential and congressional election. — [Susan B. Anthony](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/susan-b-anthony.md) |
| 1872-11-18 | Arrested at her Rochester home for voting illegally under the Enforcement Act of 1870. — [Susan B. Anthony](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/susan-b-anthony.md) |
| 1873 | Harper's Weekly and other national journals repeat the story as fact. — [Betsy Ross flag myth](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/betsy-ross-flag-myth.md) |
| 1873 | Mark Twain and Charles Dudley Warner publish The Gilded Age: A Tale of Today, a satire of postwar corruption that gives the era its name. — [Gilded Age](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/gilded-age.md) |
| 1873 | The Slaughter-House Cases narrow the Privileges or Immunities Clause. — [Fourteenth Amendment](../articles/rights-and-law/fourteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1873 | The Slaughter-House Cases recognize formerly enslaved African Americans as the clause's primary intended beneficiaries while noting its words reach everyone. — [Equal protection](../articles/rights-and-law/equal-protection.md) |
| 1873 | The railroads' financial bubble bursts, beginning years of depression, layoffs and repeated wage cuts. — [Great Railroad Strike of 1877](../articles/economy-and-work/great-railroad-strike-of-1877.md) |
| 1873 | White paramilitaries kill as many as 150 Black men at Colfax, Louisiana; in September the Panic of 1873 opens a long depression. — [Reconstruction era](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/reconstruction-era.md) |
| 1873-01-21 | A federal judge in Albany refuses to discharge her on a writ of habeas corpus. — [Susan B. Anthony](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/susan-b-anthony.md) |
| 1873-01-24 | A federal grand jury indicts her. — [Susan B. Anthony](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/susan-b-anthony.md) |
| 1873-05 | A stock market crash in Vienna, Austria, the first of two that year, leads European investors to start selling American securities, especially railroad bonds. — [Panic of 1873](../articles/economy-and-work/panic-of-1873.md) |
| 1873-06-18 | Justice Ward Hunt directs a guilty verdict in Canandaigua and fines her $100, which she refuses to pay. — [Susan B. Anthony](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/susan-b-anthony.md) |
| 1873-09-18 – 1879 | **[Panic of 1873](../articles/economy-and-work/panic-of-1873.md)** (Event) |
| 1873-09-18 | Jay Cooke and Company, heavily invested in the Northern Pacific Railway, suspends payments and goes bankrupt. — [Panic of 1873](../articles/economy-and-work/panic-of-1873.md) |
| 1873-09-20 | The New York Stock Exchange closes for the first time in its history and stays shut for ten days. — [Panic of 1873](../articles/economy-and-work/panic-of-1873.md) |
| 1873-09-24 | The New York Clearing House suspends cash payments in New York. — [Panic of 1873](../articles/economy-and-work/panic-of-1873.md) |
| 1873-10 | The acute panic subsides by mid-October, but a long depression follows. — [Panic of 1873](../articles/economy-and-work/panic-of-1873.md) |
| 1874-03 | Frederick Douglass is elected president of the struggling Freedman's Savings and Trust Company; Congress passes a bill to issue more greenback currency, which President Grant vetoes. — [Panic of 1873](../articles/economy-and-work/panic-of-1873.md) |
| 1874-06-29 | The Freedman's Savings and Trust Company closes, days after Congress puts its assets under a three-member board. — [Panic of 1873](../articles/economy-and-work/panic-of-1873.md) |
| 1874-08-10 – 1964-10-20 | **[Herbert Hoover](../articles/presidents-and-founders/herbert-hoover.md)** (Person) |
| 1874-08-10 | Born in a two-room cottage in West Branch, Iowa, to a Quaker family. — [Herbert Hoover](../articles/presidents-and-founders/herbert-hoover.md) |
| 1875 | Congress passes a Civil Rights Act requiring equal treatment in public places and on juries. — [Reconstruction era](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/reconstruction-era.md) |
| 1875 | Tennessee's Chapter 130 permits discrimination in hotels, theaters, trains and streetcars. — [Jim Crow laws](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/jim-crow-laws.md) |
| 1875 | The Page Act bars contract laborers, prostitutes and felons from China and Japan; its enforcement keeps most Chinese women out. — [Chinese Exclusion Act](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/chinese-exclusion-act.md) |
| 1875 | The mounds of St. Louis, once called "Mound City," are almost entirely destroyed; only Sugarloaf Mound survives. — [Cahokia](../articles/native-nations/cahokia.md) |
| 1875-01 | The Specie Resumption Act commits the Treasury to retire greenbacks and return to gold-backed currency within four years. — [Panic of 1873](../articles/economy-and-work/panic-of-1873.md) |
| 1875-01-30 | The United States and the Kingdom of Hawaiʻi sign a Treaty of Reciprocity. — [Hawaii](../articles/states-and-territories/hawaii.md) |
| 1875-03-29 | Minor v. Happersett holds that citizenship does not carry a right to vote. — [Fourteenth Amendment](../articles/rights-and-law/fourteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1876 | Alexander Graham Bell patents the telephone; Western Union declines to buy the patent. — [Electric telegraph](../articles/science-and-innovation/telegraph.md) |
| 1876 | United States v. Reese and United States v. Cruikshank narrow federal enforcement. — [Fifteenth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/fifteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1876-05-10 | The Centennial Exhibition, the first major U.S. world's fair, opens in Fairmount Park. — [Philadelphia](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/philadelphia.md) |
| 1876-06 | George Armstrong Custer and 268 of his men are killed at the Little Bighorn. — [The West](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/the-west.md) |
| 1876-07-04 | Reads a Declaration of Rights of Women outside Independence Hall during the Centennial. — [Susan B. Anthony](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/susan-b-anthony.md) |
| 1877 – 1968 | **[Jim Crow laws](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/jim-crow-laws.md)** (Policy) |
| 1877 | An 8–7 electoral commission vote and the Compromise of 1877 seat Rutherford B. Hayes; federal troops are withdrawn from the South in April. — [Reconstruction era](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/reconstruction-era.md) |
| 1877 | Appointed U.S. marshal for the District of Columbia by President Hayes. — [Frederick Douglass](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/frederick-douglass.md) |
| 1877 | The Great Railroad Strike spreads from Baltimore to St. Louis; about 100 people die before federal troops end it. — [Gilded Age](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/gilded-age.md) |
| 1877 | The last Union occupation troops, including U.S. Colored Troops, are withdrawn from Vicksburg. — [Siege of Vicksburg](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/siege-of-vicksburg.md) |
| 1877-07 | Railroad wage cuts during the depression set off the Great Railroad Strike. — [Panic of 1873](../articles/economy-and-work/panic-of-1873.md) |
| 1877-07-16 – 1877-08 | **[Great Railroad Strike of 1877](../articles/economy-and-work/great-railroad-strike-of-1877.md)** (Event) |
| 1877-07-16 | Railroad workers at Martinsburg, West Virginia, walk off the job over a ten percent wage cut on the Baltimore and Ohio Railroad and stop trains. — [Great Railroad Strike of 1877](../articles/economy-and-work/great-railroad-strike-of-1877.md) |
| 1877-07-18 | Governor Henry M. Mathews asks for federal help, and President Rutherford B. Hayes proclaims that those obstructing the railroad must disperse by noon the next day. — [Great Railroad Strike of 1877](../articles/economy-and-work/great-railroad-strike-of-1877.md) |
| 1877-07-19 | Federal troops arrive at Martinsburg and end the violence there. — [Great Railroad Strike of 1877](../articles/economy-and-work/great-railroad-strike-of-1877.md) |
| 1877-07-21 | Hayes issues a similar proclamation for Maryland; in Pittsburgh, militia from Philadelphia fire into a crowd near the 28th Street crossing, killing about twenty people. — [Great Railroad Strike of 1877](../articles/economy-and-work/great-railroad-strike-of-1877.md) |
| 1877-07-22 | Fires in Pittsburgh destroy the Pennsylvania Railroad's Union Depot, more than thirty buildings and thousands of locomotives and cars. — [Great Railroad Strike of 1877](../articles/economy-and-work/great-railroad-strike-of-1877.md) |
| 1877-07-24 | Unrest in Chicago peaks over five days of walkouts, sympathy strikes and clashes with police, militia and U.S. Army troops. — [Great Railroad Strike of 1877](../articles/economy-and-work/great-railroad-strike-of-1877.md) |
| 1877-07-31 | By the end of July the strikes have largely been put down along the trunk lines. — [Great Railroad Strike of 1877](../articles/economy-and-work/great-railroad-strike-of-1877.md) |
| 1877-08-23 | About 800 Nez Perce (Nimiipuu) people fleeing the U.S. Army enter the park and cross it in 13 days. — [Yellowstone National Park](../articles/geography-and-environment/yellowstone-national-park.md) |
| 1878 | J. Franklin Reigart's book embellishes the tale, crediting Ross with originating the first flag. — [Betsy Ross flag myth](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/betsy-ross-flag-myth.md) |
| 1878-01-10 | Senator Aaron Sargent introduces the woman suffrage amendment that suffragists later call the Anthony Amendment. — [Susan B. Anthony](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/susan-b-anthony.md) |
| 1878-01-10 | Senator Aaron Sargent of California introduces the woman suffrage amendment as S.Res. 12. — [Nineteenth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/nineteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1878-01-10 | Senator Aaron Sargent of California introduces the woman suffrage amendment in the Senate. — [Women's suffrage movement](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/womens-suffrage-movement.md) |
| 1878-03-18 | Northern Arapaho led by Black Coal arrive under military escort on the Shoshone reservation at Wind River. — [Wyoming](../articles/states-and-territories/wyoming.md) |
| 1879 | Henry George publishes Progress and Poverty, a bestselling critique of inequality. — [Gilded Age](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/gilded-age.md) |
| 1879 | The depression begins to lift early in the year. — [Panic of 1873](../articles/economy-and-work/panic-of-1873.md) |
| 1880 | By early 1880 the railroads have restored the wages they had cut. — [Great Railroad Strike of 1877](../articles/economy-and-work/great-railroad-strike-of-1877.md) |
| 1880 | Ferdinand de Lesseps's French company begins excavating a canal in Panama, then part of Colombia. — [Panama Canal](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/panama-canal.md) |
| 1880 | The Angell Treaty allows the United States to regulate or limit, but not wholly prohibit, Chinese immigration. — [Chinese Exclusion Act](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/chinese-exclusion-act.md) |
| 1880 | The bay's oyster harvest peaks at about 6.3 million bushels of market oysters. — [Chesapeake Bay](../articles/geography-and-environment/chesapeake-bay.md) |
| 1880-06 | Graduates from Harvard College. — [Theodore Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/theodore-roosevelt.md) |
| 1880-10-18 | After Auk Tlingit chief Kowee brings in gold ore, prospectors stake the Silver Bow Basin townsite that becomes Juneau. — [Alaska](../articles/states-and-territories/alaska.md) |
| 1881 | Tennessee orders segregated railroad cars, a law often called the first Jim Crow law. — [Jim Crow laws](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/jim-crow-laws.md) |
| 1881 | The first volume of the History of Woman Suffrage, by Stanton, Anthony and Matilda Joslyn Gage, is published. — [Susan B. Anthony](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/susan-b-anthony.md) |
| 1882 | Congress passes the Chinese Exclusion Act, barring Chinese laborers and making Chinese immigrants ineligible for citizenship. — [Angel Island Immigration Station](../articles/society-and-people/angel-island-immigration-station.md) |
| 1882 | The Chinese Exclusion Act suspends the immigration of Chinese laborers and bars Chinese immigrants from naturalization. — [Chinese Americans](../articles/society-and-people/chinese-americans.md) |
| 1882 | Wins a seat in the New York State Assembly. — [Theodore Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/theodore-roosevelt.md) |
| 1882-01-30 – 1945-04-12 | **[Franklin D. Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/franklin-d-roosevelt.md)** (Person) |
| 1882-01-30 | Born in Hyde Park, New York. — [Franklin D. Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/franklin-d-roosevelt.md) |
| 1882-04-04 | President Chester A. Arthur vetoes a bill that would have suspended Chinese labor immigration for twenty years. — [Chinese Exclusion Act](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/chinese-exclusion-act.md) |
| 1882-05-06 – 1943 | **[Chinese Exclusion Act](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/chinese-exclusion-act.md)** (Law) |
| 1882-05-06 | Arthur approves a revised act suspending Chinese labor immigration for ten years and barring Chinese naturalization. — [Chinese Exclusion Act](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/chinese-exclusion-act.md) |
| 1882-05-06 | President Chester A. Arthur signs the Chinese Exclusion Act, barring Chinese laborers for ten years. — [Gilded Age](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/gilded-age.md) |
| 1882-09-05 | New York's Central Labor Union holds the first Labor Day parade. — [Labor movement](../articles/economy-and-work/labor-movement.md) |
| 1883 | Buffalo Bill Cody's Wild West show begins touring, spreading a romantic image of the West. — [The West](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/the-west.md) |
| 1883 | Congress passes the Pendleton Civil Service Act, two years after President James Garfield's assassination. — [Gilded Age](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/gilded-age.md) |
| 1883 | In the Civil Rights Cases the Supreme Court strikes down key parts of the Civil Rights Act of 1875. — [Jim Crow laws](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/jim-crow-laws.md) |
| 1883-09 | First travels to the Dakota Badlands, where he later ranches. — [Theodore Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/theodore-roosevelt.md) |
| 1883-11-26 | Dies at her home in Battle Creek, Michigan, and is buried in Oak Hill Cemetery. — [Sojourner Truth](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/sojourner-truth.md) |
| 1884 | Graduates from Great Barrington's high school, its first African American graduate. — [W. E. B. Du Bois](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/w-e-b-du-bois.md) |
| 1884 | His mother and his first wife, Alice, die in the same house on the same day. — [Theodore Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/theodore-roosevelt.md) |
| 1884-11-03 | Elk v. Wilkins holds that Native Americans who had left their nations were still not U.S. citizens. — [Fourteenth Amendment](../articles/rights-and-law/fourteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1885 | A Tiffany & Co. die corrects the errors of the 1841 and 1877 dies. — [Great Seal of the United States](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/great-seal-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1885 | Enrolls at Fisk University in Nashville, Tennessee. — [W. E. B. Du Bois](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/w-e-b-du-bois.md) |
| 1885 | The bell travels to the World's Industrial and Cotton Centennial Exposition in New Orleans, the first of seven trips. — [Liberty Bell](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/liberty-bell.md) |
| 1885-08-10 | James Marshall dies at 75 after decades of setbacks, having once been stranded penniless. — [California Gold Rush](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/california-gold-rush.md) |
| 1885-09-02 | White miners in Rock Springs kill 28 Chinese coal miners and wound 15. — [Wyoming](../articles/states-and-territories/wyoming.md) |
| 1885-09-02 | White miners kill 28 Chinese miners and burn the Chinatown of Rock Springs, Wyoming Territory. — [Chinese Americans](../articles/society-and-people/chinese-americans.md) |
| 1885-09-02 | White miners who had met at a Knights of Labor hall kill 28 Chinese miners at Rock Springs, Wyoming Territory. — [Labor movement](../articles/economy-and-work/labor-movement.md) |
| 1885-11 | Orphaned, he moves at 11 to live with his uncle Henry Minthorn in Oregon. — [Herbert Hoover](../articles/presidents-and-founders/herbert-hoover.md) |
| 1886 | United States v. Kagama upholds federal criminal laws on reservations as a power to protect dependent peoples. — [Tribal sovereignty](../articles/native-nations/tribal-sovereignty.md) |
| 1886 | Yick Wo v. Hopkins applies equal protection beyond race to discriminatory enforcement of an ordinance. — [Equal protection](../articles/rights-and-law/equal-protection.md) |
| 1886-05 | A bomb at a labor rally in Chicago's Haymarket Square kills seven policemen; four anarchists are later hanged. — [Gilded Age](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/gilded-age.md) |
| 1886-05-01 | Workers strike nationwide for the eight-hour day, including more than 30,000 in Chicago. — [Labor movement](../articles/economy-and-work/labor-movement.md) |
| 1886-05-04 | A bomb is thrown at police at a Haymarket Square protest in Chicago, killing an officer and setting off a backlash against labor. — [Labor movement](../articles/economy-and-work/labor-movement.md) |
| 1886-08-20 | The U.S. Army takes charge of the park after Congress declines to fund its civilian administration. — [Yellowstone National Park](../articles/geography-and-environment/yellowstone-national-park.md) |
| 1886-12 | Samuel Gompers and other craft-union leaders form the American Federation of Labor. — [Labor movement](../articles/economy-and-work/labor-movement.md) |
| 1887 | The General Allotment (Dawes) Act breaks up tribal lands into individual allotments and opens land west of the Mississippi to non-Indian settlers. — [Native Americans](../articles/native-nations/native-americans.md) |
| 1887 | The Hatch Act funds agricultural experiment stations at the land-grant colleges. — [Morrill Land-Grant College Act](../articles/science-and-innovation/morrill-land-grant-college-act.md) |
| 1887 | White landowners and businessmen force King Kalākaua to sign a constitution that strips him of power. — [Hawaii](../articles/states-and-territories/hawaii.md) |
| 1887-01-25 | The Senate holds its first vote on the amendment and defeats it, 16 to 34. — [Nineteenth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/nineteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1887-01-25 | The Senate takes its first vote on the amendment and defeats it, 16 to 34. — [Women's suffrage movement](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/womens-suffrage-movement.md) |
| 1887-02-08 | **[Dawes Act](../articles/native-nations/dawes-act.md)** (Law) |
| 1887-02-08 | Congress approves the Dawes Act, dividing reservation land held in common into individual allotments. — [Gilded Age](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/gilded-age.md) |
| 1887-02-08 | President Grover Cleveland signs the General Allotment Act, drafted by Senator Henry L. Dawes of Massachusetts. — [Dawes Act](../articles/native-nations/dawes-act.md) |
| 1887-02-08 | The Dawes General Allotment Act divides reservation land into individual allotments and opens the 'surplus' to settlers. — [The West](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/the-west.md) |
| 1888-10-01 | The Scott Act voids the return certificates of Chinese laborers abroad, stranding about 20,000 people. — [Chinese Exclusion Act](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/chinese-exclusion-act.md) |
| 1889 | Appointed U.S. minister resident and consul general to Haiti, serving until 1891. — [Frederick Douglass](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/frederick-douglass.md) |
| 1889 | Appointed to the U.S. Civil Service Commission by President Benjamin Harrison. — [Theodore Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/theodore-roosevelt.md) |
| 1889 | Jane Addams and Ellen Gates Starr open Hull House, a settlement house in Chicago. — [Progressive Era](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/progressive-era.md) |
| 1889 | The U.S. Navy adopts the song for flag ceremonies. — [The Star-Spangled Banner](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/star-spangled-banner.md) |
| 1889-05-13 | In Chae Chan Ping v. United States the Supreme Court upholds the Scott Act under Congress's plenary power over immigration. — [Chinese Exclusion Act](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/chinese-exclusion-act.md) |
| 1889-11-05 | Voters ratify the state constitution, which keeps equal suffrage for women. — [Wyoming](../articles/states-and-territories/wyoming.md) |
| 1890 – 1920 | **[Progressive Era](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/progressive-era.md)** (Era) |
| 1890 | A second Morrill Act adds annual appropriations and leads to the founding of separate land-grant institutions for Black students in the segregated South. — [Morrill Land-Grant College Act](../articles/science-and-innovation/morrill-land-grant-college-act.md) |
| 1890 | Earns a BA cum laude from Harvard, followed by an MA in 1891. — [W. E. B. Du Bois](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/w-e-b-du-bois.md) |
| 1890 | Jacob Riis publishes How the Other Half Lives, exposing conditions in New York City tenements. — [Progressive Era](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/progressive-era.md) |
| 1890 | Jacob Riis publishes How the Other Half Lives, using photographs to expose New York City tenement conditions. — [Muckrakers](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/muckrakers.md) |
| 1890 | Louisiana passes the Separate Car Act, one of nine state laws of 1887–1892 separating passengers by race. — [Jim Crow laws](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/jim-crow-laws.md) |
| 1890 | Louisiana's legislature passes the Separate Car Act (Act No. 111), requiring separate railroad cars for Black and white passengers. — [Plessy v. Ferguson](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/plessy-v-ferguson.md) |
| 1890 | New York State takes the Onondaga Nation's wampum belts. — [Haudenosaunee](../articles/native-nations/haudenosaunee.md) |
| 1890 | The census counts 1,099,850 residents, making Chicago the second-largest U.S. city. — [Chicago](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/chicago.md) |
| 1890 | The rival associations merge as the National American Woman Suffrage Association; Wyoming enters the Union with women's suffrage. — [Women's suffrage movement](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/womens-suffrage-movement.md) |
| 1890 | The rival suffrage associations merge as the National American Woman Suffrage Association. — [Susan B. Anthony](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/susan-b-anthony.md) |
| 1890-07-10 | **[Wyoming](../articles/states-and-territories/wyoming.md)** (State) |
| 1890-07-10 | President Benjamin Harrison signs the statehood bill; Wyoming becomes the 44th state. — [Wyoming](../articles/states-and-territories/wyoming.md) |
| 1890-12-29 | U.S. troops kill between 150 and 300 Lakota men, women and children at Wounded Knee. — [The West](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/the-west.md) |
| 1891 | Eighteen New Orleans residents form the Comité des Citoyens to challenge the law in court. — [Plessy v. Ferguson](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/plessy-v-ferguson.md) |
| 1891-09-05 | Creole of color activists announce the Comité des Citoyens to challenge Louisiana's Separate Car Law. — [New Orleans](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/new-orleans.md) |
| 1892 | After the lynching of her friend Thomas Moss in Memphis, Ida B. Wells investigates lynchings across the South and publishes Southern Horrors; a mob destroys her newspaper office. — [Muckrakers](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/muckrakers.md) |
| 1892 | Becomes president of NAWSA, serving until Carrie Chapman Catt succeeds her in 1900. — [Susan B. Anthony](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/susan-b-anthony.md) |
| 1892 | Begins two years of study at the University of Berlin. — [W. E. B. Du Bois](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/w-e-b-du-bois.md) |
| 1892 | The Geary Act extends exclusion for ten more years and requires Chinese residents to carry certificates of residence. — [Chinese Exclusion Act](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/chinese-exclusion-act.md) |
| 1892 | The Homestead Strike ends in defeat for the steelworkers' union. — [Pennsylvania](../articles/states-and-territories/pennsylvania.md) |
| 1892 | The Homestead strike is broken at Carnegie's Pennsylvania steel works; the People's Party adopts its Omaha Platform; Ida B. Wells publishes Southern Horrors. — [Gilded Age](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/gilded-age.md) |
| 1892-02-24 | Daniel Desdunes boards a whites-only car on an interstate train in a first test case. — [Plessy v. Ferguson](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/plessy-v-ferguson.md) |
| 1892-05-25 | In Abbott v. Hicks the Louisiana Supreme Court rules that the act does not apply to interstate passengers. — [Plessy v. Ferguson](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/plessy-v-ferguson.md) |
| 1892-06-07 | Homer Plessy is arrested after sitting in the whites-only coach of an East Louisiana Railroad train to Covington. — [Plessy v. Ferguson](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/plessy-v-ferguson.md) |
| 1892-07-06 | A gunfight between strikers and Pinkerton guards at Carnegie Steel's Homestead works leaves three Pinkertons and six workers dead. — [Labor movement](../articles/economy-and-work/labor-movement.md) |
| 1892-11-18 | Judge John Howard Ferguson of the Orleans Parish Criminal Court rules against Plessy. — [Plessy v. Ferguson](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/plessy-v-ferguson.md) |
| 1892-12 | The Louisiana Supreme Court upholds Ferguson's ruling. — [Plessy v. Ferguson](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/plessy-v-ferguson.md) |
| 1893 | Charles Weisgerber's painting of the scene draws crowds at the World's Columbian Exposition and is widely reproduced. — [Betsy Ross flag myth](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/betsy-ross-flag-myth.md) |
| 1893 | Chicago hosts the World's Columbian Exposition. — [Chicago](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/chicago.md) |
| 1893 | Colorado becomes the first state to adopt a state amendment enfranchising women. — [Women's suffrage movement](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/womens-suffrage-movement.md) |
| 1893 | Frederick Jackson Turner presents his frontier thesis to the American Historical Association in Chicago. — [The West](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/the-west.md) |
| 1893 | S. S. McClure and John S. Phillips launch McClure's Magazine at fifteen cents a copy. — [Muckrakers](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/muckrakers.md) |
| 1893-01-17 | A Committee of Safety, backed by U.S. Minister John L. Stevens and U.S. naval forces landed the day before, overthrows Queen Liliʻuokalani. — [Hawaii](../articles/states-and-territories/hawaii.md) |
| 1893-05-09 | Edison's Kinetoscope, a peephole motion-picture viewer developed largely by W. K. L. Dickson, is first shown publicly at the Brooklyn Institute of Arts and Sciences. — [Hollywood](../articles/culture-and-arts/hollywood.md) |
| 1893-11-01 | Dawes, now retired, is appointed to head a commission to negotiate the end of common landholding with the Five Tribes, whom the 1887 act had exempted. — [Dawes Act](../articles/native-nations/dawes-act.md) |
| 1894 | After soldiers arrest the bison poacher Ed Howell, Congress passes the National Park Protection Act (Lacey Act) banning hunting in the park. — [Yellowstone National Park](../articles/geography-and-environment/yellowstone-national-park.md) |
| 1894 | Cyrus Thomas's Bureau of Ethnology study concludes that American Indians built the eastern mounds, refuting the "lost race" theory. — [Cahokia](../articles/native-nations/cahokia.md) |
| 1894 | Federal troops break the Pullman sympathy strike led by the American Railway Union, and Eugene Debs is jailed. — [Gilded Age](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/gilded-age.md) |
| 1894-05-11 | Pullman factory workers strike; the American Railway Union's boycott of Pullman cars follows. — [Chicago](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/chicago.md) |
| 1894-05-11 | The Pullman strike begins; the American Railway Union boycott spreads nationwide until federal troops break it in July. — [Labor movement](../articles/economy-and-work/labor-movement.md) |
| 1894-06-28 | Congress makes the first Monday in September a national Labor Day holiday. — [Labor movement](../articles/economy-and-work/labor-movement.md) |
| 1895 | Around this time uptown cornetist Charles "Buddy" Bolden begins improvising on the blues and speeding up dance tunes, a style many early musicians later called the first jazz. — [Jazz](../articles/culture-and-arts/jazz.md) |
| 1895 | Cornetist Buddy Bolden forms his own band, an early landmark of New Orleans jazz. — [New Orleans](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/new-orleans.md) |
| 1895 | Graduates in geology from the new Stanford University, where he met Lou Henry. — [Herbert Hoover](../articles/presidents-and-founders/herbert-hoover.md) |
| 1895 | In United States v. E. C. Knight the Supreme Court holds that the national government cannot regulate manufacturing, a hallmark of dual federalism. — [Federalism](../articles/constitution-and-government/federalism.md) |
| 1895 | Receives a Harvard PhD; his dissertation on the suppression of the African slave trade is published in 1896. — [W. E. B. Du Bois](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/w-e-b-du-bois.md) |
| 1895 | Resigns from the Civil Service Commission and becomes a New York City police commissioner. — [Theodore Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/theodore-roosevelt.md) |
| 1895 | Southern states begin adopting grandfather clauses that exempt mostly white voters from literacy tests. — [Fifteenth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/fifteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1895-02-20 | Dies at Cedar Hill, his Washington home, after attending a meeting of the National Council of Women. — [Frederick Douglass](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/frederick-douglass.md) |
| 1895-08 | U.S.-born Wong Kim Ark is refused reentry at San Francisco, starting the case that would confirm his citizenship. — [Chinese Americans](../articles/society-and-people/chinese-americans.md) |
| 1896 | Black clubwomen including Mary Church Terrell, Ida B. Wells-Barnett and Harriet Tubman form the National Association of Colored Women. — [Women's suffrage movement](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/womens-suffrage-movement.md) |
| 1896 | Plessy v. Ferguson upholds state-mandated segregation under the separate but equal doctrine. — [Jim Crow laws](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/jim-crow-laws.md) |
| 1896 | William McKinley, backed by business and the gold standard, defeats William Jennings Bryan for the presidency. — [Gilded Age](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/gilded-age.md) |
| 1896-05-18 | **[Plessy v. Ferguson](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/plessy-v-ferguson.md)** (CourtCase) |
| 1896-05-18 | The U.S. Supreme Court affirms, 7 to 1, with Justice Henry Billings Brown writing and Justice John Marshall Harlan dissenting. — [Plessy v. Ferguson](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/plessy-v-ferguson.md) |
| 1896-08 | Skookum Jim and his family find gold in the Klondike region of Canada's Yukon; stampeders pour through Skagway and Dyea in 1897–98. — [Alaska](../articles/states-and-territories/alaska.md) |
| 1897-01-11 | Plessy pleads guilty in New Orleans and pays a $25 fine. — [Plessy v. Ferguson](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/plessy-v-ferguson.md) |
| 1897-08-21 | Pennsylvania enacts the Campbell Act, a tax of three cents a day on coal operators for each non-citizen they employ. — [Lattimer massacre](../articles/economy-and-work/lattimer-massacre.md) |
| 1897-09-10 | **[Lattimer massacre](../articles/economy-and-work/lattimer-massacre.md)** (Event) |
| 1897-09-10 | Sheriff James Martin's deputies fire on about 400 marching strikers near Lattimer, killing nineteen and wounding dozens. — [Lattimer massacre](../articles/economy-and-work/lattimer-massacre.md) |
| 1897-09-11 | Members of Hui Aloha ʻĀina sign petitions against annexation; the drive gathers 21,269 Native Hawaiian signatures. — [Hawaii](../articles/states-and-territories/hawaii.md) |
| 1897-12 | Begins teaching at Atlanta University and directing empirical studies of Black life in the South. — [W. E. B. Du Bois](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/w-e-b-du-bois.md) |
| 1898 | In United States v. Wong Kim Ark the Supreme Court rules 6–2 that he is a citizen by birth under the Fourteenth Amendment. — [Chinese Americans](../articles/society-and-people/chinese-americans.md) |
| 1898 | Louisiana's new constitution mandates segregated schools and restricts Black men's voting. — [Jim Crow laws](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/jim-crow-laws.md) |
| 1898 | United States v. Wong Kim Ark applies birthright citizenship to a U.S.-born son of Chinese parents. — [Fourteenth Amendment](../articles/rights-and-law/fourteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1898 | Wilbur H. Siebert publishes The Underground Railroad from Slavery to Freedom, long the standard account. — [Underground Railroad](../articles/civil-war-and-reconstruction/underground-railroad.md) |
| 1898-02-01 | Martin and his deputies go on trial for murder at the Luzerne County Courthouse in Wilkes-Barre. — [Lattimer massacre](../articles/economy-and-work/lattimer-massacre.md) |
| 1898-03-02 | The jury acquits all the defendants. — [Lattimer massacre](../articles/economy-and-work/lattimer-massacre.md) |
| 1898-06-28 | The Curtis Act ratifies the Choctaw-Chickasaw and Creek allotment agreements and gives the Dawes Commission power over tribal enrollment. — [Dawes Act](../articles/native-nations/dawes-act.md) |
| 1898-07-01 | Leads the Rough Riders in the fighting for the San Juan Heights in Cuba, alongside Black regulars of the 9th and 10th Cavalry. — [Theodore Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/theodore-roosevelt.md) |
| 1898-07-07 | President McKinley signs the Newlands Resolution annexing Hawaiʻi by joint resolution, after an annexation treaty failed in the Senate. — [Hawaii](../articles/states-and-territories/hawaii.md) |
| 1898-08-12 | The United States formally annexes Hawaiʻi. — [Hawaii](../articles/states-and-territories/hawaii.md) |
| 1898-12-19 | Queen Liliuokalani writes to the House objecting to the U.S. claim of ownership over Hawaii. — [United States](../articles/states-and-territories/united-states.md) |
| 1899 | Publishes The Philadelphia Negro, a study of the city's Seventh Ward. — [W. E. B. Du Bois](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/w-e-b-du-bois.md) |
| 1899-01-19 | Congress passes H.R. 4982, raising her pension to $20 a month in recognition of her service as an army nurse. — [Harriet Tubman](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/harriet-tubman.md) |
| 1900 | About 41 percent of the American workforce is employed in agriculture. — [Economic history of the United States](../articles/economy-and-work/economic-history-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1900 | Hawaiʻi becomes a U.S. territory with a single non-voting delegate in the House. — [Hawaii](../articles/states-and-territories/hawaii.md) |
| 1900 | Pennsylvania produces 60 percent of U.S. steel. — [Pennsylvania](../articles/states-and-territories/pennsylvania.md) |
| 1900 | The Gold Standard Act effectively settles the currency debate; the census counts 76,212,168 people. — [Gilded Age](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/gilded-age.md) |
| 1900 | The Sanitary and Ship Canal permanently reverses the flow of the Chicago River away from Lake Michigan. — [Chicago](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/chicago.md) |
| 1900 | The Wrights begin testing gliders on the Outer Banks near Kitty Hawk, whose weather station had answered their inquiry about steady winds and open sand. — [Wright brothers' first flight](../articles/science-and-innovation/wright-brothers-first-flight.md) |
| 1900-10-29 | A UMW-led anthracite strike of about 125,000 workers ends. — [Lattimer massacre](../articles/economy-and-work/lattimer-massacre.md) |
| 1901 | Alabama's constitution disfranchises Black voters, segregates schools and bars interracial marriage. — [Jim Crow laws](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/jim-crow-laws.md) |
| 1901 | Alabama's new constitution requires legislative seats to be reapportioned by population after each federal census; no statewide reapportionment follows the 1900 census. — [Reynolds v. Sims](../articles/democracy-and-elections/reynolds-v-sims.md) |
| 1901 | The Hay-Pauncefote Treaty supersedes Clayton-Bulwer, letting the United States build and run its own canal. — [Panama Canal](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/panama-canal.md) |
| 1901 | The ten-story Asch Building is completed at 23–29 Washington Place in Greenwich Village; the Triangle Waist Company later occupies its eighth, ninth and tenth floors. — [Triangle Shirtwaist Factory fire](../articles/economy-and-work/triangle-shirtwaist-factory-fire.md) |
| 1901 | Theodore Roosevelt becomes president, and Progressive reform reaches the federal government. — [Progressive Era](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/progressive-era.md) |
| 1901-03 | Congress extends the allotment law to the Five Tribes and other nations of Indian Territory. — [Dawes Act](../articles/native-nations/dawes-act.md) |
| 1901-09-14 | Sworn in as president at 42 after the assassination of William McKinley. — [Theodore Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/theodore-roosevelt.md) |
| 1901-10 | Dines with Booker T. Washington at the White House, provoking a storm of criticism from white Southerners. — [Theodore Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/theodore-roosevelt.md) |
| 1902 | Brings coal operators and miners' leaders to the White House and pushes them into arbitration to end the anthracite coal strike. — [Theodore Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/theodore-roosevelt.md) |
| 1902 | British, German and Italian warships blockade Venezuela's ports after it defaults on debts to foreign bondholders. — [Roosevelt Corollary](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/roosevelt-corollary.md) |
| 1902 | Exclusion is made permanent. — [Chinese Exclusion Act](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/chinese-exclusion-act.md) |
| 1902 | Park managers buy 21 bison from private owners to rebuild a wild herd that had fallen to about two dozen animals. — [Yellowstone National Park](../articles/geography-and-environment/yellowstone-national-park.md) |
| 1902-06-19 | The U.S. Senate votes to build the canal through Panama rather than Nicaragua. — [Panama Canal](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/panama-canal.md) |
| 1902-10 | Wilbur glides 622 feet in 21 seconds, setting American distance and time records for gliders. — [Wright brothers' first flight](../articles/science-and-innovation/wright-brothers-first-flight.md) |
| 1902-10-23 | Miners return to work after the five-month anthracite strike of 1902. — [Lattimer massacre](../articles/economy-and-work/lattimer-massacre.md) |
| 1902-11 | **[Muckrakers](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/muckrakers.md)** (Movement) |
| 1902-11 | Ida Tarbell's History of the Standard Oil Company begins appearing in McClure's. — [Muckrakers](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/muckrakers.md) |
| 1903 | Bandleader W. C. Handy settles in Clarksdale, Mississippi, and around this year hears a slide guitarist playing a blues at the Tutwiler railroad station. — [Blues](../articles/culture-and-arts/blues.md) |
| 1903 | In Lone Wolf v. Hitchcock the Supreme Court rejects a Kiowa challenge to allotment, holding that a treaty cannot limit Congress's power over tribal land. — [Dawes Act](../articles/native-nations/dawes-act.md) |
| 1903 | Publishes The Souls of Black Folk. — [W. E. B. Du Bois](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/w-e-b-du-bois.md) |
| 1903-01 | McClure's prints exposés by Tarbell, Lincoln Steffens and Ray Stannard Baker in one issue, with an editorial linking them. — [Muckrakers](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/muckrakers.md) |
| 1903-03-23 | The brothers file their patent application for a "Flying-Machine". — [Wright brothers' first flight](../articles/science-and-innovation/wright-brothers-first-flight.md) |
| 1903-11-03 | Panama declares independence from Colombia while U.S. warships stand off Colón and Panama City. — [Panama Canal](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/panama-canal.md) |
| 1903-11-06 | The United States recognizes the Republic of Panama. — [Panama Canal](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/panama-canal.md) |
| 1903-11-18 | The Hay-Bunau-Varilla Treaty grants the United States a ten-mile-wide Canal Zone in perpetuity. — [Panama Canal](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/panama-canal.md) |
| 1903-12-08 | Samuel Langley's Smithsonian-backed Great Aerodrome crashes on its second launch attempt. — [Wright brothers' first flight](../articles/science-and-innovation/wright-brothers-first-flight.md) |
| 1903-12-14 | Wilbur wins a coin toss for the first try, but the Flyer stalls and noses into the sand. — [Wright brothers' first flight](../articles/science-and-innovation/wright-brothers-first-flight.md) |
| 1903-12-17 | **[Wright brothers' first flight](../articles/science-and-innovation/wright-brothers-first-flight.md)** (Event) |
| 1903-12-17 | Four powered flights from Kill Devil Hills; Orville flies 120 feet in 12 seconds, Wilbur 852 feet in 59 seconds. — [Wright brothers' first flight](../articles/science-and-innovation/wright-brothers-first-flight.md) |
| 1904 | The Supreme Court upholds the government's antitrust suit against the Northern Securities Company; Roosevelt wins a full term in a landslide. — [Theodore Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/theodore-roosevelt.md) |
| 1904 | The Supreme Court upholds, 5–4, the government's breakup of the Northern Securities railroad trust. — [Progressive Era](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/progressive-era.md) |
| 1904 | The Wrights move their experiments to Huffman Prairie near Dayton with a sturdier new machine. — [Wright brothers' first flight](../articles/science-and-innovation/wright-brothers-first-flight.md) |
| 1904 | Theodore Roosevelt's corollary claims an international police power for the United States in the hemisphere. — [Monroe Doctrine](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/monroe-doctrine.md) |
| 1904-05-04 | The United States formally takes over the French works and begins construction. — [Panama Canal](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/panama-canal.md) |
| 1904-05-23 | Before a dozen invited reporters, the Wrights' new Flyer II fails to leave the ground because of engine trouble. — [Huffman Prairie Flying Field](../articles/science-and-innovation/huffman-prairie.md) |
| 1904-09-07 | The brothers first use a weight-and-derrick catapult to launch the Flyer in the prairie's light winds. — [Huffman Prairie Flying Field](../articles/science-and-innovation/huffman-prairie.md) |
| 1904-09-20 | The first complete circle by an airplane is flown over the pasture. — [Huffman Prairie Flying Field](../articles/science-and-innovation/huffman-prairie.md) |
| 1904-12-06 – 1934 | **[Roosevelt Corollary](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/roosevelt-corollary.md)** (Policy) |
| 1904-12-06 | Theodore Roosevelt announces the corollary in his annual message to Congress. — [Roosevelt Corollary](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/roosevelt-corollary.md) |
| 1904-12-09 | The season ends after 105 starts adding up to under an hour in the air. — [Huffman Prairie Flying Field](../articles/science-and-innovation/huffman-prairie.md) |
| 1905 | Lochner v. New York strikes down a workplace regulation on "freedom of contract" grounds. — [Due process](../articles/rights-and-law/due-process.md) |
| 1905 | The War Department transfers 20 acres of Angel Island for an immigration station. — [Angel Island Immigration Station](../articles/society-and-people/angel-island-immigration-station.md) |
| 1905 | Twenty-nine activists led by Du Bois and William Monroe Trotter meet at Fort Erie, Ontario, founding the Niagara Movement. — [W. E. B. Du Bois](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/w-e-b-du-bois.md) |
| 1905 | W. E. B. Du Bois and other Black leaders found the Niagara Movement on the Canadian side of Niagara Falls. — [Progressive Era](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/progressive-era.md) |
| 1905-01-20 | A U.S.-Dominican executive agreement gives the United States control of Dominican customhouses; critics say it bypasses the Senate. — [Roosevelt Corollary](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/roosevelt-corollary.md) |
| 1905-03-17 | Marries Eleanor Roosevelt. — [Franklin D. Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/franklin-d-roosevelt.md) |
| 1905-04-01 | With Senate action on a treaty blocked, Roosevelt puts a customs receivership into effect by a modus vivendi. — [Roosevelt Corollary](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/roosevelt-corollary.md) |
| 1905-06-23 | Orville makes the first flight of the rebuilt Wright Flyer III. — [Huffman Prairie Flying Field](../articles/science-and-innovation/huffman-prairie.md) |
| 1905-07-14 | A crash that throws Orville through the upper wing prompts a redesign of the controls. — [Huffman Prairie Flying Field](../articles/science-and-innovation/huffman-prairie.md) |
| 1905-10-05 | Wilbur flies about 24 miles in some 39 minutes, a world record. — [Huffman Prairie Flying Field](../articles/science-and-innovation/huffman-prairie.md) |
| 1905-11-11 | The last case of yellow fever in Panama City is reported. — [Panama Canal](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/panama-canal.md) |
| 1906 | Awarded the Nobel Peace Prize for his role in ending the Russo-Japanese War. — [Theodore Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/theodore-roosevelt.md) |
| 1906 | Congress passes the Meat Inspection Act and the Pure Food and Drug Act. — [Progressive Era](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/progressive-era.md) |
| 1906 | The Burke Act withholds citizenship until an allottee receives a fee patent and lets the Interior secretary declare allottees competent. — [Dawes Act](../articles/native-nations/dawes-act.md) |
| 1906 | The Niagara Movement holds its second meeting at Storer College in Harpers Ferry. — [W. E. B. Du Bois](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/w-e-b-du-bois.md) |
| 1906 | The San Francisco earthquake and fire destroy many birth and immigration records, opening the way for "paper son" identities. — [Angel Island Immigration Station](../articles/society-and-people/angel-island-immigration-station.md) |
| 1906 | The seat of government moves from Sitka to Juneau. — [Alaska](../articles/states-and-territories/alaska.md) |
| 1906-02 | Attends her last NAWSA convention, in Baltimore, and gives her final public remarks. — [Susan B. Anthony](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/susan-b-anthony.md) |
| 1906-02 | Cosmopolitan begins David Graham Phillips's nine-part series The Treason of the Senate. — [Muckrakers](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/muckrakers.md) |
| 1906-03-13 | Dies at her home in Rochester; she is buried in Mount Hope Cemetery. — [Susan B. Anthony](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/susan-b-anthony.md) |
| 1906-03-21 | A split at McClure's comes to a head; Tarbell, Steffens, Baker and John Phillips leave and take over The American Magazine. — [Muckrakers](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/muckrakers.md) |
| 1906-04-14 | Theodore Roosevelt delivers The Man with the Muck-Rake at the cornerstone laying of the House office building in Washington. — [Muckrakers](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/muckrakers.md) |
| 1906-04-18 | A major earthquake on the San Andreas Fault strikes San Francisco. — [California](../articles/states-and-territories/california.md) |
| 1906-05-22 | U.S. Patent 821,393 for the Wrights' flying machine is granted. — [Wright brothers' first flight](../articles/science-and-innovation/wright-brothers-first-flight.md) |
| 1906-06-08 | Signs the Antiquities Act, which he then uses to proclaim 18 national monuments. — [Theodore Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/theodore-roosevelt.md) |
| 1906-06-27 | The House follows the Senate in choosing a lock canal over a sea-level design. — [Panama Canal](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/panama-canal.md) |
| 1906-06-30 | Roosevelt signs the Food and Drugs Act; the Meat Inspection Act also becomes law that year after The Jungle. — [Muckrakers](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/muckrakers.md) |
| 1906-08-13 | A shooting in Brownsville, Texas, is blamed on Black soldiers of the 25th Infantry; Roosevelt later discharges 167 of them without honor or trial. — [Theodore Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/theodore-roosevelt.md) |
| 1907 | Pianist and composer Jelly Roll Morton begins touring outside New Orleans. — [Jazz](../articles/culture-and-arts/jazz.md) |
| 1907-02-08 | The United States and the Dominican Republic sign a convention at Santo Domingo making the receivership formal. — [Roosevelt Corollary](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/roosevelt-corollary.md) |
| 1907-02-25 | The U.S. Senate advises ratification of the Dominican convention. — [Roosevelt Corollary](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/roosevelt-corollary.md) |
| 1907-03-04 | Enrollment closes on the Dawes Rolls of the Five Tribes. — [Dawes Act](../articles/native-nations/dawes-act.md) |
| 1907-04-01 | Chief engineer John F. Stevens resigns and George Washington Goethals succeeds him. — [Panama Canal](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/panama-canal.md) |
| 1907-07-25 | Roosevelt proclaims the convention in force. — [Roosevelt Corollary](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/roosevelt-corollary.md) |
| 1908 | Ray Stannard Baker publishes Following the Color Line, on the oppression of Black Americans. — [Muckrakers](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/muckrakers.md) |
| 1908 | The AME Zion Church opens the Harriet Tubman Home for aged and poor African Americans on land she had transferred to it in 1903. — [Harriet Tubman](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/harriet-tubman.md) |
| 1908-12 | Edison, Biograph and other licensed producers form the Motion Picture Patents Company, a patent pool that tries to control American film production. — [Hollywood](../articles/culture-and-arts/hollywood.md) |
| 1909 | Canby's brother and nephew publish The Evolution of the American Flag, fixing the story in public memory. — [Betsy Ross flag myth](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/betsy-ross-flag-myth.md) |
| 1909 | Leaves the presidency after keeping a pledge not to seek another term. — [Theodore Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/theodore-roosevelt.md) |
| 1909 | Over the next two decades farmers break about 32 million acres of Great Plains sod, much of it for wheat. — [Dust Bowl](../articles/depression-and-world-war/dust-bowl.md) |
| 1909 | The NAACP is founded in the aftermath of the 1908 Springfield, Illinois, race riot. — [Progressive Era](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/progressive-era.md) |
| 1909 | Triangle workers join the garment strike known as the Uprising of the 20,000, which lasts about three months. — [Triangle Shirtwaist Factory fire](../articles/economy-and-work/triangle-shirtwaist-factory-fire.md) |
| 1909-05-31 | Speaks at the National Negro Conference in New York, which leads to the NAACP. — [W. E. B. Du Bois](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/w-e-b-du-bois.md) |
| 1909-08-24 | Workers pour the first concrete at the Gatun lock site. — [Gatun Locks](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/gatun-locks.md) |
| 1909-11 | The independent New York Motion Picture Company arrives in Los Angeles and stays; many more companies follow in 1910. — [Hollywood](../articles/culture-and-arts/hollywood.md) |
| 1910 – 1940 | **[Chinese detention-barracks poetry](../articles/culture-and-arts/chinese-detention-barracks-poetry.md)** (Work) |
| 1910 – 1970 | **[Great Migration](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/great-migration.md)** (Event) |
| 1910 | Almost nine in ten Black Americans live in the South; the First Great Migration begins. — [Great Migration](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/great-migration.md) |
| 1910 | Elected to the New York State Senate. — [Franklin D. Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/franklin-d-roosevelt.md) |
| 1910 | Southwesterners begin moving west to California in significant numbers. — [Okies](../articles/society-and-people/okies.md) |
| 1910 | The Angel Island Immigration Station opens in San Francisco Bay; it operates until 1940. — [Chinese Americans](../articles/society-and-people/chinese-americans.md) |
