| 1910 | The Great Migration out of the South begins; about six million people move north and west by 1970. — [African Americans](../articles/society-and-people/african-americans.md) |
| 1910 | The town of Hollywood merges with Los Angeles to secure a water supply. — [Hollywood](../articles/culture-and-arts/hollywood.md) |
| 1910-01-01 | Ford's Highland Park plant opens; it introduces a moving automobile assembly line in 1913. — [Detroit](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/detroit.md) |
| 1910-01-21 – 1940-11-05 | **[Angel Island Immigration Station](../articles/society-and-people/angel-island-immigration-station.md)** (Landmark) |
| 1910-01-21 | The U.S. Immigration Station on Angel Island opens. — [Angel Island Immigration Station](../articles/society-and-people/angel-island-immigration-station.md) |
| 1910-01-21 | The U.S. Immigration Station on Angel Island opens; poems begin to appear on the barracks walls within about ten months. — [Chinese detention-barracks poetry](../articles/culture-and-arts/chinese-detention-barracks-poetry.md) |
| 1910-02-16 | Wilbur Wright picks Frank Kohn's plantation near Montgomery, Alabama, for spring flight training. — [Wright Company School of Aviation](../articles/science-and-innovation/wright-company-school-of-aviation.md) |
| 1910-03 – 1916 | **[Wright Company School of Aviation](../articles/science-and-innovation/wright-company-school-of-aviation.md)** (Organization) |
| 1910-03-19 | The House adopts George Norris's resolution, 191 to 156, removing Speaker Joseph Cannon from the Rules Committee. — [United States House of Representatives](../articles/constitution-and-government/house-of-representatives.md) |
| 1910-03-26 | Orville makes Montgomery's first powered flight; training of the first students follows. — [Wright Company School of Aviation](../articles/science-and-innovation/wright-company-school-of-aviation.md) |
| 1910-05 | The Wright School of Aviation moves its training to Huffman Field. — [Huffman Prairie Flying Field](../articles/science-and-innovation/huffman-prairie.md) |
| 1910-05 | The school moves its permanent operations to Huffman Field (Simms Station) east of Dayton. — [Wright Company School of Aviation](../articles/science-and-innovation/wright-company-school-of-aviation.md) |
| 1910-05-06 | Walter Brookins flies solo for about 12 minutes, the Montgomery camp's only graduate. — [Wright Company School of Aviation](../articles/science-and-innovation/wright-company-school-of-aviation.md) |
| 1910-11 | Launches The Crisis as the NAACP's monthly magazine, which he edits until 1934. — [W. E. B. Du Bois](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/w-e-b-du-bois.md) |
| 1911 | A federal law fixes the House at 433 seats, plus one each for Arizona and New Mexico on statehood. — [United States Congress](../articles/constitution-and-government/united-states-congress.md) |
| 1911 | Deep in debt, S. S. McClure surrenders McClure's Magazine to creditors in the autumn. — [Muckrakers](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/muckrakers.md) |
| 1911 | In the 1911–1912 season the Nestor company builds what is regarded as the first studio in Hollywood, at Sunset and Gower. — [Hollywood](../articles/culture-and-arts/hollywood.md) |
| 1911-03-25 | **[Triangle Shirtwaist Factory fire](../articles/economy-and-work/triangle-shirtwaist-factory-fire.md)** (Event) |
| 1911-03-25 | Fire breaks out on the eighth floor near closing time; the first alarm goes out at 4:45 p.m. and 146 workers die. — [Triangle Shirtwaist Factory fire](../articles/economy-and-work/triangle-shirtwaist-factory-fire.md) |
| 1911-04-05 | The ILGWU organizes a funeral procession; about 100,000 people march behind the hearses on Fifth Avenue. — [Triangle Shirtwaist Factory fire](../articles/economy-and-work/triangle-shirtwaist-factory-fire.md) |
| 1911-04-11 | A grand jury indicts owners Max Blanck and Isaac Harris for manslaughter. — [Triangle Shirtwaist Factory fire](../articles/economy-and-work/triangle-shirtwaist-factory-fire.md) |
| 1911-05-07 | Army lieutenants Henry H. Arnold and Thomas DeW. Milling and Lt. John Rodgers arrive in Dayton for lessons. — [Wright Company School of Aviation](../articles/science-and-innovation/wright-company-school-of-aviation.md) |
| 1911-06-30 | The New York legislature creates the Factory Investigating Commission. — [Triangle Shirtwaist Factory fire](../articles/economy-and-work/triangle-shirtwaist-factory-fire.md) |
| 1911-07 | Erection of the upper lock gates at Gatun gets under way. — [Gatun Locks](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/gatun-locks.md) |
| 1911-08-08 | Congress fixes the House at 433 seats, plus one each for Arizona and New Mexico on statehood. — [Census and apportionment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/census-and-apportionment.md) |
| 1911-10 | The Society of American Indians, the first Native rights organization run by Native people, holds its founding conference in Columbus, Ohio. — [Progressive Era](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/progressive-era.md) |
| 1911-10-05 | Date given in the earliest dated poem yet recorded (Island poem 100). — [Chinese detention-barracks poetry](../articles/culture-and-arts/chinese-detention-barracks-poetry.md) |
| 1911-12-27 | A jury acquits Blanck and Harris. — [Triangle Shirtwaist Factory fire](../articles/economy-and-work/triangle-shirtwaist-factory-fire.md) |
| 1912 | **[Minimum wage](../articles/economy-and-work/minimum-wage.md)** (Policy) |
| 1912 | Carl Laemmle, a German-Jewish immigrant who had fought the Patents Company, founds Universal; he opens Universal City in 1915. — [Hollywood](../articles/culture-and-arts/hollywood.md) |
| 1912 | Congress makes Alaska a territory. — [Alaska](../articles/states-and-territories/alaska.md) |
| 1912 | Handy publishes "The Memphis Blues" and sells its rights for $100. — [Blues](../articles/culture-and-arts/blues.md) |
| 1912 | Massachusetts enacts the first state minimum wage law; it takes effect on July 1, 1913. — [Minimum wage](../articles/economy-and-work/minimum-wage.md) |
| 1912 | President William Howard Taft standardizes the 48-star flag in six rows of eight. — [National symbols of the United States](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/national-symbols-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1912 | Woodrow Wilson wins the presidency with 42 percent of the popular vote after Roosevelt's Progressive Party splits the Republicans. — [Progressive Era](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/progressive-era.md) |
| 1912-06-11 | Arthur L. Welsh, the school's first Dayton-trained pilot and later an instructor, is killed in a crash at College Park, Maryland. — [Wright Company School of Aviation](../articles/science-and-innovation/wright-company-school-of-aviation.md) |
| 1912-08 | Leads the new Progressive ("Bull Moose") Party after losing the Republican nomination to William Howard Taft. — [Theodore Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/theodore-roosevelt.md) |
| 1912-10-14 | Shot in Milwaukee before a speech; his folded speech and spectacle case slow the bullet. — [Theodore Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/theodore-roosevelt.md) |
| 1913 | AT&T gives up its stake in Western Union under federal antitrust pressure (the Kingsbury Commitment). — [Electric telegraph](../articles/science-and-innovation/telegraph.md) |
| 1913 | Charles Beard makes Federalist No. 10 the centrepiece of his economic critique of the framers. — [The Federalist Papers](../articles/constitution-and-government/federalist-papers.md) |
| 1913 | The Seventeenth Amendment, providing for direct election of senators, is ratified. — [Muckrakers](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/muckrakers.md) |
| 1913-03-03 | Alice Paul's suffrage procession in Washington, held the day before Woodrow Wilson's inauguration, is attacked by hostile crowds. — [Women's suffrage movement](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/womens-suffrage-movement.md) |
| 1913-03-10 | Dies of pneumonia in Auburn and is buried at Fort Hill Cemetery. — [Harriet Tubman](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/harriet-tubman.md) |
| 1913-03-17 | Sworn in as assistant secretary of the Navy. — [Franklin D. Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/franklin-d-roosevelt.md) |
| 1913-03-26 | The workforce peaks at 44,733 men on the job. — [Panama Canal](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/panama-canal.md) |
| 1913-04-08 | The Seventeenth Amendment, providing for popular election of senators, is ratified. — [United States Congress](../articles/constitution-and-government/united-states-congress.md) |
| 1913-04-08 | The Seventeenth Amendment, providing for popular election of senators, is ratified; the Sixteenth Amendment (income tax) and the Federal Reserve Act also date from 1913. — [Progressive Era](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/progressive-era.md) |
| 1913-08 | Blanck is fined $20 for locking a factory door during working hours at another shop. — [Triangle Shirtwaist Factory fire](../articles/economy-and-work/triangle-shirtwaist-factory-fire.md) |
| 1913-09-01 | Concreting of the canal's locks is completed. — [Gatun Locks](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/gatun-locks.md) |
| 1913-09-26 | **[Gatun Locks](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/gatun-locks.md)** (Landmark) |
| 1913-09-26 | The tug Gatun makes the first trial lockage, with valves worked by hand. — [Gatun Locks](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/gatun-locks.md) |
| 1913-10-10 | The Gamboa dike is destroyed, joining Gatun Lake with the Culebra Cut. — [Gatun Locks](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/gatun-locks.md) |
| 1913-12-23 | President Woodrow Wilson signs the Federal Reserve Act, creating a central bank after the Panic of 1907. — [Economic history of the United States](../articles/economy-and-work/economic-history-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1914 | Cecil B. DeMille makes Hollywood his permanent studio home. — [Hollywood](../articles/culture-and-arts/hollywood.md) |
| 1914 | Explores an uncharted river in Brazil. — [Theodore Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/theodore-roosevelt.md) |
| 1914 | Ford begins paying five dollars for an eight-hour day. — [Detroit](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/detroit.md) |
| 1914 | Handy publishes "St. Louis Blues," his best-known composition. — [Blues](../articles/culture-and-arts/blues.md) |
| 1914 | The Clayton Antitrust Act and the Federal Trade Commission law are enacted. — [Progressive Era](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/progressive-era.md) |
| 1914 | The Smith-Lever Act creates the Cooperative Extension System. — [Morrill Land-Grant College Act](../articles/science-and-innovation/morrill-land-grant-college-act.md) |
| 1914-03-11 | Blanck and Harris settle 23 civil suits, paying $75 for each life lost. — [Triangle Shirtwaist Factory fire](../articles/economy-and-work/triangle-shirtwaist-factory-fire.md) |
| 1914-03-19 | A Senate majority of 35 to 34 falls well short of the two-thirds needed. — [Nineteenth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/nineteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1914-08-15 | **[Panama Canal](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/panama-canal.md)** (Landmark) |
| 1914-08-15 | The Ancon makes the canal's first official transit. — [Gatun Locks](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/gatun-locks.md) |
| 1914-08-15 | The cement boat Ancon makes the first official transit, opening the canal. — [Panama Canal](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/panama-canal.md) |
| 1914-10 | Founds the Commission for Relief in Belgium to feed civilians in German-occupied Belgium, later northern France as well. — [Herbert Hoover](../articles/presidents-and-founders/herbert-hoover.md) |
| 1915 | Boll weevil damage and floods in 1915–1916 ruin cotton crops and push sharecroppers off the land. — [Great Migration](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/great-migration.md) |
| 1915 | Guinn v. United States unanimously strikes down Oklahoma's grandfather clause. — [Fifteenth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/fifteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1915 | Tom Brown's band leaves New Orleans for Chicago. — [Jazz](../articles/culture-and-arts/jazz.md) |
| 1915-06 | In Guinn v. United States the Supreme Court rules Oklahoma's grandfather clause violates the Fifteenth Amendment. — [Jim Crow laws](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/jim-crow-laws.md) |
| 1915-07 | U.S. Marines land in Haiti after the assassination of President Jean Vilbrun Guillaume Sam, beginning a nineteen-year occupation. — [Good Neighbor Policy](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/good-neighbor-policy.md) |
| 1915-07-05 | The bell leaves on its last journey, a tour of more than 10,000 miles to the Panama-Pacific Exposition in San Francisco. — [Liberty Bell](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/liberty-bell.md) |
| 1915-10 | Orville Wright sells the Wright Company to New York financiers. — [Wright Company School of Aviation](../articles/science-and-innovation/wright-company-school-of-aviation.md) |
| 1916 | President Woodrow Wilson names it the anthem of the armed forces; Congress does not act. — [The Star-Spangled Banner](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/star-spangled-banner.md) |
| 1916 | President Woodrow Wilson proclaims a national Flag Day on June 14. — [Betsy Ross flag myth](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/betsy-ross-flag-myth.md) |
| 1916 | President Woodrow Wilson proclaims a national Flag Day on June 14. — [National symbols of the United States](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/national-symbols-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1916 | The Adamson Act sets the first federally mandated eight-hour day, for railroad workers. — [Progressive Era](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/progressive-era.md) |
| 1916 | The Wright Company stops using the field after training 119 students there. — [Huffman Prairie Flying Field](../articles/science-and-innovation/huffman-prairie.md) |
| 1916 | The flying school at Huffman Field closes after training 119 students since May 1910. — [Wright Company School of Aviation](../articles/science-and-innovation/wright-company-school-of-aviation.md) |
| 1916-09 | Carrie Chapman Catt unveils NAWSA's Winning Plan at its Atlantic City convention. — [Women's suffrage movement](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/womens-suffrage-movement.md) |
| 1917 | Appointed by Woodrow Wilson to head the U.S. Food Administration. — [Herbert Hoover](../articles/presidents-and-founders/herbert-hoover.md) |
| 1917 | The Army leases the land around the field for Wilbur Wright Field, a World War I training post. — [Huffman Prairie Flying Field](../articles/science-and-innovation/huffman-prairie.md) |
| 1917 | The Senate adopts its first cloture rule to limit filibusters. — [United States Congress](../articles/constitution-and-government/united-states-congress.md) |
| 1917 | With European immigration cut off by World War I, northern labor agents recruit Black workers; almost half a million Black southerners leave in 1917–1918. — [Great Migration](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/great-migration.md) |
| 1917-01 | Alice Paul's National Woman's Party begins picketing the White House. — [Progressive Era](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/progressive-era.md) |
| 1917-01 | The National Woman's Party's Silent Sentinels begin picketing the White House. — [Women's suffrage movement](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/womens-suffrage-movement.md) |
| 1917-02 | The Original Dixieland Jazz Band makes the first commercial jazz recordings in New York City. — [Jazz](../articles/culture-and-arts/jazz.md) |
| 1917-04-02 | Jeannette Rankin of Montana is sworn in as the first woman elected to Congress. — [United States House of Representatives](../articles/constitution-and-government/house-of-representatives.md) |
| 1917-11 | New Orleans closes its Storyville red-light district. — [Jazz](../articles/culture-and-arts/jazz.md) |
| 1917-11 | New York adopts woman suffrage. — [Women's suffrage movement](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/womens-suffrage-movement.md) |
| 1918 | A Navy band plays it at Game 1 of the World Series, starting a ballpark tradition. — [The Star-Spangled Banner](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/star-spangled-banner.md) |
| 1918 | Congress passes a minimum wage law for women and minors in the District of Columbia. — [Minimum wage](../articles/economy-and-work/minimum-wage.md) |
| 1918 | The new National Park Service takes over management and the Army leaves. — [Yellowstone National Park](../articles/geography-and-environment/yellowstone-national-park.md) |
| 1918-01-10 | The House narrowly reaches a two-thirds majority for the amendment. — [Nineteenth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/nineteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1918-09-30 | President Wilson addresses the Senate to urge passage of the amendment, which fails the next day. — [Women's suffrage movement](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/womens-suffrage-movement.md) |
| 1918-10-01 | A day after President Woodrow Wilson addresses the Senate in its favor, the amendment fails 53 to 31, two votes short. — [Nineteenth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/nineteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1919 | Five days of racial violence leave 23 Black Chicagoans dead and about 300 wounded. — [Chicago](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/chicago.md) |
| 1919 | Takes part in a Pan-African congress in Paris on the fate of Africa's colonies after the First World War. — [W. E. B. Du Bois](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/w-e-b-du-bois.md) |
| 1919 | The Red Summer brings anti-Black riots in northern and southern cities as white residents react to demographic change. — [Great Migration](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/great-migration.md) |
| 1919-01-06 | Dies at Oyster Bay, New York. — [Theodore Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/theodore-roosevelt.md) |
| 1919-02 | Congress creates the American Relief Administration, which Hoover directs, with a $100 million budget. — [Herbert Hoover](../articles/presidents-and-founders/herbert-hoover.md) |
| 1919-02-10 | The Senate rejects it again, 55 to 29, one vote short. — [Nineteenth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/nineteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1919-05-21 | The House of Representatives passes the amendment. — [Women's suffrage movement](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/womens-suffrage-movement.md) |
| 1919-05-21 | The House of the new Sixty-sixth Congress passes the amendment. — [Nineteenth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/nineteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1919-06-04 | The Senate passes it 56 to 25, sending it to the states. — [Nineteenth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/nineteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1919-06-04 | The Senate passes the amendment 56 to 25, sending it to the states. — [Women's suffrage movement](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/womens-suffrage-movement.md) |
| 1919-06-10 | Wisconsin becomes the first state to ratify. — [Women's suffrage movement](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/womens-suffrage-movement.md) |
| 1919-06-10 | Wisconsin, Illinois and Michigan ratify; Wisconsin is counted as the first state. — [Nineteenth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/nineteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1920 | During the 1920s about 810,000 Black southerners leave the region, including 260,000 Georgians. — [Great Migration](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/great-migration.md) |
| 1920 | Mamie Smith records "Crazy Blues," the first commercial vaudeville blues record by a Black vocalist. — [Blues](../articles/culture-and-arts/blues.md) |
| 1920 | The census shows a shift to cities; Congress fails to reapportion for the only time. — [Census and apportionment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/census-and-apportionment.md) |
| 1920-01-27 | Wyoming ratifies the Nineteenth Amendment. — [Wyoming](../articles/states-and-territories/wyoming.md) |
| 1920-08-18 | **[Nineteenth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/nineteenth-amendment.md)** (Law) |
| 1920-08-18 | Tennessee becomes the 36th state to ratify the Nineteenth Amendment, the same year national Prohibition under the Eighteenth Amendment takes effect. — [Progressive Era](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/progressive-era.md) |
| 1920-08-18 | Tennessee becomes the thirty-sixth state to ratify, completing ratification. — [Women's suffrage movement](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/womens-suffrage-movement.md) |
| 1920-08-18 | Tennessee's House approves ratification 49 to 47, making Tennessee the thirty-sixth state. — [Nineteenth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/nineteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1920-08-26 | Secretary of State Bainbridge Colby certifies the Nineteenth Amendment. — [Women's suffrage movement](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/womens-suffrage-movement.md) |
| 1920-08-26 | Secretary of State Bainbridge Colby certifies the amendment. — [Nineteenth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/nineteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1920-09-25 | Pennsylvania suffragists first ring their replica Justice Bell at Independence Square after the Nineteenth Amendment. — [Liberty Bell](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/liberty-bell.md) |
| 1920-11-02 | Loses the vice-presidential election on the Democratic ticket headed by James Cox. — [Franklin D. Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/franklin-d-roosevelt.md) |
| 1921 | Becomes secretary of commerce under Warren Harding, a post he keeps under Calvin Coolidge. — [Herbert Hoover](../articles/presidents-and-founders/herbert-hoover.md) |
| 1921-08 | Stricken with polio at Campobello, New Brunswick; he never again walks unaided. — [Franklin D. Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/franklin-d-roosevelt.md) |
| 1921-08 | The Dow Jones Industrial Average stands at 63. — [Stock market crash of 1929](../articles/economy-and-work/stock-market-crash-of-1929.md) |
| 1922 | Kid Ory's band records in California, the first recording by a Black New Orleans jazz band; Louis Armstrong joins King Oliver's Creole Jazz Band in Chicago. — [Jazz](../articles/culture-and-arts/jazz.md) |
| 1922 | Producers form the Motion Picture Producers and Distributors of America under Will H. Hays to head off government censorship through self-regulation. — [Hollywood](../articles/culture-and-arts/hollywood.md) |
| 1922 | Reclamation surveyors J. G. Tierney and Harold Connelly drown in the Colorado River while studying possible dam sites. — [Hoover Dam](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/hoover-dam.md) |
| 1922-02-27 | In Leser v. Garnett the Supreme Court rejects challenges to the amendment's validity. — [Nineteenth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/nineteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1922-11-24 | Representatives of seven states and the federal government sign the Colorado River Compact at Santa Fe, New Mexico. — [Colorado River](../articles/geography-and-environment/colorado-river.md) |
| 1922-11-24 | Seven basin states sign the Colorado River Compact at Santa Fe. — [Hoover Dam](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/hoover-dam.md) |
| 1923 | Bessie Smith makes her first recordings, beginning a 14-year recording career of more than 150 songs. — [Blues](../articles/culture-and-arts/blues.md) |
| 1923 | In Adkins v. Children's Hospital the Supreme Court voids the District of Columbia law; sixteen states then have minimum wage laws for women. — [Minimum wage](../articles/economy-and-work/minimum-wage.md) |
| 1924 | Ma Rainey records "See See Rider Blues" with Louis Armstrong and Fletcher Henderson. — [Blues](../articles/culture-and-arts/blues.md) |
| 1924-06-02 | The Indian Citizenship Act makes Native Americans born in the United States citizens; about 40 percent had not been citizens before. — [Native Americans](../articles/native-nations/native-americans.md) |
| 1924-06-24 | The Indian Citizenship Act makes Native Americans U.S. citizens. — [Fourteenth Amendment](../articles/rights-and-law/fourteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1925 | A. Philip Randolph and Pullman porters found the Brotherhood of Sleeping Car Porters in New York City. — [Labor movement](../articles/economy-and-work/labor-movement.md) |
| 1925 | Armstrong's Hot Five and Hot Seven recordings (1925–1928) begin shifting jazz toward the improvising soloist. — [Jazz](../articles/culture-and-arts/jazz.md) |
| 1925 | Gitlow v. New York treats freedom of speech and of the press as protected against the states, beginning selective incorporation in earnest. — [Bill of Rights](../articles/rights-and-law/bill-of-rights.md) |
| 1925 | Illinois sets aside part of Cahokia as a state park. — [Cahokia](../articles/native-nations/cahokia.md) |
| 1925-01-05 | Nellie Tayloe Ross takes office as the first elected woman governor in the United States. — [Wyoming](../articles/states-and-territories/wyoming.md) |
| 1926 | U.S. Marines return to Nicaragua amid renewed fighting, a year after an earlier deployment ended. — [Good Neighbor Policy](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/good-neighbor-policy.md) |
| 1927 | Duke Ellington's band begins its engagement at Harlem's whites-only Cotton Club, with nightly radio broadcasts. — [Jazz](../articles/culture-and-arts/jazz.md) |
| 1927-03-02 | President Calvin Coolidge signs the act creating the Kill Devil Hill National Memorial. — [Wright brothers' first flight](../articles/science-and-innovation/wright-brothers-first-flight.md) |
| 1927-04-21 | The Mound Landing levee breaks during the Mississippi River flood; Hoover chairs the relief effort. — [Herbert Hoover](../articles/presidents-and-founders/herbert-hoover.md) |
| 1927-04-29 | During the Great Flood of 1927, engineers begin dynamiting the levee at Caernarvon, Louisiana, below New Orleans. — [Mississippi River](../articles/geography-and-environment/mississippi-river.md) |
| 1928 | Amid a feud with the Smithsonian, Orville ships the 1903 Flyer to the Science Museum in London. — [Wright brothers' first flight](../articles/science-and-innovation/wright-brothers-first-flight.md) |
| 1928 | Congress passes the Flood Control Act, shifting federal policy from levees alone to spillways and floodways. — [Mississippi River](../articles/geography-and-environment/mississippi-river.md) |
| 1928 | The Boulder Canyon Project Act approves the Compact, authorizes Hoover Dam and divides the Lower Basin's share among Arizona, California and Nevada. — [Colorado River](../articles/geography-and-environment/colorado-river.md) |
| 1928-11-06 | Elected governor of New York. — [Franklin D. Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/franklin-d-roosevelt.md) |
| 1928-12-17 | Undersecretary of State J. Reuben Clark's memorandum challenges the corollary on legal grounds. — [Roosevelt Corollary](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/roosevelt-corollary.md) |
| 1928-12-21 | President Coolidge signs the Boulder Canyon Project Act authorizing the dam. — [Hoover Dam](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/hoover-dam.md) |
| 1929-01-15 – 1968-04-04 | **[Martin Luther King Jr.](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/martin-luther-king-jr.md)** (Person) |
| 1929-01-15 | Born Michael King Jr. in Atlanta, Georgia; in 1934 his father changes both their names to Martin Luther King. — [Martin Luther King Jr.](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/martin-luther-king-jr.md) |
| 1929-02-02 | The Federal Reserve Board asks reserve banks to deny credit to member banks that lend to stock speculators. — [Stock market crash of 1929](../articles/economy-and-work/stock-market-crash-of-1929.md) |
| 1929-03-04 | Inaugurated as president after defeating Alfred E. Smith, 444 electoral votes to 87. — [Herbert Hoover](../articles/presidents-and-founders/herbert-hoover.md) |
| 1929-04-15 | Representative John Linthicum of Maryland introduces H.R. 14 to make it the national anthem. — [The Star-Spangled Banner](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/star-spangled-banner.md) |
| 1929-06-18 | The Reapportionment and Census Act makes apportionment automatic after the 1930 census and keeps 435 seats. — [Census and apportionment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/census-and-apportionment.md) |
| 1929-08 – 1941 | **[Great Depression](../articles/depression-and-world-war/great-depression.md)** (Era) |
| 1929-08 | The Board approves a rise in the New York Fed's discount rate to 6 percent. — [Stock market crash of 1929](../articles/economy-and-work/stock-market-crash-of-1929.md) |
| 1929-08 | The expansion of the 1920s ends and the economy begins to contract. — [Great Depression](../articles/depression-and-world-war/great-depression.md) |
| 1929-09-03 | The Dow closes at its peak of 381.17. — [Stock market crash of 1929](../articles/economy-and-work/stock-market-crash-of-1929.md) |
| 1929-10-24 – 1929-11 | **[Stock market crash of 1929](../articles/economy-and-work/stock-market-crash-of-1929.md)** (Event) |
| 1929-10-24 | Black Thursday: nearly 13 million shares trade; leading New York banks buy blue-chip stocks to steady prices. — [Stock market crash of 1929](../articles/economy-and-work/stock-market-crash-of-1929.md) |
| 1929-10-24 | Black Thursday: the New York Stock Exchange loses 11 percent of its value. — [Great Depression](../articles/depression-and-world-war/great-depression.md) |
| 1929-10-25 | President Hoover tells the press that the nation's basic business is sound. — [Stock market crash of 1929](../articles/economy-and-work/stock-market-crash-of-1929.md) |
| 1929-10-28 | Black Monday: the Dow falls nearly 13 percent. — [Stock market crash of 1929](../articles/economy-and-work/stock-market-crash-of-1929.md) |
| 1929-10-29 | Black Tuesday: more than 16 million shares change hands and over $14 billion in wealth is lost in a day. — [Great Depression](../articles/depression-and-world-war/great-depression.md) |
| 1929-10-29 | Black Tuesday: the Dow falls nearly 12 percent on record trading of over 16 million shares. — [Stock market crash of 1929](../articles/economy-and-work/stock-market-crash-of-1929.md) |
| 1929-10-30 | Before trading opens, the New York Fed announces it will buy $100 million in government securities. — [Stock market crash of 1929](../articles/economy-and-work/stock-market-crash-of-1929.md) |
| 1929-11 | After the stock market crash, asks business leaders to hold wages and labor leaders not to press for raises. — [Herbert Hoover](../articles/presidents-and-founders/herbert-hoover.md) |
| 1929-11 | By mid-November the Dow has lost almost half its value. — [Stock market crash of 1929](../articles/economy-and-work/stock-market-crash-of-1929.md) |
| 1930 | Depression-era hardship slows the outflow to nearly 400,000 during the 1930s. — [Great Migration](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/great-migration.md) |
| 1930 | Son House records for Paramount. — [Blues](../articles/culture-and-arts/blues.md) |
| 1930 | The industry adopts the Production Code; from 1934 the Production Code Administration under Joseph Breen enforces it. — [Hollywood](../articles/culture-and-arts/hollywood.md) |
| 1930-04-21 | The House of Representatives passes H.R. 14. — [The Star-Spangled Banner](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/star-spangled-banner.md) |
| 1930-06 | Hoover signs the Smoot-Hawley Tariff, raising duties on farm and industrial imports to historic highs. — [Great Depression](../articles/depression-and-world-war/great-depression.md) |
| 1930-06 | Signs the Smoot-Hawley Tariff. — [Herbert Hoover](../articles/presidents-and-founders/herbert-hoover.md) |
| 1930-09-17 | At a ceremony opening work on the project, Interior Secretary Ray Lyman Wilbur announces the name Hoover Dam. — [Hoover Dam](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/hoover-dam.md) |
| 1931 – 1940 | **[Dust Bowl](../articles/depression-and-world-war/dust-bowl.md)** (Event) |
| 1931 | Detainee Smiley Jann (Jann Mon Fong) copies 99 poems from the walls. — [Chinese detention-barracks poetry](../articles/culture-and-arts/chinese-detention-barracks-poetry.md) |
| 1931 | Drought ruins the Southern Plains wheat crop in the autumn, leaving fields bare. — [Dust Bowl](../articles/depression-and-world-war/dust-bowl.md) |
| 1931-03-03 | Congress makes The Star-Spangled Banner the national anthem. — [National symbols of the United States](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/national-symbols-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1931-03-03 | The Senate passes the bill and President Herbert Hoover signs it into law. — [The Star-Spangled Banner](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/star-spangled-banner.md) |
| 1931-03-11 | The construction contract is awarded to Six Companies on a bid of about $48.9 million. — [Hoover Dam](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/hoover-dam.md) |
| 1931-04-20 | **[Hoover Dam](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/hoover-dam.md)** (Landmark) |
| 1931-04-20 | Six Companies receives notice to proceed, the formal start of construction. — [Hoover Dam](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/hoover-dam.md) |
| 1931-08-07 | Workers strike after a pay cut for tunnel crews; the strike collapses and the cut stands. — [Hoover Dam](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/hoover-dam.md) |
| 1931-10 | After Britain leaves the gold standard, the New York Fed raises its discount rate; 522 commercial banks close that month. — [Great Depression](../articles/depression-and-world-war/great-depression.md) |
| 1932 | Detainee Tet Yee (Yee Tet Ming) copies 96 poems from the walls. — [Chinese detention-barracks poetry](../articles/culture-and-arts/chinese-detention-barracks-poetry.md) |
| 1932 | Thousands of World War I veterans march on Washington as the Bonus Army to demand early payment of promised bonuses, a precedent planners later wanted to avoid. — [Servicemen's Readjustment Act of 1944](../articles/cold-war-and-civil-rights/servicemens-readjustment-act-of-1944.md) |
| 1932-01 | By late January winds are lifting loose soil into dust storms. — [Dust Bowl](../articles/depression-and-world-war/dust-bowl.md) |
| 1932-01 | Creates the Reconstruction Finance Corporation to lend to banks, railroads and farm groups. — [Herbert Hoover](../articles/presidents-and-founders/herbert-hoover.md) |
| 1932-01-12 | Hattie Caraway becomes the first woman elected to the Senate. — [United States Congress](../articles/constitution-and-government/united-states-congress.md) |
| 1932-03-02 | The Senate authorizes its Banking and Currency Committee to investigate stock exchange practices. — [Stock market crash of 1929](../articles/economy-and-work/stock-market-crash-of-1929.md) |
| 1932-07 | Federal troops drive the Bonus Army of unemployed World War I veterans out of their camps in Washington, D.C. — [Great Depression](../articles/depression-and-world-war/great-depression.md) |
| 1932-07 | Troops under Douglas MacArthur, exceeding Hoover's orders, drive the Bonus Army out of Washington. — [Herbert Hoover](../articles/presidents-and-founders/herbert-hoover.md) |
| 1932-07-02 | Accepting the Democratic nomination in Chicago, Franklin D. Roosevelt pledges a new deal for the American people. — [New Deal](../articles/depression-and-world-war/new-deal.md) |
| 1932-07-08 | The Dow bottoms at 41.22, about 89 percent below its 1929 peak. — [Stock market crash of 1929](../articles/economy-and-work/stock-market-crash-of-1929.md) |
| 1932-10-13 | Chief Justice Charles Evans Hughes lays the cornerstone of the Supreme Court Building. — [Supreme Court of the United States](../articles/constitution-and-government/supreme-court-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1932-11-08 | Elected president, defeating Herbert Hoover. — [Franklin D. Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/franklin-d-roosevelt.md) |
| 1932-11-14 | The river is turned through the diversion tunnels around the dam site. — [Hoover Dam](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/hoover-dam.md) |
| 1933 | At the Montevideo conference the United States pledges non-intervention in the hemisphere. — [Roosevelt Corollary](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/roosevelt-corollary.md) |
| 1933 | California's Indigent Act makes it a crime to bring a poor nonresident into the state. — [Okies](../articles/society-and-people/okies.md) |
| 1933 | John and Alan Lomax first record Lead Belly while he is imprisoned in the Louisiana State Penitentiary. — [Blues](../articles/culture-and-arts/blues.md) |
| 1933-01-02 | The last U.S. Marines leave Nicaragua, two months before Franklin D. Roosevelt takes office. — [Good Neighbor Policy](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/good-neighbor-policy.md) |
| 1933-01-23 | The Twentieth Amendment is ratified, moving the end of presidential terms to noon on January 20. — [Presidency of the United States](../articles/constitution-and-government/presidency-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1933-03 – 1938 | **[New Deal](../articles/depression-and-world-war/new-deal.md)** (Policy) |
| 1933-03-04 | **[Good Neighbor Policy](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/good-neighbor-policy.md)** (Policy) |
| 1933-03-04 | Franklin D. Roosevelt is inaugurated with nearly 25 percent of the labor force jobless, and soon declares a national bank holiday. — [Great Depression](../articles/depression-and-world-war/great-depression.md) |
| 1933-03-04 | Leaves office after losing the 1932 election to Franklin D. Roosevelt. — [Herbert Hoover](../articles/presidents-and-founders/herbert-hoover.md) |
| 1933-03-04 | Roosevelt's first inaugural address dedicates the nation to the policy of the good neighbor. — [Good Neighbor Policy](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/good-neighbor-policy.md) |
| 1933-03-04 | Unemployment has reached about 25 percent when Franklin D. Roosevelt is inaugurated. — [Economic history of the United States](../articles/economy-and-work/economic-history-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1933-03-09 | Days after Roosevelt declares a bank holiday, he signs the Emergency Banking Act hours after Congress first sees it. — [New Deal](../articles/depression-and-world-war/new-deal.md) |
| 1933-03-12 | Gives his first radio fireside chat, on the banking crisis. — [Franklin D. Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/franklin-d-roosevelt.md) |
| 1933-03-12 | Roosevelt's first fireside chat urges Americans to return their savings to reopened banks. — [New Deal](../articles/depression-and-world-war/new-deal.md) |
| 1933-05 | Interior Secretary Harold Ickes orders the structure renamed Boulder Dam. — [Hoover Dam](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/hoover-dam.md) |
| 1933-05-12 | The Agricultural Adjustment Act begins paying farmers to cut production. — [New Deal](../articles/depression-and-world-war/new-deal.md) |
| 1933-06 | The Glass-Steagall Banking Act separates commercial and investment banking and creates the FDIC; the Hundred Days end. — [New Deal](../articles/depression-and-world-war/new-deal.md) |
| 1933-06-06 | The first concrete is placed. — [Hoover Dam](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/hoover-dam.md) |
| 1933-09 | The Soil Erosion Service is set up in the Interior Department with Hugh Hammond Bennett as chief. — [Dust Bowl](../articles/depression-and-world-war/dust-bowl.md) |
| 1933-12 | At the Montevideo Conference the Roosevelt administration backs a declaration that no state has the right to intervene in another's affairs. — [Monroe Doctrine](../articles/early-republic-and-expansion/monroe-doctrine.md) |
| 1933-12-22 | At Montevideo, Secretary of State Cordell Hull votes for the convention's non-intervention article but enters a U.S. reservation. — [Good Neighbor Policy](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/good-neighbor-policy.md) |
| 1933-12-26 | The Convention on Rights and Duties of States, whose Article 8 forbids intervention, is signed at Montevideo. — [Good Neighbor Policy](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/good-neighbor-policy.md) |
| 1934 | Franklin D. Roosevelt's Good Neighbor policy officially abandons the corollary. — [Roosevelt Corollary](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/roosevelt-corollary.md) |
| 1934 | The 1903 treaty with Cuba based on the Platt Amendment is abrogated, U.S. forces withdraw from Haiti, and Congress passes the Reciprocal Trade Agreements Act. — [Good Neighbor Policy](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/good-neighbor-policy.md) |
| 1934 | The Indian Reorganization Act ends allotment; about 48 million acres remain in Native hands, down from about 138 million in 1887. — [Dawes Act](../articles/native-nations/dawes-act.md) |
| 1934 | The Indian Reorganization Act ends the Dawes-era allotment policy and encourages tribal self-government. — [Great Depression](../articles/depression-and-world-war/great-depression.md) |
| 1934 | The Indian Reorganization Act ends the allotment policy of the Dawes Act and encourages tribal self-government. — [New Deal](../articles/depression-and-world-war/new-deal.md) |
| 1934 | The Indian Reorganization Act establishes modern tribal governments. — [Native Americans](../articles/native-nations/native-americans.md) |
| 1934-05-11 | A great dust storm carries Plains soil over Washington, D.C., and out over the Atlantic. — [Dust Bowl](../articles/depression-and-world-war/dust-bowl.md) |
| 1934-06 | Resigns from the NAACP after disputes with its leadership. — [W. E. B. Du Bois](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/w-e-b-du-bois.md) |
| 1934-06-06 | Roosevelt signs the Securities Exchange Act of 1934. — [Stock market crash of 1929](../articles/economy-and-work/stock-market-crash-of-1929.md) |
| 1935 | Congress passes the Social Security Act, the Wagner Act and the relief appropriation that funds the Works Progress Administration. — [Great Depression](../articles/depression-and-world-war/great-depression.md) |
| 1935 | Franklin D. Roosevelt approves a one-dollar bill design showing both sides of the Great Seal, putting the motto on the back of the note. — [E pluribus unum](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/e-pluribus-unum.md) |
| 1935 | Franklin D. Roosevelt approves a one-dollar bill showing both sides of the seal. — [Great Seal of the United States](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/great-seal-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1935 | John L. Lewis and allied unions form the Committee for Industrial Organization inside the AFL. — [Labor movement](../articles/economy-and-work/labor-movement.md) |
| 1935 | Publishes Black Reconstruction in America. — [W. E. B. Du Bois](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/w-e-b-du-bois.md) |
| 1935 | The Court moves into its own building in its 146th year. — [Supreme Court of the United States](../articles/constitution-and-government/supreme-court-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1935 | The migration to California begins in earnest; that summer economist Paul Taylor reports on white migrant families in the farm districts. — [Okies](../articles/society-and-people/okies.md) |
| 1935-02-01 | The dam begins impounding water in Lake Mead. — [Hoover Dam](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/hoover-dam.md) |
| 1935-03-21 | A second March dust cloud darkens Washington as Congress holds hearings on soil conservation. — [Dust Bowl](../articles/depression-and-world-war/dust-bowl.md) |
| 1935-04-14 | Black Sunday: a dust front sweeps from central Nebraska toward the Mexican border; a reporter's account names the Dust Bowl. — [Dust Bowl](../articles/depression-and-world-war/dust-bowl.md) |
| 1935-04-27 | Roosevelt signs the Soil Conservation Act, creating the permanent Soil Conservation Service in the Agriculture Department. — [Dust Bowl](../articles/depression-and-world-war/dust-bowl.md) |
| 1935-05 | In the Schechter case the Supreme Court strikes down the National Industrial Recovery Act. — [New Deal](../articles/depression-and-world-war/new-deal.md) |
| 1935-05-29 | The last concrete is placed. — [Hoover Dam](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/hoover-dam.md) |
| 1935-06 | Roosevelt sends Congress a list of must-pass bills, opening the Second New Deal; that summer Congress passes the Wagner Act and the Social Security Act. — [New Deal](../articles/depression-and-world-war/new-deal.md) |
| 1935-07-05 | President Franklin D. Roosevelt signs the National Labor Relations (Wagner) Act. — [Labor movement](../articles/economy-and-work/labor-movement.md) |
| 1935-08-14 | Signs the Social Security Act. — [Franklin D. Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/franklin-d-roosevelt.md) |
| 1935-09-30 | President Franklin D. Roosevelt dedicates the dam, then called Boulder Dam; Hansen's star map later records the sky at this moment. — [Winged Figures of the Republic](../articles/culture-and-arts/winged-figures-of-the-republic.md) |
| 1935-09-30 | President Franklin D. Roosevelt dedicates the dam. — [Hoover Dam](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/hoover-dam.md) |
| 1936 | In United States v. Butler the Supreme Court strikes down the Agricultural Adjustment Act's processing tax. — [New Deal](../articles/depression-and-world-war/new-deal.md) |
| 1936 | Los Angeles police post a border patrol, the "bum blockade", to turn back poor arrivals; it is withdrawn under threat of lawsuits. — [Okies](../articles/society-and-people/okies.md) |
| 1936 | Robert Johnson makes the first of his 1936–1937 recordings. — [Blues](../articles/culture-and-arts/blues.md) |
| 1936-02-29 | Reclamation formally accepts the completed dam from Six Companies. — [Hoover Dam](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/hoover-dam.md) |
| 1936-09-06 | In a fireside chat Roosevelt reports on his tour of nine drought states and outlines work relief and land-use plans. — [Dust Bowl](../articles/depression-and-world-war/dust-bowl.md) |
| 1936-10-26 | The first generating unit, N-2, goes into full operation. — [Hoover Dam](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/hoover-dam.md) |
| 1936-12-03 | The Buenos Aires peace conference opens with Roosevelt in attendance; the United States accepts its non-intervention protocol without reservation. — [Good Neighbor Policy](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/good-neighbor-policy.md) |
| 1936-12-30 | Auto workers sit down inside General Motors' Fisher Body plant in Flint, Michigan. — [Labor movement](../articles/economy-and-work/labor-movement.md) |
| 1937 | After federal spending cuts, a sharp recession pushes unemployment up again and cuts industrial production by about a third. — [Great Depression](../articles/depression-and-world-war/great-depression.md) |
| 1937 | In Breedlove v. Suttles the Court holds that a Georgia poll tax charged to men but not to non-registering women does not violate the amendment. — [Nineteenth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/nineteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1937 | In February Roosevelt asks Congress to add up to six justices to the Supreme Court; the plan fails, but the Court begins upholding New Deal laws. — [New Deal](../articles/depression-and-world-war/new-deal.md) |
| 1937 | Migration peaks in 1937 and 1938; up to 37,000 migrants reach Arizona's cotton fields. — [Okies](../articles/society-and-people/okies.md) |
| 1937 | Spending cuts help bring on a sharp recession beginning in the fall; industrial production falls by a third. — [New Deal](../articles/depression-and-world-war/new-deal.md) |
| 1937 | The Brownlow Committee reports that the president needs more staff help. — [Presidency of the United States](../articles/constitution-and-government/presidency-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1937 | West Coast Hotel Co. v. Parrish repudiates the Lochner approach to economic regulation. — [Due process](../articles/rights-and-law/due-process.md) |
| 1937-02-05 | Asks Congress to add justices to the Supreme Court; the plan is shelved in July. — [Franklin D. Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/franklin-d-roosevelt.md) |
| 1937-02-05 | President Franklin D. Roosevelt asks Congress for up to six additional justices. — [Supreme Court of the United States](../articles/constitution-and-government/supreme-court-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1937-02-11 | After 44 days General Motors reaches an agreement with the United Automobile Workers. — [Labor movement](../articles/economy-and-work/labor-movement.md) |
| 1937-03-29 | In West Coast Hotel Co. v. Parrish the Court upholds Washington's minimum wage law and overrules Adkins. — [Minimum wage](../articles/economy-and-work/minimum-wage.md) |
| 1937-03-29 | The Court upholds a Washington State minimum-wage law for women in West Coast Hotel v. Parrish. — [Supreme Court of the United States](../articles/constitution-and-government/supreme-court-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1937-05-24 | Franklin D. Roosevelt sends a wages-and-hours bill to Congress. — [Minimum wage](../articles/economy-and-work/minimum-wage.md) |
| 1937-07-07 | Fighting at the Marco Polo Bridge near Beijing opens full-scale war between Japan and China, turning U.S. opinion toward China. — [Attack on Pearl Harbor](../articles/depression-and-world-war/attack-on-pearl-harbor.md) |
| 1937-07-22 | Roosevelt's plan to enlarge the Court is defeated in the Senate. — [Supreme Court of the United States](../articles/constitution-and-government/supreme-court-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1937-08-04 | The first soil conservation district is organized, in North Carolina's Brown Creek watershed. — [Dust Bowl](../articles/depression-and-world-war/dust-bowl.md) |
| 1938 | The Fair Labor Standards Act sets a federal minimum wage and a 40-hour week, the last major New Deal law. — [Great Depression](../articles/depression-and-world-war/great-depression.md) |
| 1938 | The Fair Labor Standards Act sets a minimum wage and a 40-hour week, the last major New Deal law. — [New Deal](../articles/depression-and-world-war/new-deal.md) |
| 1938 | The industrial unions form the independent Congress of Industrial Organizations. — [Labor movement](../articles/economy-and-work/labor-movement.md) |
| 1938 | The monument plaza on the Nevada side is completed. — [Winged Figures of the Republic](../articles/culture-and-arts/winged-figures-of-the-republic.md) |
| 1938-03-18 | Mexican President Lázaro Cárdenas expropriates nearly all foreign oil companies' assets in Mexico. — [Good Neighbor Policy](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/good-neighbor-policy.md) |
| 1938-04-15 | A Reclamation photograph shows Hansen checking fabrication of the Circle of Precession for the terrazzo star map. — [Winged Figures of the Republic](../articles/culture-and-arts/winged-figures-of-the-republic.md) |
| 1938-05-26 | The House creates the Special Committee on Un-American Activities, known as the Dies Committee after its chair, Martin Dies Jr. of Texas. — [McCarthyism](../articles/cold-war-and-civil-rights/mccarthyism.md) |
| 1938-06-25 | Roosevelt signs the Fair Labor Standards Act. — [Minimum wage](../articles/economy-and-work/minimum-wage.md) |
| 1938-08-15 | Hansen is photographed at the figures with Carl J. Hambro, president of the Norwegian Parliament. — [Winged Figures of the Republic](../articles/culture-and-arts/winged-figures-of-the-republic.md) |
| 1938-10-24 | The first federal minimum wage, 25 cents an hour, takes effect. — [Minimum wage](../articles/economy-and-work/minimum-wage.md) |
| 1939 | Franklin D. Roosevelt creates the Executive Office of the President. — [Presidency of the United States](../articles/constitution-and-government/presidency-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1939 | Franklin D. Roosevelt moves Thanksgiving to the second-to-last Thursday of November; states split over which date to observe. — [Thanksgiving](../articles/culture-and-arts/thanksgiving.md) |
| 1939 | John Steinbeck's The Grapes of Wrath and Carey McWilliams's Factories in the Field are published; county prosecutors charge more than two dozen people under the Indigent Act. — [Okies](../articles/society-and-people/okies.md) |
| 1939 | Lane v. Wilson holds that the amendment reaches subtle as well as overt discrimination. — [Fifteenth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/fifteenth-amendment.md) |
| 1939-02-24 | The canal report later adopted as the plan for the work (House Document No. 210) is dated. — [Third Locks Project](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/third-locks-project.md) |
| 1939-03-02 | Massachusetts ratifies the Bill of Rights on its 150th anniversary; Georgia and Connecticut follow later that spring. — [Bill of Rights](../articles/rights-and-law/bill-of-rights.md) |
| 1939-08-11 – 1942-05-23 | **[Third Locks Project](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/third-locks-project.md)** (Event) |
| 1939-08-11 | President Franklin D. Roosevelt signs H.R. 5129, capping the cost at $277 million, and objects to its citizenship hiring rule. — [Third Locks Project](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/third-locks-project.md) |
| 1939-08-19 | The State Department sends Roosevelt's statement to the U.S. ambassador to share with Panama's government. — [Third Locks Project](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/third-locks-project.md) |
| 1939-09 | War breaks out in Europe; Roosevelt declares the United States neutral in law, and a new Neutrality Act lets warring nations buy American arms only on a cash-and-carry basis. — [World War II and the United States](../articles/depression-and-world-war/world-war-ii-and-the-united-states.md) |
| 1939-12 | Fred Edwards drives from California to Spur, Texas, to bring back his brother-in-law Frank Duncan, and is later convicted. — [Okies](../articles/society-and-people/okies.md) |
| 1940 | The Attorney General concludes that no American Indians remain 'not taxed' for census purposes. — [Census and apportionment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/census-and-apportionment.md) |
| 1940 | The Second Great Migration begins as defense industries expand; about 1.45 million Black southerners leave in the 1940s, many for Los Angeles, Oakland, Portland and Seattle. — [Great Migration](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/great-migration.md) |
| 1940 | With near-normal rain returning from 1938, the drought ends. — [Dust Bowl](../articles/depression-and-world-war/dust-bowl.md) |
| 1940-01-13 | The State Department tells Panama that a funding bill will again lack equal hiring for Panamanians, with an amending bill promised. — [Third Locks Project](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/third-locks-project.md) |
| 1940-07-01 | Excavation begins for a wider third set of locks near the existing ones. — [Gatun Locks](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/gatun-locks.md) |
| 1940-07-01 | Excavation begins, with dredging at the Pacific end of the channel to the new Miraflores lock site. — [Third Locks Project](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/third-locks-project.md) |
| 1940-08-12 | Fire destroys the administration building, including the women's quarters and many records. — [Angel Island Immigration Station](../articles/society-and-people/angel-island-immigration-station.md) |
| 1940-08-12 | Fire destroys the administration building, which held the women's quarters. — [Chinese detention-barracks poetry](../articles/culture-and-arts/chinese-detention-barracks-poetry.md) |
| 1940-09-02 | Roosevelt signs the destroyers-for-bases agreement, trading more than 50 old destroyers to Britain for 99-year leases on bases in Newfoundland and the Caribbean. — [World War II and the United States](../articles/depression-and-world-war/world-war-ii-and-the-united-states.md) |
| 1940-09-27 | Japan signs the Tripartite Pact with Germany and Italy. — [Attack on Pearl Harbor](../articles/depression-and-world-war/attack-on-pearl-harbor.md) |
| 1940-11-05 | The immigration station moves to the mainland. — [Chinese detention-barracks poetry](../articles/culture-and-arts/chinese-detention-barracks-poetry.md) |
| 1940-11-05 | The station's operations move to the mainland in San Francisco and the site returns to the Army. — [Angel Island Immigration Station](../articles/society-and-people/angel-island-immigration-station.md) |
| 1941 | Alan Lomax records Muddy Waters for the Library of Congress. — [Blues](../articles/culture-and-arts/blues.md) |
| 1941 | Thelonious Monk is house pianist at Minton's Playhouse in Harlem, where after-hours sessions help create bebop. — [Jazz](../articles/culture-and-arts/jazz.md) |
| 1941 | United States v. Darby calls the Tenth Amendment a truism, adding nothing to the question of enumerated federal powers. — [Federalism](../articles/constitution-and-government/federalism.md) |
| 1941 | Wartime demand restores full employment, ending the Depression. — [Great Depression](../articles/depression-and-world-war/great-depression.md) |
| 1941-02-03 | In United States v. Darby the Court upholds Congress's power to set wage and hour standards. — [Minimum wage](../articles/economy-and-work/minimum-wage.md) |
| 1941-02-19 | Dry excavation begins in the Gatun sector. — [Third Locks Project](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/third-locks-project.md) |
| 1941-03-11 | The Lend-Lease Act is approved, letting the President sell, lend or lease war materiel to any country whose defense he deems vital to U.S. defense. — [World War II and the United States](../articles/depression-and-world-war/world-war-ii-and-the-united-states.md) |
| 1941-06-25 | After A. Philip Randolph threatens a march on Washington, Executive Order 8802 bars discrimination in defense industries with government contracts. — [Civil rights movement](../articles/cold-war-and-civil-rights/civil-rights-movement.md) |
| 1941-06-25 | Issues Executive Order 8802 against discrimination in defense industries. — [Franklin D. Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/franklin-d-roosevelt.md) |
| 1941-07 | After Japanese forces move into French Indochina, the United States and Britain impose a full oil embargo and Washington freezes Japanese assets. — [Attack on Pearl Harbor](../articles/depression-and-world-war/attack-on-pearl-harbor.md) |
| 1941-11-15 | Congress adopts the method of equal proportions for all future apportionments. — [Census and apportionment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/census-and-apportionment.md) |
| 1941-11-24 | In Edwards v. California the Supreme Court strikes down the law, holding that states cannot bar poor migrants from other states. — [Okies](../articles/society-and-people/okies.md) |
| 1941-12-07 | **[Attack on Pearl Harbor](../articles/depression-and-world-war/attack-on-pearl-harbor.md)** (Conflict) |
| 1941-12-07 – 1945-09-02 | **[World War II and the United States](../articles/depression-and-world-war/world-war-ii-and-the-united-states.md)** (Conflict) |
| 1941-12-07 | About 7:55 a.m. the first wave of 183 Japanese aircraft strikes Oʻahu; a second wave of 170 arrives about 8:50 a.m.; the attack lasts about 75 minutes. — [Attack on Pearl Harbor](../articles/depression-and-world-war/attack-on-pearl-harbor.md) |
| 1941-12-07 | Japan attacks Pearl Harbor; a presidential proclamation makes Japanese nationals in the U.S. liable to be detained as enemy aliens. — [Japanese American incarceration during World War II](../articles/depression-and-world-war/japanese-american-incarceration.md) |
| 1941-12-07 | Japan attacks the U.S. fleet and airfields at Pearl Harbor, Hawaiʻi. — [World War II and the United States](../articles/depression-and-world-war/world-war-ii-and-the-united-states.md) |
| 1941-12-07 | On the day of the Pearl Harbor attack, the territorial governor signs a declaration of martial law. — [Hawaii](../articles/states-and-territories/hawaii.md) |
| 1941-12-08 | Asks Congress to declare war on Japan after the attack on Pearl Harbor. — [Franklin D. Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/franklin-d-roosevelt.md) |
| 1941-12-08 | Congress declares war on Japan, 82-0 in the Senate and 388-1 in the House. — [World War II and the United States](../articles/depression-and-world-war/world-war-ii-and-the-united-states.md) |
| 1941-12-08 | Roosevelt addresses a joint session of Congress at 12:30 p.m.; Congress declares war on Japan, with one dissent, and he signs the declaration at 4 p.m. — [Attack on Pearl Harbor](../articles/depression-and-world-war/attack-on-pearl-harbor.md) |
| 1941-12-11 | Germany and Italy declare war on the United States, and Congress answers with declarations of war against both the same day. — [World War II and the United States](../articles/depression-and-world-war/world-war-ii-and-the-united-states.md) |
| 1941-12-11 | Germany declares war on the United States. — [Attack on Pearl Harbor](../articles/depression-and-world-war/attack-on-pearl-harbor.md) |
| 1941-12-26 | Roosevelt signs H.J. Res. 41, fixing Thanksgiving on the fourth Thursday of November from 1942. — [Thanksgiving](../articles/culture-and-arts/thanksgiving.md) |
| 1942 | A joint resolution of Congress puts the Pledge of Allegiance into federal law. — [National symbols of the United States](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/national-symbols-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1942 | Japan occupies Attu and Kiska; U.S. authorities evacuate 881 Unangax̂ from nine villages to camps in Southeast Alaska. — [Alaska](../articles/states-and-territories/alaska.md) |
| 1942-02-19 – 1946-03-20 | **[Japanese American incarceration during World War II](../articles/depression-and-world-war/japanese-american-incarceration.md)** (Event) |
| 1942-02-19 | President Franklin D. Roosevelt signs Executive Order 9066, letting military commanders designate areas from which any person may be excluded. — [Japanese American incarceration during World War II](../articles/depression-and-world-war/japanese-american-incarceration.md) |
| 1942-02-19 | Roosevelt signs Executive Order 9066, leading to the forced removal and incarceration of Japanese Americans. — [World War II and the United States](../articles/depression-and-world-war/world-war-ii-and-the-united-states.md) |
| 1942-02-19 | Roosevelt signs Executive Order 9066, which leads to the mass incarceration of Japanese Americans on the West Coast. — [Attack on Pearl Harbor](../articles/depression-and-world-war/attack-on-pearl-harbor.md) |
| 1942-02-19 | Signs Executive Order 9066, which leads to the mass incarceration of Japanese Americans. — [Franklin D. Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/franklin-d-roosevelt.md) |
| 1942-02-28 | White crowds confront and turn back the first Black families moving into the federally funded Sojourner Truth Homes; the mayor postpones occupancy. — [1943 Detroit race riot](../articles/depression-and-world-war/detroit-race-riot-of-1943.md) |
| 1942-03-18 | Executive Order 9102 creates the War Relocation Authority (WRA) to run the camps. — [Japanese American incarceration during World War II](../articles/depression-and-world-war/japanese-american-incarceration.md) |
| 1942-03-21 | Public Law 503 makes violating an exclusion order a federal crime. — [Japanese American incarceration during World War II](../articles/depression-and-world-war/japanese-american-incarceration.md) |
| 1942-03-23 | The first Civilian Exclusion Order removes Japanese Americans from Bainbridge Island, Washington. — [Japanese American incarceration during World War II](../articles/depression-and-world-war/japanese-american-incarceration.md) |
| 1942-04-18 | The Cooke-Zevada agreement commits Mexico to pay about $29 million to American oil firms. — [Good Neighbor Policy](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/good-neighbor-policy.md) |
| 1942-04-29 | Black families move into Sojourner Truth Homes under the protection of more than 1,500 troopers and police. — [1943 Detroit race riot](../articles/depression-and-world-war/detroit-race-riot-of-1943.md) |
| 1942-05-23 | Secretary of War Henry Stimson halts the project after nearly 13 million cubic yards have been removed. — [Third Locks Project](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/third-locks-project.md) |
| 1942-05-23 | Secretary of War Henry Stimson terminates the Third Locks Project. — [Gatun Locks](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/gatun-locks.md) |
| 1942-05-27 | Admiral Chester Nimitz presents the Navy Cross to Mess Attendant Doris Miller for his conduct aboard USS West Virginia during the attack. — [Attack on Pearl Harbor](../articles/depression-and-world-war/attack-on-pearl-harbor.md) |
| 1942-06-04 | The Battle of Midway begins; by June 6 U.S. aircraft have wrecked four Japanese carriers, and USS Yorktown sinks on June 7. — [World War II and the United States](../articles/depression-and-world-war/world-war-ii-and-the-united-states.md) |
| 1942-06-22 | A federal act sets out how people should stand and salute while the anthem is played. — [The Star-Spangled Banner](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/star-spangled-banner.md) |
| 1942-08-04 | The bracero program begins bringing Mexican farm workers to the United States. — [World War II and the United States](../articles/depression-and-world-war/world-war-ii-and-the-united-states.md) |
| 1942-08-12 | The first Japanese Americans arrive at the Heart Mountain incarceration camp between Cody and Powell. — [Wyoming](../articles/states-and-territories/wyoming.md) |
| 1942-11 | American combat troops land in French Morocco, opening the campaign in North Africa. — [World War II and the United States](../articles/depression-and-world-war/world-war-ii-and-the-united-states.md) |
| 1942-11 | Congress lowers the minimum draft age to 18; Representative Jennings Randolph of West Virginia proposes a constitutional amendment to lower the voting age. — [Twenty-sixth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/twenty-sixth-amendment.md) |
| 1943 | During World War II Congress repeals the exclusion laws, sets a Chinese quota of about 105 a year and opens naturalization. — [Chinese Exclusion Act](../articles/gilded-age-and-progressive-era/chinese-exclusion-act.md) |
| 1943 | Georgia becomes the first state to lower its voting age to 18. — [Twenty-sixth Amendment](../articles/democracy-and-elections/twenty-sixth-amendment.md) |
| 1943 | In West Virginia State Board of Education v. Barnette the Supreme Court rules that public schools cannot compel the flag salute and pledge. — [National symbols of the United States](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/national-symbols-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1943 | The Magnuson Act repeals the exclusion laws, sets a Chinese quota of 105 a year and opens naturalization. — [Chinese Americans](../articles/society-and-people/chinese-americans.md) |
| 1943-02-01 | The Army activates the 442nd Regimental Combat Team, combining the 100th Battalion with mainland Nisei volunteers. — [Japanese American incarceration during World War II](../articles/depression-and-world-war/japanese-american-incarceration.md) |
| 1943-02-03 | The WRA begins giving incarcerated adults the so-called loyalty questionnaire. — [Japanese American incarceration during World War II](../articles/depression-and-world-war/japanese-american-incarceration.md) |
| 1943-06 | The National Resources Planning Board recommends education and training programs for returning service members. — [Servicemen's Readjustment Act of 1944](../articles/cold-war-and-civil-rights/servicemens-readjustment-act-of-1944.md) |
| 1943-06 | White workers at Packard walk off the job to protest the promotion of three Black workers, one of several wartime 'hate strikes' in Detroit plants. — [1943 Detroit race riot](../articles/depression-and-world-war/detroit-race-riot-of-1943.md) |
| 1943-06-20 – 1943-06-21 | **[1943 Detroit race riot](../articles/depression-and-world-war/detroit-race-riot-of-1943.md)** (Event) |
| 1943-06-20 | Fighting on Belle Isle sets off a riot in which 34 people die, 25 of them Black. — [Detroit](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/detroit.md) |
| 1943-06-20 | On a hot Sunday with nearly 100,000 people on Belle Isle, fighting between Black and white youths breaks out; police quell it by about midnight. — [1943 Detroit race riot](../articles/depression-and-world-war/detroit-race-riot-of-1943.md) |
| 1943-06-21 | At Governor Harry Kelly's request, President Roosevelt issues Proclamation 2588 and federal troops enter the city that evening. — [1943 Detroit race riot](../articles/depression-and-world-war/detroit-race-riot-of-1943.md) |
| 1943-06-21 | Competing rumors spread overnight; Black crowds loot white-owned stores in Paradise Valley while white mobs on Woodward Avenue beat Black people and burn their cars. — [1943 Detroit race riot](../articles/depression-and-world-war/detroit-race-riot-of-1943.md) |
| 1943-07 | The Navy cancels the Montana-class battleships whose width the new locks were meant to accommodate. — [Third Locks Project](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/third-locks-project.md) |
| 1943-07-31 | Tule Lake is designated a segregation center for people the government labeled disloyal. — [Japanese American incarceration during World War II](../articles/depression-and-world-war/japanese-american-incarceration.md) |
| 1943-08 | Governor Kelly's fact-finding committee issues its report, which critics call a whitewash of local authorities. — [1943 Detroit race riot](../articles/depression-and-world-war/detroit-race-riot-of-1943.md) |
| 1943-11 | The American Legion drafts its own "GI Bill of Rights"; former national commander Harry Colmery sketches its ideas at the Mayflower Hotel in Washington. — [Servicemen's Readjustment Act of 1944](../articles/cold-war-and-civil-rights/servicemens-readjustment-act-of-1944.md) |
| 1944 | A water treaty with Mexico commits 1.5 million acre-feet a year of the river's flow to Mexico. — [Colorado River](../articles/geography-and-environment/colorado-river.md) |
| 1944 | Returns to the NAACP as director of special research; forced out again in 1948. — [W. E. B. Du Bois](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/w-e-b-du-bois.md) |
| 1944 | The G.I. Bill offers veterans low-interest home loans, college stipends and business loans. — [Economic history of the United States](../articles/economy-and-work/economic-history-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1944 | The bill, S. 1767, passes both chambers of Congress unanimously in the spring. — [Servicemen's Readjustment Act of 1944](../articles/cold-war-and-civil-rights/servicemens-readjustment-act-of-1944.md) |
| 1944-01-22 | Creates the War Refugee Board. — [Franklin D. Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/franklin-d-roosevelt.md) |
| 1944-06-06 | D-Day: almost 133,000 Allied troops land on five Normandy beaches under General Eisenhower's command. — [World War II and the United States](../articles/depression-and-world-war/world-war-ii-and-the-united-states.md) |
| 1944-06-22 | **[Servicemen's Readjustment Act of 1944](../articles/cold-war-and-civil-rights/servicemens-readjustment-act-of-1944.md)** (Law) |
| 1944-06-22 | President Franklin D. Roosevelt signs the Servicemen's Readjustment Act, sixteen days after D-Day. — [Servicemen's Readjustment Act of 1944](../articles/cold-war-and-civil-rights/servicemens-readjustment-act-of-1944.md) |
| 1944-11-07 | Elected to a fourth term, defeating Thomas E. Dewey. — [Franklin D. Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/franklin-d-roosevelt.md) |
| 1944-12-18 | The Supreme Court upholds exclusion in Korematsu v. United States but rules in Ex parte Endo that loyal citizens cannot be held. — [Japanese American incarceration during World War II](../articles/depression-and-world-war/japanese-american-incarceration.md) |
| 1945 – 1991-12-25 | **[Cold War](../articles/cold-war-and-civil-rights/cold-war.md)** (Era) |
| 1945 | U.S. telegraph traffic peaks at about 236 million messages; Western Union and Postal Telegraph merge. — [Electric telegraph](../articles/science-and-innovation/telegraph.md) |
| 1945-01-02 | The West Coast exclusion orders are lifted. — [Japanese American incarceration during World War II](../articles/depression-and-world-war/japanese-american-incarceration.md) |
| 1945-01-03 | The House makes the Committee on Un-American Activities (HUAC) a permanent standing committee. — [McCarthyism](../articles/cold-war-and-civil-rights/mccarthyism.md) |
| 1945-02-16 | The territorial legislature approves the Anti-Discrimination Act after testimony by Elizabeth Peratrovich. — [Alaska](../articles/states-and-territories/alaska.md) |
| 1945-04-12 | Dies at Warm Springs, Georgia. — [Franklin D. Roosevelt](../articles/presidents-and-founders/franklin-d-roosevelt.md) |
| 1945-05 | Berlin falls days after Hitler's suicide, and Americans celebrate V-E Day. — [World War II and the United States](../articles/depression-and-world-war/world-war-ii-and-the-united-states.md) |
| 1945-07-17 | The Potsdam Conference opens; Truman and Stalin clash over German reparations and the Big Three fail to reach a lasting postwar settlement. — [Cold War](../articles/cold-war-and-civil-rights/cold-war.md) |
| 1945-08-06 | A U.S. atomic bomb destroys Hiroshima; a second bomb strikes Nagasaki on August 9. — [World War II and the United States](../articles/depression-and-world-war/world-war-ii-and-the-united-states.md) |
| 1945-09-02 | Japan signs the Instrument of Surrender aboard USS Missouri in Tokyo Bay. — [World War II and the United States](../articles/depression-and-world-war/world-war-ii-and-the-united-states.md) |
| 1945-10-24 | The federal minimum reaches 40 cents an hour. — [Minimum wage](../articles/economy-and-work/minimum-wage.md) |
| 1945-11-10 | The last incarcerees leave Heart Mountain. — [Wyoming](../articles/states-and-territories/wyoming.md) |
| 1945-11-15 | A Joint Congressional Committee opens the eighth and most thorough investigation of the attack. — [Attack on Pearl Harbor](../articles/depression-and-world-war/attack-on-pearl-harbor.md) |
| 1946 | The Chinese War Brides Act lets wives of Chinese American servicemen enter outside the quota. — [Chinese Americans](../articles/society-and-people/chinese-americans.md) |
| 1946 | The Legislative Reorganization Act reforms the committee system. — [United States Congress](../articles/constitution-and-government/united-states-congress.md) |
| 1946-02-22 | Dizzy Gillespie makes the first major-label bebop recording, for RCA's Bluebird label. — [Jazz](../articles/culture-and-arts/jazz.md) |
| 1946-02-22 | George F. Kennan sends his "Long Telegram" from the U.S. embassy in Moscow, urging containment of Soviet power. — [Cold War](../articles/cold-war-and-civil-rights/cold-war.md) |
| 1946-02-25 | In Duncan v. Kahanamoku the Supreme Court voids military-tribunal convictions of civilians under martial law. — [Hawaii](../articles/states-and-territories/hawaii.md) |
| 1946-03-05 | Winston Churchill warns in a speech in Missouri that an iron curtain has divided Europe. — [Cold War](../articles/cold-war-and-civil-rights/cold-war.md) |
| 1946-03-20 | Tule Lake closes, the last of the ten WRA camps; the WRA is disbanded on June 30, 1946. — [Japanese American incarceration during World War II](../articles/depression-and-world-war/japanese-american-incarceration.md) |
| 1947 – 1957 | **[McCarthyism](../articles/cold-war-and-civil-rights/mccarthyism.md)** (Era) |
| 1947 | Muddy Waters, now in Chicago, is recording for the Aristocrat label (later Chess). — [Blues](../articles/culture-and-arts/blues.md) |
| 1947 | Named chairman of the first Hoover Commission on executive-branch organization. — [Herbert Hoover](../articles/presidents-and-founders/herbert-hoover.md) |
| 1947 | The Speaker becomes second in the line of presidential succession, after the vice president. — [United States House of Representatives](../articles/constitution-and-government/house-of-representatives.md) |
| 1947 | Veterans make up 49 percent of students at American colleges. — [Servicemen's Readjustment Act of 1944](../articles/cold-war-and-civil-rights/servicemens-readjustment-act-of-1944.md) |
| 1947-03 | President Harry S. Truman issues Executive Order 9835, creating loyalty reviews for federal employees. — [McCarthyism](../articles/cold-war-and-civil-rights/mccarthyism.md) |
| 1947-03-12 | President Truman asks Congress for $400 million in aid to Greece and Turkey, announcing the Truman Doctrine. — [Cold War](../articles/cold-war-and-civil-rights/cold-war.md) |
| 1947-03-21 | Executive Order 9835 creates a loyalty program for federal employees, launching the domestic Red Scare. — [Cold War](../articles/cold-war-and-civil-rights/cold-war.md) |
| 1947-04-30 | A congressional resolution makes the name Hoover Dam official. — [Hoover Dam](../articles/regions-cities-and-landmarks/hoover-dam.md) |
| 1947-06 | Congress overrides President Truman's veto to pass the Taft-Hartley (Labor Management Relations) Act. — [Labor movement](../articles/economy-and-work/labor-movement.md) |
| 1947-10-30 | Screenwriter Ring Lardner Jr. is removed from the stand at a House Un-American Activities Committee hearing; he and nine others become the blacklisted Hollywood Ten. — [Hollywood](../articles/culture-and-arts/hollywood.md) |
| 1948 | Graduates from Morehouse College, where Benjamin E. Mays and George Kelsey shape his view of Christianity as a force for social change. — [Martin Luther King Jr.](../articles/people-who-shaped-america/martin-luther-king-jr.md) |
| 1948 | The National Park Service takes custody of the bell and Independence Hall. — [Liberty Bell](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/liberty-bell.md) |
| 1948 | The terrazzo floor receives its first repairs. — [Winged Figures of the Republic](../articles/culture-and-arts/winged-figures-of-the-republic.md) |
| 1948-03 | Britain, France, Belgium, the Netherlands and Luxembourg sign the Brussels Treaty, a five-nation collective defense pact. — [North Atlantic Treaty Organization](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/nato.md) |
| 1948-05-03 | In United States v. Paramount Pictures the Supreme Court rules block booking and price-fixing illegal, opening the way to separating studios from their theaters. — [Hollywood](../articles/culture-and-arts/hollywood.md) |
| 1948-06 | The Soviets cut land and water routes to West Berlin; the Western Allies answer with an airlift. — [Cold War](../articles/cold-war-and-civil-rights/cold-war.md) |
| 1948-06-11 | The Senate adopts the Vandenberg Resolution, backing U.S. association with regional collective-defense arrangements through constitutional processes. — [North Atlantic Treaty Organization](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/nato.md) |
| 1948-07-26 | Executive Order 9981 directs equal treatment and opportunity in the armed forces regardless of race. — [Civil rights movement](../articles/cold-war-and-civil-rights/civil-rights-movement.md) |
| 1948-08-03 | Whittaker Chambers testifies before HUAC, beginning the case that ends in Alger Hiss's perjury conviction. — [McCarthyism](../articles/cold-war-and-civil-rights/mccarthyism.md) |
| 1948-12-17 | The Flyer is installed at the Smithsonian, 45 years to the day after it flew. — [Wright brothers' first flight](../articles/science-and-innovation/wright-brothers-first-flight.md) |
| 1949 | Lorenzo Dow Turner publishes Africanisms in the Gullah Dialect, documenting thousands of African-derived words and names. — [Gullah Geechee](../articles/society-and-people/gullah-geechee.md) |
| 1949 | President Harry Truman signs legislation designating June 14 as national Flag Day. — [National symbols of the United States](../articles/symbols-and-national-identity/national-symbols-of-the-united-states.md) |
| 1949-04 | The United States, Canada and Western European states form NATO, pledging mutual defense. — [Cold War](../articles/cold-war-and-civil-rights/cold-war.md) |
| 1949-04-04 | **[North Atlantic Treaty Organization](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/nato.md)** (Organization) |
| 1949-04-04 | Twelve nations sign the North Atlantic Treaty in Washington; Secretary of State Dean Acheson signs for the United States. — [North Atlantic Treaty Organization](../articles/foreign-relations-and-military/nato.md) |
